Fascicle 5 · 摩訶止觀卷第五

Three Thousand in a Single Thought

This page continues Ru-Yi's own English translation of Zhiyi's Mohe Zhiguan (摩訶止觀, "The Great Calming and Contemplation"). Fascicle 5 opens topic seven, the actual practice (正修止觀) — the treatise's longest topic, and the one the previous six topics and the twenty-five preliminary conditions were themselves only preparation for. It names the famous ten objects of contemplation, then works the first of the ten modes of contemplation through the first object, the five aggregates, in full detail — including the single most celebrated passage in the entire treatise, "three thousand [realms] in a single thought" (一念三千), and the equally demanding "comprehensively breaking through every dharma" that follows it. We translate slowly, section by section, with the Chinese source beside the English so readers can study the meaning, not merely read around it.

Status: fascicle 5 complete Translator: Ru-Yi Meditation Center Source: Taishō T46, no. 1911 Covers: topic seven, opening — the ten objects of contemplation, and the first four of the ten modes of contemplation applied to the first object

Continuing from Fascicle 4

Fascicle 4 gave the whole of topic six, the preliminary expedients, in exhaustive practical detail — the twenty-five preliminary conditions that must be in place before formal contemplation can even begin. Fascicle 5 (again combining CBETA's upper and lower halves into a single continuous page, per this site's existing convention) turns to topic seven, the actual practice (正修止觀) — by far the longest and most demanding of the treatise's ten topics, worked out across the famous ten objects of contemplation (十境) and, for each object in turn, the equally famous ten modes of contemplation (十法). Fascicle 5 covers only the beginning of this vast undertaking: the ten objects are named and their own inner logic explained, and then the first four of the ten modes are worked through in full for the first object alone, the five aggregates — including the treatise's single most influential passage, on the three thousand realms contained in a single thought. See the translation notes for the running glossary, and the source-and-copyright note there regarding this site's independent-translation policy.

Opening Fascicle Five 卷第五上

Chinese Source

摩訶止觀卷第五上
隋天台智者大師說
門人灌頂記

Ru-Yi Translation

The Great Calming and Contemplation, Fascicle Five, Upper Part
Spoken by the Great Master Zhiyi of Tiantai, of the Sui dynasty
Recorded by his disciple Guanding

VII. The Actual Practice: Who This Teaching Is For 正修止觀 · 對機

Chinese Source

第七、正修止觀者，前六重依修多羅以開妙解，今依妙解以立正行。膏明相賴，目足更資。行解既勤，三障、四魔紛然競起；重昏巨散，翳動定明。不可隨，不可畏。隨之，將人向惡道；畏之，妨修正法。當以觀觀昏，即昏而朗；以止止散，即散而寂。如猪揩金山，眾流入海；薪熾於火，風益求羅耳。此金剛觀，割煩惱陣；此牢強足，越生死野。慧淨於行，行進於慧。照潤導達，交絡瑩飾。一體二手，更互揩摩。非但開拓遮障，內進已道；又精通經論，外啟未聞。自匠匠他，兼利具足。人師國寶，非此是誰？而復學佛慈悲，無諸慳悋，說於止觀，施於彼者，即是開門傾藏，捨如意珠。此珠放光而復雨寶，照闇豐乏，朗夜濟窮。馳二輪而致遠，翥兩翅以高飛。玉潤碧鮮，可勝言哉？香城粉骨、雪嶺投身，亦何足以報德？快馬見鞭影，即著正路。

Ru-Yi Translation

Topic seven, the actual practice of calming-and-contemplation: the six topics that came before relied on the sūtras to open up sublime understanding; here, relying on that sublime understanding, correct practice itself is established. Like oil and wick depending on each other, like the eye and the foot supplying one another's need. Once practice and understanding are both diligently pursued, the three obstructions and four māras arise all at once, contending in confusion; dense torpor and great agitation obscure and disturb the very brightness of samādhi. One must neither follow them nor fear them: following them drives a person toward the evil destinies; fearing them obstructs the cultivation of the true Dharma. One should use contemplation to contemplate torpor, so that torpor itself becomes clarity; use calming to calm agitation, so that agitation itself becomes stillness. It is like a hog rubbing itself against a golden mountain, or every stream flowing into the sea; like firewood only feeding the flame higher, or wind only fanning the hunt's own pursuit. This vajra-like contemplation cuts through the battle-ranks of affliction; these firm and sturdy feet cross the wilderness of birth-and-death. Wisdom is purified through practice, and practice advances through wisdom; each illuminates, moistens, guides, and reaches the other, interwoven and burnished bright. One body, two hands, mutually rubbing and polishing each other. This is not only opening up and clearing away one's own inward obstructions, advancing one's own path — it is also mastering the sūtras and treatises in full, so as to open, outwardly, what has never yet been heard. Perfecting oneself and perfecting others, benefiting both at once — who else could be called the treasure of the nation, the teacher of humankind, if not one who does this? And further, learning the Buddha's own compassion, free of any stinginess or reluctance, teaching calming-and-contemplation and bestowing it upon others — this is opening the gate and emptying the treasury, giving away the wish-fulfilling jewel itself. This jewel radiates light and rains down treasure, illuminating darkness and supplying want, brightening the night and rescuing poverty. It races on two wheels to reach the distance, spreads two wings to soar aloft. Its lustrous jade, its gleaming jasper — how could this be put into words? Grinding one's own bones at the Fragrant City, casting one's own body from the Snow Mountains — how could even these ever repay such a debt? A swift horse, seeing merely the shadow of the whip, arrives at once on the correct road.

其癡鈍者，毒氣深入，失本心故。既其不信，則不入手；無聞法鉤，故聽不能解。乏智慧眼，不別真偽；舉身痺癩，動步不前，不覺不知。大罪聚人，何勞為說？設厭世者，翫下劣乘，攀附枝葉，狗狎作務；敬獼猴為帝釋，宗瓦礫是明珠。此黑闇人豈可論道？又一種禪人，不達他根性，純教乳藥。體心踏心，和融覺覓，若泯若了，斯一轍之意。障難萬途，紛然不識；纔見異相，即判是道；自非法器，復闕匠他；盲跛師徒，二俱墮落，瞽蹶夜遊，甚可憐愍。不應對上諸人，說此止觀。

But the foolish and the dull, the poison already sunk deep within them, having lost their own original mind — since they do not believe, the teaching cannot take hold in them; lacking the hook of having heard the Dharma, listening brings them no understanding at all. Lacking the eye of wisdom, they cannot distinguish the true from the false; their whole body numb as with leprosy, they cannot take a single step forward, neither perceiving nor knowing. Persons who have accumulated grave offenses — why trouble to teach them at all? Should some world-weary person indulge instead in some inferior vehicle, clinging to its outer branches and leaves, growing dog-like intimate with its lesser tasks — revering a monkey as though it were Indra, honoring a shard of tile as though it were a bright pearl — how could a person so benighted possibly discuss the path at all? And there is, further, a certain kind of meditation teacher who, failing to grasp another's own capacity, teaches only the milk-remedy indiscriminately to everyone — embodying mind treading upon mind, blending and seeking awakening, whether dissolved or understood, all held to be one and the same track. Obstacles and difficulties run in ten thousand directions, and such a teacher, confused, fails to recognize any of them; the moment some unusual sign appears, he judges it at once to be the path itself. Being himself no fit vessel for the Dharma, and further lacking any means to instruct another, both the blind teacher and his lame disciple fall together, stumbling about in the dark of night — a matter deeply to be pitied. This calming-and-contemplation should not be taught to any of the persons described above.

Reading note: Topic seven opens with the treatise's most self-conscious statement of its own stakes: everything from fascicle 1 onward — the lineage, the great intent, the Four Teachings, the twenty-five preliminary conditions — was preparation for this. The warning against the careless meditation teacher who "teaches only the milk-remedy indiscriminately to everyone," unable to distinguish one student's own capacity from another's, previews the entire logic of the ten objects and ten modes that follow: this is not one technique but a diagnostic system, precisely because uniform treatment fails.

The Ten Objects of Contemplation 十境

Chinese Source

夫止觀者，高尚者高尚，卑劣者卑劣。開止觀為十：一、陰界入，二、煩惱，三、病患，四、業相，五、魔事，六、禪定，七、諸見，八、增上慢，九、二乘，十、菩薩。此十境，通能覆障。「陰」在初者二義：一、現前，二、依經。《大品》云：「聲聞人依四念處行道；菩薩初觀色乃至一切種智。」章章皆爾，故不違經。又行人受身，誰不陰入重擔現前？是故初觀。後發異相，別為次耳。夫五陰與四大合，若不照察，不覺紛馳。如閉舟順水，寧知奔迸？若其迴泝，始覺馳流。既觀陰果，則動煩惱因，故次五陰而論四分也。四大是身病，三毒是心病，以其等故，情中不覺。今大、分俱觀，衝擊脈藏，故四蛇偏起，致有患生。無量諸業不可稱計，散善微弱不能令動。今修止觀，健病不虧，動生死輪。或善萌故動，惡壞故動；善示受報故動，惡來責報故動。故次病說業也。以惡動故，惡欲滅；善動故，善欲生。魔遽出境，作諸留難，或壞其道。故次業說魔。若過魔事，則功德生。或過去習因，或現在行力，諸禪競起；或味、或淨，或橫、或竪。故次魔說禪。禪有觀支，因生邪慧；逸觀於法，僻起諸倒，邪辯猛利。故次禪說見。若識見為非，息其妄著，貪、瞋利鈍，二俱不起，無智者謂證涅槃；小乘亦有橫計四禪為四果；大乘亦有魔來與記。並是未得謂得增上慢人。故次見說慢。見、慢既靜，先世小習，因靜而生。身子捨眼，即其事也。《大品》云：「恒沙菩薩發大心，若一若二入菩薩位，多墮二乘。」故次慢說二乘。若憶本願，故不墮空者，諸方便道菩薩境界即起也。《大品》云：「有菩薩不久行六波羅蜜，若聞深法，即起誹謗，墮泥犁中。」此是六度菩薩耳。通教方便位，亦有謗義；入真道，不謗也。別教初心知有深法，是則不謗。此等悉是諸權善根，故次二乘後說也。

Ru-Yi Translation

Calming-and-contemplation is, of itself, exalted for the exalted, and base for the base. Calming-and-contemplation opens out into ten objects: first, the aggregates, entries, and elements; second, affliction; third, illness; fourth, the marks of karma; fifth, demonic affairs; sixth, meditative states; seventh, views; eighth, overweening pride; ninth, the two lesser vehicles; tenth, the bodhisattva. These ten objects, in general, are all able to cover over and obstruct. The aggregates come first for two reasons: first, because they are already present; second, in accordance with the sūtras. The Larger Sūtra says: "The śrāvaka practices the path by means of the four foundations of mindfulness; the bodhisattva first contemplates form, and so on up through the wisdom of all modes." Every chapter follows this same pattern, and so this order does not contradict the sūtras. Moreover, when a practitioner takes on a body at all, who does not already have the heavy burden of the aggregates, entries, and elements right there before them? This is why they are contemplated first; whatever unusual signs arise afterward are simply given their own separate place in the sequence. The five aggregates, joined with the four elements — if one does not carefully observe them, one fails even to notice their own restless movement, like a boat with its cabin closed, drifting with the current, unaware of how swiftly it races along; only by turning against the current does one first become aware of that swift flow at all. Once the fruit of the aggregates is contemplated, the cause of affliction is thereby stirred into motion — hence affliction is discussed next, after the five aggregates, in terms of the fourfold division. The four elements are the body's own sickness; the three poisons are the mind's own sickness — and because these correspond so closely, one fails to notice them within one's own feeling. Now, contemplating both the elements and the divisions of affliction together, striking against the very channels and organs, the four serpents rise up out of balance, and illness results. Karma of boundless kinds cannot be reckoned in number, and scattered wholesome acts are too weak to stir it into motion; but now, cultivating calming-and-contemplation with vigor, illness is no longer able to weaken it, and the wheel of birth-and-death is set turning. Sometimes it is stirred because wholesome sprouts arise; sometimes because evil breaks apart; sometimes because wholesome deeds show themselves in the reward to be received; sometimes because evil comes seeking its own reckoning. This is why karma is discussed next, after illness. Because evil is thus stirred, evil is about to be extinguished; because wholesomeness is thus stirred, wholesomeness is about to arise — and māra rushes out into view at just this point, creating every kind of obstacle and difficulty, sometimes even destroying the path itself. This is why māra is discussed next, after karma. Once demonic affairs are surpassed, merit arises: whether from a habituated cause in the past, or from the power of present practice, the various dhyānas compete to arise, some tainted, some pure, some deviant, some upright. This is why meditative states are discussed next, after māra. Dhyāna has its own branch of contemplation, from which deviant wisdom can arise; observation of the Dharma runs to excess, wrongly giving rise to every kind of inversion, deviant eloquence growing fierce and sharp. This is why views are discussed next, after meditative states. Should one recognize a view as wrong and still its false attachment, so that craving and aversion, sharp and dull alike, no longer arise at all — one without wisdom may then suppose this to be the realization of nirvāṇa; even the Hīnayāna has its own mistaken reckoning of the four dhyānas as the four fruits, and the Mahāyāna, too, has cases of māra coming to bestow a false prediction of buddhahood. All of these are persons of overweening pride, not yet having attained, and yet supposing they have attained. This is why overweening pride is discussed next, after views. Once views and pride have both been stilled, the slight habituated tendencies of a former life, on account of this very stillness, come to arise — Śāriputra's own abandonment of the bodhisattva path over the matter of his eye is exactly such a case. The Larger Sūtra says: "Bodhisattvas numerous as the sands of the Ganges give rise to the great resolve, and yet one or two among them, entering the bodhisattva's own stage, very often fall back into the two lesser vehicles." This is why the two lesser vehicles are discussed next, after overweening pride. Should one, remembering one's own original vow, thereby avoid falling into mere emptiness, the bodhisattva's own state, on the various paths of expedient means, then arises. The Larger Sūtra says: "There are bodhisattvas who, not long after practicing the six pāramitās, on hearing some profound teaching, at once give rise to slander, and fall into hell." This concerns only the bodhisattva of the six pāramitās; even at the expedient stage of the shared teaching, this same slander can still occur, though not once one has entered the real path; and the distinct teaching's own beginner, already knowing that some profound teaching exists, does not slander it at all. All of these belong to the provisional wholesome roots, and this is exactly why the bodhisattva's own state is discussed last, after the two lesser vehicles.

此十種境，始自凡夫正報，終至聖人方便。陰入一境常自現前，若發、不發，恒得為觀；餘九境，發可為觀，不發何所觀？又八境去正道遠，深加防護，得歸正轍；二境去正道近，至此位時不慮無觀，薄修即正。又若不解諸境互發，大起疑網；如在岐道，不知所從。先若聞之，恣其變怪，心安若空。

These ten objects begin with the ordinary being's own present retribution and end with the sage's own expedient means. The single object of the aggregates, entries, and elements is always, of itself, already present, whether or not it has yet arisen into some further manifestation, and so it can always be taken as an object of contemplation; but the remaining nine objects can only be contemplated once they have actually arisen — if they have not arisen, what is there left to contemplate? Moreover, the first eight of these objects lie far from the correct path, and require deep and careful guarding before one can return to the correct track; the last two objects lie near to the correct path, and, once one reaches this stage, there is no need to worry over lacking an object of contemplation at all — even a slight cultivation is already correct. Furthermore, should one fail to understand how these objects mutually arise from one another, a great net of doubt will be raised, as though standing at a fork in the road with no idea which way to go; but should one hear of this beforehand, then however strange or unpredictable their arising may be, the mind remains as much at ease as empty space itself.

Reading note: The ten objects are not a checklist to complete in sequence so much as a map of what can actually surface for a practitioner sitting down to meditate — and the ordering argument given here is itself worth noticing as a piece of psychological realism: affliction follows the aggregates because attending closely to embodied experience stirs affliction into motion; illness follows affliction because the two "correspond so closely" that they mask each other; māra follows karma because "evil is about to be extinguished" is exactly the moment resistance intensifies. Even Śāriputra — traditionally the Buddha's foremost disciple in wisdom — is cited by name as a case of dormant habituated tendency resurfacing once views and pride are stilled, a small but pointed reminder that this list describes obstacles that can arise at any stage of practice, not failures unique to beginners.

How the Ten Objects Arise from One Another 十境 · 互發

Chinese Source

互發有十，謂：次第不次第、雜不雜、具不具、作意不作意、成不成、益不益、久不久、難不難、更不更，三障四魔，九雙七隻。次第者有三義，謂：法、修、發。法者，次第淺深法也。修者，先世已曾研習次第，或此世次第修也。發者，依次修而次發也。不次亦三義，謂：法、修、發。發則不定，或前發菩薩境，後發陰入。雖不次第，十數宛足。修者，若四大違返則先修病患；若四分增多則先修煩惱。如是一一隨強者先修。法者，眼、耳、鼻、舌，陰、入、界等，皆是寂靜門，亦是法界，何須捨此就彼？出《寶篋經》云云。當知法界外，更無復有法而為次第也。煩惱即法界，如《無行經》云：「貪欲即是道。」《淨名》云：「行於非道，通達佛道。」佛道既通，無復次第也。病患是法界者，《淨名》云：「今我病者非真非有；眾生病亦非真非有。」以此自調，亦度眾生。方丈託疾，雙林病行，即其義也。業相為法界者，業是行陰。《法華》云：「深達罪福相，遍照於十方，微妙淨法身，具相三十二。」達業從緣生，不自在故空，此業能破業。若眾生應以此業得度示現諸業，以此業立業。業與不業，縛脫叵得。普門示現，雙照縛脫，故名「深達」，何啻堪為方等師耶？魔事為法界者，《首楞嚴》云：「魔界如，佛界如，一如無二如。」實際中尚不見佛，況見有魔耶？設有魔者，良藥塗屣，堪任乘御云云。禪為法界者，「能觀心性，名為上定」，即首楞嚴；不昧不亂，入王三昧，一切三昧悉入其中。見為法界者，《淨名》云：「以邪相入正相，於諸見不動而修三十七品。」又動修、不動修、亦動亦不動修、非動非不動修三十七品。以見為門，以見為侍。慢為法界者，還是煩惱耳。觀慢無慢、慢大慢、非慢非不慢，成祕密藏，入大涅槃。二乘為法界者，若「但見於空，不見不空。」云云。「智者見空及與不空。」「決了聲聞法，是諸經之王，聞已諦思惟，得近無上道。」菩薩境為法界者，底惡生死、下劣小乘尚即是法界，況菩薩法寧非佛道？又菩薩方便之權，即權而實，亦即非權非實，成祕密藏，入大涅槃。是一一法皆即法界，是為不次第法相也。

Ru-Yi Translation

Mutual arising among the objects has ten aspects: sequential or non-sequential, mixed or unmixed, complete or incomplete, deliberate or spontaneous, successful or unsuccessful, beneficial or harmful, difficult or easy to arouse, lasting or fleeting, and recurring or non-recurring — together with the three obstructions and four māras, making nine pairs and one further single item. As for the sequential: this has three senses, namely dharma, cultivation, and arising. "Dharma" means the dharma of shallow and deep laid out in sequence. "Cultivation" means either having already studied this sequence in a former life, or cultivating it in sequence in this present life. "Arising" means arising in sequence, in accordance with cultivation carried out in sequence. The non-sequential likewise has three senses — dharma, cultivation, and arising — where "arising" is not fixed: the bodhisattva's own object might arise first, and the aggregates only afterward; though not in sequence, the full count of ten is nonetheless still complete. As for "cultivation": should the four elements fall out of balance, illness is cultivated first; should the fourfold division of affliction grow excessive, affliction is cultivated first — in every case, whichever is strongest is cultivated first. As for "dharma": the eye, ear, nose, and tongue, the aggregates, entries, and elements, are all, every one, already a gate of quiescence, already the dharma-realm itself — why, then, would one need to set this aside in favor of something else? This is given in the Ratnakūṭa Sūtra. One should know that outside the dharma-realm, there is no further dharma left over to serve as any sequence at all. That affliction is itself the dharma-realm: as the Sūtra on the Practice of Non-Action says, "Craving and desire are themselves the path." The Vimalakīrti Sūtra says, "Practicing what is not the path, one penetrates through to the buddha-path itself." Since the buddha-path is already penetrated through in this way, there is no further sequence left at all. That illness is itself the dharma-realm: the Vimalakīrti Sūtra says, "My own illness now is neither real nor existent; the illness of sentient beings, too, is neither real nor existent." By this, one regulates oneself, and also delivers sentient beings — Vimalakīrti's own feigned illness in his one-room dwelling, and the Buddha's own final illness between the twin śāla trees, are exactly this same sense. That the marks of karma are themselves the dharma-realm: karma is itself the aggregate of formations. The Lotus Sūtra says, "Fully realizing the mark of offense and merit, illuminating throughout the ten directions, the subtle and sublime pure Dharma-body, complete with the thirty-two marks." Realizing that karma arises from conditions, and, lacking any self-mastery, is thus empty — this very karma is itself able to break through karma. Should sentient beings need to be delivered by means of some karma, manifesting every kind of karmic act, one establishes karma by means of karma itself; between karma and no-karma, bondage and liberation, neither can ultimately be found. Manifesting through the universal gate, illuminating both bondage and liberation at once, is exactly why this is named "fully realizing" — how could this be no more than fit to be a teacher of the Vaipulya rite alone? That demonic affairs are themselves the dharma-realm: the Śūraṅgama Sūtra says, "The realm of māra, just as it is, and the realm of the Buddha, just as it is, are a single suchness, with no second suchness at all." Within the very bounds of reality itself, not even the Buddha is to be found — how much less any māra? Should any māra still appear, it is itself the very medicine smeared on one's own sandal, fit to be mounted and ridden upon. That meditative states are themselves the dharma-realm: "able to contemplate the very nature of mind — this is named the supreme samādhi," which is precisely the Śūraṅgama itself; neither obscured nor disordered, one enters the king-samādhi, and every samādhi whatsoever enters into it entirely. That views are themselves the dharma-realm: the Vimalakīrti Sūtra says, "By means of a deviant mark, one enters the correct mark, cultivating the thirty-seven factors of enlightenment while remaining unmoved with regard to every view." There is, further, cultivating the thirty-seven factors while stirred, while unstirred, both stirred and unstirred, and neither stirred nor unstirred — taking view itself as the gate, taking view itself as one's own attendant. That pride is itself the dharma-realm: this, too, is simply affliction. Contemplating pride as without pride, pride as great pride, and neither pride nor no-pride, one accomplishes the secret treasury and enters the great nirvāṇa. That the two lesser vehicles are themselves the dharma-realm: "seeing only emptiness, and not seeing the not-empty" — while "the wise see both emptiness and the not-empty," and "definitively clarifying the śrāvaka's own dharma, this is the king of every sūtra; having heard it, one reflects on it directly, and thereby draws near the unsurpassed path." That the bodhisattva's own state is itself the dharma-realm: even the very depths of evil, of birth-and-death, even the humblest of the lesser vehicles, are already the dharma-realm — how much more, then, could the bodhisattva's own dharma fail to be the buddha-path? And the bodhisattva's own provisional expedient means, being provisional and yet real, and also neither provisional nor real, accomplishes the secret treasury and enters the great nirvāṇa. Each and every one of these dharmas is thus already the dharma-realm itself — this is the mark of the non-sequential dharma.

雜不雜者，發一境已，更發一境，歷歷分明，是為不雜。適發陰入，復起煩惱；煩惱未謝，復業、復魔、禪、見、慢等，交橫並沓，是為雜發。雖雜，不出十種。具不具者，十數足名具；九去名不具。次不次、雜不雜，皆論具不具，又總具、總不具，別具、別不具；十數足是總具，十數不委悉是總不具；九數欠是別不具，九數中委悉是別具。又橫具、橫不具，竪具、竪不具：例如發四禪至非想是竪具，至不用處是竪不具；發通明、背捨等是橫具，止發七背捨是橫不具。又發初禪至四禪是竪具；三禪來是竪不具。又初禪九品是竪具；八品來是竪不具。又一品五支足是橫具；四支已來是橫不具。其餘例此可知云云。修不修者，作意修陰界入，界入開解，是修發；不作意，陰界入自發，通達色心，是不修發，乃至菩薩境亦如是。應有四句為根本，句句織成三十六句，例如下煩惱境中說。成不成者，若發一境，究竟成就；成就已謝，更發餘境，餘境亦究竟成。若發一種，作起、乍滅，非但品數缺少，於分分中亦曖昧不明。前具不具止明頭數，此中論體，分始終。益不益者，或發惡法，於止觀巨益，明靜轉深；或發善法，於止觀大損，損其靜照。或增靜損照、或損靜增照、俱增、俱損。難發不難發者，或惡法難易、或善法難易、俱難、俱易。久不久者，自有一境久久不去；或有一境即起即去云云。更不更者，自有一境，一更、兩更，乃至多多；自有一境，一發即休，後不復發。如是等種種不同，善識其意，莫謬去取。然皆以止觀研之，使無滯也。

As for mixed or unmixed: once one object has arisen, and then a further object arises, each remaining distinct and clear in turn, this is "unmixed." Should the aggregates just now have arisen, and affliction rise up as well before that has yet subsided, followed by karma, then māra, meditative states, views, pride, and the rest, crossing and overlapping in confusion, this is "mixed" arising. Though mixed, it never exceeds these ten kinds. As for complete or incomplete: the full count of ten is named "complete"; anything short of nine is named "incomplete." Sequential or non-sequential, and mixed or unmixed, are both discussed in terms of complete or incomplete as well — and there is, further, complete or incomplete overall, and complete or incomplete in particular: the full count of ten is complete overall; the ten not fully accounted for is incomplete overall; missing from the count of nine is incomplete in particular; nine fully accounted for is complete in particular. There is, further, complete or incomplete horizontally, and complete or incomplete vertically: for instance, giving rise to the four dhyānas up through neither-perception-nor-non-perception is vertically complete, while stopping at the sphere of nothingness is vertically incomplete; giving rise to the six penetrations and clarities, the eight forms of liberation, and the like, is horizontally complete, while giving rise to only the seven forms of liberation is horizontally incomplete. Giving rise to the first dhyāna through the fourth is vertically complete; stopping at the third is vertically incomplete. The nine grades of the first dhyāna, complete, is vertically complete; stopping at the eighth grade is vertically incomplete. A single grade complete with all five branches is horizontally complete; stopping at four branches is horizontally incomplete. The rest may be understood by the same pattern. As for cultivated or uncultivated: deliberately cultivating the aggregates, entries, and elements, so that they open into understanding, is "cultivated arising"; without any deliberate intent, the aggregates, entries, and elements arising of themselves, penetrating through to form and mind directly, is "uncultivated arising" — and the same holds, on through the bodhisattva's own object as well. This should form a fourfold proposition as its root, each proposition then woven out into thirty-six further propositions, exactly as explained below in the discussion of the object of affliction. As for successful or unsuccessful: should one object arise and reach ultimate accomplishment, and then, once that accomplishment has passed, a further object arise and likewise reach ultimate accomplishment in turn — or should one kind arise only fitfully, now rising, now vanishing, not only deficient in its own full count but also obscure and unclear in each and every one of its particulars. The "complete or incomplete" discussed above concerned only the bare count; here, the very substance itself is discussed, distinguishing beginning from end. As for beneficial or harmful: sometimes an evil dharma arises that proves of great benefit to calming-and-contemplation, its clarity and stillness only deepening further; sometimes a wholesome dharma arises that proves of great harm to calming-and-contemplation, damaging its own stillness and illumination. Sometimes stillness increases while illumination is damaged; sometimes stillness is damaged while illumination increases; sometimes both increase together; sometimes both are damaged together. As for difficult or easy to arouse: sometimes an evil dharma is difficult or easy to arouse; sometimes a wholesome dharma is difficult or easy to arouse; sometimes both are difficult; sometimes both are easy. As for lasting or fleeting: a given object may of itself linger on for a very long time without departing; or a given object may arise and depart in the very same instant. As for recurring or non-recurring: a given object may, of itself, recur once, or twice, or even many, many times over; or a given object may arise just once and then cease entirely, never arising again thereafter. Differences of this kind, of every variety, must be well understood in their own sense, so that one does not mistakenly accept or reject them at random. In every case, however, all of this should be investigated by means of calming-and-contemplation itself, so that one is never brought to a standstill by any of it.

Reading note: "Craving and desire are themselves the path," reached only after an extended dialectical struggle in fascicle 2, is here simply one item on a list — evidence of how thoroughly the treatise expects that earlier hard-won conclusion to have become working knowledge by fascicle 5. The ten-fold taxonomy of mutual arising (sequential, mixed, complete, deliberate, successful, beneficial, difficult, lasting, recurring) is not decoration; it is a genuinely practical diagnostic vocabulary, built so that a meditation teacher — or a solitary practitioner without one — has language precise enough to describe what is actually happening moment to moment, rather than only "something arose."

The Three Obstructions and Four Māras, and Why the Objects Arise as They Do 三障四魔

Chinese Source

三障四魔者，《普賢觀》云：「閻浮提人三障重故。」陰入、病患是報障；煩惱、見、慢是煩惱障；業、魔、禪、二乘、菩薩是業障。障止觀不明靜，塞菩提道，令行人不得通至五品、六根清淨位，故名為「障」。四魔者，陰入正是陰魔；業、禪、二乘、菩薩等是行陰，名為陰魔。煩惱、見、慢等是煩惱魔。病患是死因，名死魔。魔事是天子魔。魔名「奪者」。破觀名「奪命」；破止名「奪身」。又魔名「磨訛」，磨觀訛令黑闇；磨止訛令散逸，故名為魔云云。

Ru-Yi Translation

As for the three obstructions and four māras: the Samantabhadra Contemplation Sūtra says, "The people of Jambudvīpa are heavy with the three obstructions." The aggregates, entries, and elements, together with illness, are the obstruction of retribution; affliction, views, and pride are the obstruction of affliction; karma, māra, meditative states, the two lesser vehicles, and the bodhisattva's own state are the obstruction of karma. These obstruct calming-and-contemplation from becoming clear and still, blocking the path to bodhi, and prevent the practitioner from passing through to the five grades of disciples and the purification of the six faculties — hence they are named "obstructions." As for the four māras: the aggregates, entries, and elements are precisely the māra of the aggregates; karma, meditative states, the two lesser vehicles, and the bodhisattva's own state are all the aggregate of formations, and so are likewise named the māra of the aggregates. Affliction, views, pride, and the like are the māra of affliction. Illness is the very cause of death, and is named the māra of death. Demonic affairs themselves are the māra of the celestial king. "Māra" means "the one who seizes": breaking contemplation is named "seizing life"; breaking calming is named "seizing the body." Māra is also named "grinding falsehood": grinding down contemplation into falsehood plunges one into darkness; grinding down calming into falsehood scatters one into dissipation — hence the name māra.

問：何意互發？答：皆由二世因緣。昔有漸觀種子，今得修行之雨，即次第發；昔有頓觀種子，即不次第發；昔有不定種子，即雜發。昔修時數具，即具發；昔修時數不具，即不具發。昔曾證得，今發則成；昔但修不證，今發不成。昔因強，今不修而發；今緣強，待修而發。昔因、今緣，二俱善巧，迴向上道，今發則益；昔因緣中雜毒，是則致損。發所因處弱，則不久；發因處強，是則久。麁、細住乃至四禪，傳傳判強弱云云。善易發，關遮輕；善難發，由遮重。惡難發，由根利；惡易發，由根鈍。惡欲滅而告謝；善欲生而相知，則一而不更。善欲滅而求救；惡欲興而求受，則更更更更。此中皆須口決，用智慧籌量；不得師心，謬判是非，爾其慎之！勤之！重之！

Question: for what reason do these objects arise from one another in this way? Answer: this is entirely on account of causal conditions spanning two lifetimes. If one formerly held the seed of gradual contemplation, and now receives the rain of actual cultivation, this arises in sequence; if one formerly held the seed of sudden contemplation, this arises out of sequence; if one formerly held an indeterminate seed, this arises mixed. If one's former cultivation was complete in its own count, this arises complete; if incomplete, this arises incomplete. If one has formerly already realized something, its present arising reaches accomplishment; if one merely cultivated it formerly without realization, its present arising does not reach accomplishment. If the former cause is strong, this arises now even without further cultivation; if the present condition is strong, this arises only once cultivation is applied. Where the former cause and the present condition are both skillfully applied, turning and dedicating themselves toward the higher path, this present arising proves beneficial; where the former cause and condition were themselves mixed with poison, this instead brings about harm. Where the place from which the arising springs is weak, it does not last long; where that place is strong, it does last. From coarse and fine states of stability, on through the four dhyānas, strength and weakness are judged by the same pattern, degree by degree. Wholesome states are easy to arouse where the hindering barrier is light; wholesome states are difficult to arouse where the hindering barrier is heavy. Evil states are difficult to arouse where the faculty is sharp; evil states are easy to arouse where the faculty is dull. Where evil is about to be extinguished and takes its leave, and wholesomeness is about to arise and makes itself known — this is a single occurrence, never recurring. Where wholesomeness is about to be extinguished and seeks rescue, and evil is about to rise up and seeks admission — this recurs, and recurs, and recurs, and recurs again. In all of this, oral instruction is essential, weighed and measured by wisdom itself; one should not follow one's own mind alone, mistakenly judging right and wrong. Be careful with this! Apply diligence to this! Take this seriously!

Reading note: The māra-etymologies given here — "the one who seizes," "grinding falsehood" — are folk etymologies rather than linguistic ones, but they do real conceptual work: māra is defined entirely in terms of its effect on practice (seizing life by breaking contemplation, seizing the body by breaking calming) rather than as an external entity with independent existence, consistent with the treatise's earlier declaration that "the realm of māra, just as it is, and the realm of the Buddha, just as it is, are a single suchness." The closing warning against "following one's own mind alone" in judging why a given object has arisen is a rare moment where Zhiyi flags the limits of the text itself: this diagnostic scheme requires a teacher's oral transmission to apply correctly, not silent reading alone.

General and Specific: How Each Object Contains the Rest 十境 · 通別

Chinese Source

私料簡者，法若塵沙，境何定十？答：譬如大地一，能生種種芽；數方不廣略，令義易明了，故言十耳。問：十境通、別云何？答：受身之始無不有身，諸經說觀多從色起，故以陰為初耳。以陰本、陰因、陰患、陰主、善陰，又陰因、別陰等云云。通言煩惱者，見、慢同煩惱；陰入、病是煩惱果；業是煩惱因；禪是無動業，業即煩惱用；魔即統欲界，即煩惱主；二乘、菩薩即別煩惱攝云云。通稱病患者，陰界入即病本；煩惱、見、慢等即是煩惱病。《淨名》云：「今我病者，皆從前世妄想諸煩惱生。」業亦是病。《大經》云：「王今病重。」即指五逆為病也。魔能作病，三災為外過患，喘息、喜樂是內過患。禪有喜樂即病患也。二乘、菩薩即是空病，空病亦空。通稱業者，陰入是業果；煩惱、見、慢是業本；病是業報；魔是魔業；禪是無動業；二乘、菩薩是無漏業。通稱魔者，陰入即陰魔；煩惱、見、慢即煩惱魔；病是死魔；魔即天子魔。餘者皆是行陰魔攝。通稱禪定者，禪自是其境。陰入、煩惱、見、慢、業等悉是十大地中，心數定攝；魔是未到地定果，亦是心數定攝；二乘、菩薩，淨禪攝。又三定攝之：上定攝菩薩、二乘；中、下二定攝八境云云。通稱見者，陰入，即我見、眾生見；煩惱，具五見；病，壽者、命者見；業、禪等，作者見，亦是戒取見；魔，是使作者、使受者、使起等攝。又生死即邊見攝；慢即我見攝；二乘、方便菩薩等皆曲見攝。通稱慢者，陰入，我慢攝；煩惱，即慢慢攝；病患，不如慢攝；業即憍慢攝，由憍故造業；魔即大慢攝；禪即憍慢攝；見亦大慢攝；二乘、菩薩，增上慢攝。通稱二乘者，四念處、四諦法，攝九境也。通稱菩薩境者，以四弘誓攝得九境。

Ru-Yi Translation

A private query asks: if the dharmas are as numerous as motes of dust or grains of sand, how could the objects possibly be fixed at exactly ten? Answer: it is like the single earth, able to give rise to sprouts and shoots of every kind — the count is set neither too broad nor too narrow, so that the sense may be easily understood; hence it is said to be exactly ten. Question: how are these ten objects understood generally and how understood specifically? Answer: from the very moment one takes on a body, there is no case in which the body is not already present, and the sūtras, in discussing contemplation, mostly begin from form — hence the aggregates are taken first, whether as the root, the cause, the affliction, or the master of the aggregates, as the wholesome aggregates, or the aggregates as cause, or the aggregates taken individually, and so on. As for affliction discussed generally: views and pride are themselves alike affliction; the aggregates and illness are the fruit of affliction; karma is the cause of affliction; meditative states are karma without motion, karma itself being the very function of affliction; māra governs the entire desire realm, and is thus the very master of affliction; the two lesser vehicles and the bodhisattva belong to affliction taken in a more particular sense. As for illness discussed generally: the aggregates, entries, and elements are themselves the very root of illness; affliction, views, pride, and the rest are themselves the sickness of affliction. The Vimalakīrti Sūtra says, "My own illness now arises entirely from the deluded thoughts and every affliction of former lives." Karma, too, is a sickness — the Nirvāṇa Sūtra says, "The king is now gravely ill," referring precisely to the five heinous crimes as sickness. Māra, too, is able to cause sickness; the three calamities are an outward affliction, while shortness of breath and excessive joy are an inward affliction. The joy present in dhyāna is itself already a sickness. The two lesser vehicles and the bodhisattva are themselves the sickness of emptiness — though this sickness of emptiness, too, is itself empty. As for karma discussed generally: the aggregates, entries, and elements are the fruit of karma; affliction, views, and pride are the very root of karma; illness is the retribution of karma; māra is māra's own karma; meditative states are karma without motion; the two lesser vehicles and the bodhisattva possess uncontaminated karma. As for māra discussed generally: the aggregates, entries, and elements are themselves the māra of the aggregates; affliction, views, and pride are themselves the māra of affliction; illness is the māra of death; māra itself is the māra of the celestial king — everything else belongs to the māra of the aggregate of formations. As for meditative states discussed generally: dhyāna is, of itself, its own object; the aggregates, entries, affliction, views, pride, and karma are, every one, gathered among the ten great grounds, belonging to the mental factor of concentration; māra is the fruit of the concentration of the "not-yet-arrived" stage, likewise belonging to the mental factor of concentration; the two lesser vehicles and the bodhisattva belong to pure dhyāna. This may also be gathered under three levels of concentration: the highest concentration gathers in the bodhisattva and the two lesser vehicles; the middle and lower two gather in the remaining eight objects. As for views discussed generally: the aggregates and entries are themselves the view of a self and the view of a sentient being; affliction is complete with the five views; illness is the view of a life-span, the view of a life-force; karma, meditative states, and the rest are the view of an agent, and also the view of clinging to precept and vow; māra belongs to the categories of the one who compels action, the one who compels reception, the one who compels arising, and the like. Further, birth-and-death belongs to the extreme view; pride belongs to the view of a self; the two lesser vehicles and the expedient-stage bodhisattva all belong to the crooked view. As for pride discussed generally: the aggregates and entries belong to the pride of self; affliction belongs to the pride of pride upon pride; illness belongs to the pride of considering oneself no worse than others; karma belongs to arrogant pride, since it is on account of arrogance that karma is created; māra belongs to great pride; meditative states belong to arrogant pride; views, too, belong to great pride; the two lesser vehicles and the bodhisattva belong to overweening pride. As for the two lesser vehicles discussed generally: the four foundations of mindfulness and the dharma of the Four Noble Truths gather in the remaining nine objects. As for the bodhisattva's own state discussed generally: the four universal vows gather in the remaining nine objects.

問：境、法、名俱通者，行人亦通不？答：《大經》云：「云何未發心而名為菩薩？」前九境人亦通稱菩薩人也。通是二乘，則有四種聲聞。增上慢聲聞，攝得下八境人也；佛道聲聞，攝得菩薩人也。問：通是無常不？答：《寶性論》云：「菩薩住無漏界中，有無常倒。」問：通是有漏不？答：漏義則通，有義小異。問：通是偏真不？答：偏義則通，真義異。問：通義可領，別復云何？答：十境不同，即別義也。復有亦通亦別：陰是受身之本，又是觀慧之初，所以別當其首，此一境亦通亦別。後九境從發異相受名，但得是通、是別，不得是亦通亦別也。若爾，煩惱亦是諸法之本，元為治惑，亦是觀初。病身四大亦是事本，元為治病，亦是觀初。何意不得亦通亦別？答：若身因煩惱，屬前世。若今世煩惱由身而有，病不恒起，為本事弱。諸經論不以病為觀首，故不亦通亦別耳。非通非別者皆不思議，一陰一切陰，非一、非一切。

Question: if the object, the dharma, and the name are all held in common, is the practitioner, too, held in common? Answer: the Nirvāṇa Sūtra says, "How could one not yet have given rise to the resolve and still be called a bodhisattva?" The persons belonging to the first nine objects, too, may generally be called bodhisattvas. Where the two lesser vehicles are held in common, there are, in fact, four kinds of śrāvaka: the overweening-pride śrāvaka gathers in the persons of the lower eight objects; the buddha-path śrāvaka gathers in the bodhisattva's own persons. Question: is impermanence, too, held in common? Answer: the Ratnagotravibhāga says, "The bodhisattva, dwelling within the uncontaminated realm, still has an inversion of impermanence." Question: is being contaminated, too, held in common? Answer: the sense of contamination is indeed held in common, though the sense of existence differs slightly. Question: is partial-truth, too, held in common? Answer: the sense of partiality is held in common, though the sense of the real differs. Question: the sense of "held in common" may thus be grasped — what, then, of the specific sense? Answer: the ten objects, each different from the next, is exactly the specific sense. There is, further, what is both held in common and specific at once: the aggregates are both the very root from which the body is received and the very starting point of contemplative wisdom, and are therefore given the specific place of coming first — this one object alone is thus both held in common and specific at once. The remaining nine objects each take their own name from the particular manifestation that gives rise to them, and so can only be either held in common or specific, never both held in common and specific together. If this is so, affliction, too, is the very root of every dharma, originally treated in order to cure delusion, and is also the very starting point of contemplation; illness of the body, the four elements, too, is the very root of phenomenal existence, originally treated in order to cure illness, and is also a starting point of contemplation — why, then, could these not also be held to be both held in common and specific at once? Answer: if the body arises on account of affliction, this belongs to a former life; if the affliction of the present life exists on account of the body, illness does not constantly arise from it, and so its standing as a root is weak. None of the sūtras or treatises take illness as the very starting point of contemplation, and this is exactly why it is not held to be both held in common and specific at once. What is neither held in common nor specific is, every one, inconceivable: one aggregate is every aggregate, neither one nor every one at all.

Reading note: This cross-mapping exercise — walking affliction, illness, karma, māra, meditative states, views, and pride each through every one of the other nine objects in turn — can look at first like scholastic overkill, but its actual function is diagnostic humility: it insists that no object ever shows up in pure isolation. What presents itself as "illness" may, examined closely, really be affliction wearing illness's mask; what presents itself as pride may really be a variant of view. The qualification that closes the passage — illness alone, among the nine later objects, is denied even partial standing as a starting point of contemplation, "since it does not constantly arise" — is a small but telling admission that not every symmetry the scheme suggests is actually load-bearing.

Distinct Marks, and the Ten Dharma-Realms Contained in Each Other 十境條然 · 十法界互具

Chinese Source

問：九境相起，更立別名者，陰入解起，應立別名？答：陰解起時，非條然別，還是陰入攝。若執此解，即屬見。若約解起愛、恚，屬煩惱；招病來魔，隨事別判。若解發朗然，無九境相者，此則止觀氣分。但得通別，不得亦通亦別耳。問：十境條然別不？答：四念處是陰別；觀空聚是入別；無我是界別；五停心，煩惱別；八念，病別；十善，業別；五繫，魔別；六妙門，禪別；道品，見別；無常、苦、空，慢別；四諦、十二緣，二乘別；六度，菩薩別。問：五陰俱是境；色、心外別有觀耶？答：不思議境智，即陰是觀。亦可分別：不善、無記陰是境；善五陰是觀。觀既純熟，無惡、無無記，唯有善陰。善陰轉成方便陰，方便陰轉成無漏陰，無漏陰轉成法性陰，謂無等等陰。豈非陰外別有觀耶？小乘尚爾，況不思議耶？問：若轉陰為觀，報陰亦應轉？答：《大品》云：「色淨故，受想行識淨，般若亦淨。」《法華》云：「顏色鮮白，六根清淨。」即其義也。陰雖轉，觀境宛然云云。

Ru-Yi Translation

Question: since the nine remaining objects arise and are each given their own separate name accordingly, should understanding arising with regard to the aggregates and entries likewise be given some separate name of its own? Answer: when understanding arises with regard to the aggregates, this is not something cleanly separate — it still belongs to the aggregates, entries, and elements themselves. Should one cling to this understanding, it then belongs to views; should craving or aversion arise on the basis of this understanding, it belongs to affliction; should it bring on illness or invite māra, each is judged specifically according to what actually occurs. Should understanding arise brightly clear, with none of the marks of the nine objects present at all, this is simply the natural disposition of calming-and-contemplation itself — one may then speak only of "held in common" or "specific," never of "both held in common and specific" together. Question: are the ten objects each cleanly distinct from one another? Answer: the four foundations of mindfulness are the specific mark of the aggregates; contemplating emptiness amid a crowd is the specific mark of the entries; no-self is the specific mark of the elements; the five methods for stilling the mind are the specific mark of affliction; the eight forms of mindfulness are the specific mark of illness; the ten wholesome actions are the specific mark of karma; the five kinds of binding are the specific mark of māra; the six wondrous gates are the specific mark of meditative states; the branches of the path are the specific mark of views; impermanence, suffering, and emptiness are the specific mark of pride; the Four Noble Truths and the twelve links of dependent origination are the specific mark of the two lesser vehicles; the six pāramitās are the specific mark of the bodhisattva. Question: the five aggregates together are themselves the object — is there some further contemplation to be found apart from form and mind? Answer: the wisdom of the inconceivable object is itself already the aggregates as contemplation. One may also distinguish further: the unwholesome and indeterminate aggregates are the object; the wholesome five aggregates are the contemplation. Once contemplation has grown fully ripe, with no evil and no indeterminate state remaining, only the wholesome aggregates are left; the wholesome aggregates are transformed into the aggregates of expedient means, the aggregates of expedient means are transformed into the uncontaminated aggregates, and the uncontaminated aggregates are transformed into the aggregates of Dharma-nature, named the aggregates beyond all comparison. Is this not, then, a contemplation found apart from the aggregates after all? If even the Hīnayāna already holds this much, how much more the inconceivable teaching? Question: if the aggregates are transformed into contemplation, should the aggregates of retribution, too, be transformed? Answer: the Larger Sūtra says, "Because form is pure, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are pure, and prajñā, too, is pure." The Lotus Sūtra says, "The complexion grows fresh and bright, the six faculties pure and clear." This is exactly that same sense. Though the aggregates are transformed, the object of contemplation remains, just as it is.

問：十境與五分云何？答：五分判禪，十發約境。今當會之。若次不次，一發至後，則進分也；齊九已來，住分也；作意矜持，護分也；一發即失，退分也。達分可知。若於境境皆作五分者，可以意推，不俟分別。然五分、十境皆是法相，可得互有其義。六即、十地行位淺深不得相類。問：念性離，緣性亦離；若無緣、無念，亦無數量，云何具十法界耶？答：不可思議，無相而相，觀智宛然。他解須彌容芥，芥容須彌；火出蓮華，人能渡海；就希有事解不思議。今解：無心、無念、無能行、無能到，不思議理，理則勝事。問：十法界互相有，為因、為果？答：俱相有，而果隔難顯，因通易知。如慈童女，以地獄界發佛心。如未得記菩薩，輕得記者，若不生悔，無出罪期。更引諸例：凡聖皆具五陰，不可言聖陰如凡陰。又佛具五眼，豈可以人天果報釋佛眼？佛具五行：病行是四惡界；嬰兒行是人天界；聖行是二乘法界；梵行是菩薩法界；天行是佛法界。問：一念具十法界，為作念具？為任運具？答：法性自爾，非作所成，如一微塵具十方分云云。

Question: how do the ten objects relate to the five aspects of samādhi? Answer: the five aspects judge dhyāna itself; the ten arisings concern the objects. Here, these should be reconciled: if a given arising, sequential or not, advances all the way to the very end, this is the aspect of advancement; sustained steadily up through the ninth stage, this is the aspect of abiding; deliberately maintained with careful restraint, this is the aspect of protection; lost the very instant it arises, this is the aspect of decline. The aspect of penetration may be understood by the same pattern. Should the five aspects be applied to each and every object in turn, this may be grasped by the same reasoning, without needing further separate explanation. Yet the five aspects and the ten objects are both, alike, matters of phenomenal mark, and may well share this sense in common with one another; the six identities and the depth of practice across the stages of the ten grounds, however, cannot be classed together in the same way. Question: the nature of thought is itself apart from all else; the nature of its object, too, is apart from all else — if there is neither object nor thought, and no fixed number or measure to speak of, how, then, could the ten dharma-realms possibly be contained within it at all? Answer: this is inconceivable — without any fixed mark, and yet marked all the same, the contemplative wisdom of it is vividly present. Others explain this through comparison to rare and marvelous events: Mount Sumeru contained within a mustard seed, and the mustard seed containing Mount Sumeru in turn; fire emerging from a lotus flower; a person able to cross the ocean on foot — understanding the inconceivable by way of the extraordinary. Here, the explanation is different: no mind, no thought, no capacity to act, no capacity to arrive — the inconceivable principle is itself already superior to any mere extraordinary event. Question: do the ten dharma-realms mutually contain one another as cause, or as fruit? Answer: they mutually contain one another as both at once, though the fruit remains hidden and difficult to reveal, while the cause is generally accessible and easy to know. It is like the maiden Compassion-Child, who, from within the very realm of hell, gave rise to the buddha-mind. It is like a bodhisattva who has not yet received a prediction of buddhahood belittling one who has already received it — should this not give rise to regret, there is no prospect of ever being released from that offense. To cite further examples: ordinary beings and sages alike are complete with the five aggregates, and yet one cannot say that a sage's own aggregates are the same as an ordinary being's aggregates. The Buddha, too, is complete with the five eyes — how could one possibly explain the buddha-eye in terms of the mere karmic reward of a human or a heavenly being? The Buddha is complete with five practices: the practice of illness is the four evil realms; the practice of the infant is the human and heavenly realms; the practice of the sage is the dharma-realm of the two lesser vehicles; the practice of the pure life is the dharma-realm of the bodhisattva; the practice of the heavens is the dharma-realm of the Buddha. Question: a single thought complete with the ten dharma-realms — is this something deliberately constructed, or is it simply so, effortlessly? Answer: the nature of dharmas is simply so of itself, not something brought about by deliberate construction — just as a single mote of dust already contains within it every direction, in all ten of its aspects.

Reading note: This closing exchange is where the treatise's argument quietly pivots from cataloguing the ten objects toward the doctrine that will dominate the rest of the fascicle: the ten dharma-realms (hell through buddhahood) do not merely succeed one another causally, they already mutually contain one another, "though the fruit remains hidden and difficult to reveal, while the cause is generally accessible and easy to know." The maiden Compassion-Child giving rise to the buddha-mind from within hell itself is the cause made visible; a buddha's own aggregates being nothing like an ordinary being's aggregates, despite the word "aggregates" naming both, is the fruit staying hidden. This is the direct setup for the "three thousand in a single thought" passage that follows immediately once the treatise turns to the first object itself.

The First Object: The Aggregates, Entries, and Elements 觀陰入界境

Chinese Source

第一、觀陰入界境者，謂：五陰、十二入、十八界也。陰者，陰蓋善法，此就因得名。又陰是積聚，生死重沓，此就果得名。入者，涉入，亦名輪門。界名界別，亦名性分。《毘婆沙》明三科開合。若迷心，開心為四陰，色為一陰。若迷色，開色為十入及一入少分，心為一意入及法入少分。若俱迷者，開為十八界也。數人說：「五陰同時，識是心王，四陰是數。」約有門明義故，王、數相扶，同時而起。論人說：「識先了別，次受領納，想取相貌，行起違從，色由行感。」約空門明義故，次第相生。若就能生、所生，從細至麁，故識在前；若從修行，從麁至細，故色在前。皆不得以數隔王。若論四念處，則王在中，此就言說為便耳。又分別九種：一期色心，名果報五陰；平平想受，無記五陰；起見、起愛者，兩污穢五陰；動身口業，善惡兩五陰；變化示現，工巧五陰；五善根人，方便五陰；證四果者，無漏五陰。如是種種，源從心出。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, contemplating the object of the aggregates, entries, and elements: these are the five aggregates, the twelve entries, and the eighteen elements. "Aggregate" (陰) means shading over wholesome dharmas — this takes its name in terms of cause. "Aggregate" also means accumulation, birth-and-death piling up layer upon layer — this takes its name in terms of fruit. "Entry" (入) means passing into, also named the gate of the wheel. "Element" (界) means a boundary distinguishing one thing from another, also named the division of nature. The Vibhāṣā clarifies the opening and closing of these three categories. If one is confused about the mind, mind is opened out into four aggregates, with form as the one remaining aggregate. If one is confused about form, form is opened out into ten entries and a small portion of the eleventh, with mind as the one mental entry and a small portion of the dharma-entry. If one is confused about both, all eighteen elements are opened out in full. The Ābhidharmika masters say: "The five aggregates arise simultaneously — consciousness is the sovereign mind, and the remaining four aggregates are its mental factors." This clarifies the sense in terms of the gate of existence, and so sovereign and factor mutually support one another, arising together at the same instant. The treatise-masters say: "Consciousness first discriminates, then feeling receives and takes it in, perception grasps its features, formations arise in accord or opposition, and form is felt on account of formation." This clarifies the sense in terms of the gate of emptiness, and so each arises from the other in sequence. Considered in terms of what generates and what is generated, moving from the subtle to the coarse, consciousness comes first; considered in terms of actual practice, moving from the coarse to the subtle, form comes first. In neither case should the mental factors be separated from the sovereign mind itself. Discussed in terms of the four foundations of mindfulness, the sovereign mind is placed in the middle — this is simply a matter of convenience in exposition. There are, further, nine distinct kinds to be distinguished: the form and mind of a single lifetime, named the aggregates of karmic retribution; ordinary, undistinguished perception and feeling, the indeterminate aggregates; the aggregates in which view or craving arises, the two defiled aggregates; the aggregates that stir bodily and verbal karma, the two aggregates, wholesome and unwholesome; the aggregates that transform and manifest, the aggregates of skillful craft; the aggregates of the person with the five wholesome roots, the aggregates of expedient means; and the aggregates of one who has realized the four fruits, the uncontaminated aggregates. Every kind of this sort, in every case, has its source in the mind.

《正法念》云：「如畫師手，畫出五彩，黑、青、赤、黃、白、白白。」畫手譬心；黑色譬地獄陰；青色譬鬼；赤譬畜；黃譬脩羅；白譬人；白白譬天。此六種陰，止齊界內。若依《華嚴》云：「心如工畫師，畫種種五陰。」界內、界外，一切世間中，莫不從心造。世間色心尚叵窮盡，況復出世，寧可凡心知？凡眼翳，尚不見近，那得見遠？彌生曠劫，不覩界內一隅，況復界外邊衣？如渴鹿逐炎、狂狗齧雷，何有得理？縱令解悟小乘，終非大道。故《大集》云：「常見之人說異念斷；斷見之人說一念斷。」皆墮二邊，不會中道。況佛去世，後人根轉鈍，執名起諍，互相是非，悉墮邪見。故龍樹破五陰一異、同時、前後，皆如炎、幻、響、化，悉不可得。寧更執於王數同時、異時耶？然界內外一切陰入，皆由心起。佛告比丘：「一法攝一切法，所謂心是。」論云：「一切世間中，但有名與色；若欲如實觀，但當觀名色。」心是惑本，其義如是。若欲觀察，須伐其根，如灸病得穴。今當去丈就尺，去尺就寸；置色等四陰，但觀識陰。識陰者，心是也。

The Sūtra on the Right Mindfulness of the Dharma says: "It is like the painter's own hand, painting the five colors — black, blue, red, yellow, white, and pure white." The painter's hand is a simile for the mind; the color black is a simile for the aggregates of the hell-realm; blue for the hungry ghosts; red for animals; yellow for the asuras; white for humans; and pure white for the heavens. These six kinds of aggregate remain confined within the three realms alone. The Avataṃsaka Sūtra, for its part, says: "The mind is like a skillful painter, painting every kind of aggregate." Within the three realms and beyond them, throughout every world without exception, there is nothing that is not created by the mind. Even the form and mind of the mundane world can scarcely be fathomed to their very limit — how much more, then, the world-transcending, which the ordinary mind could never possibly know? If even the ordinary eye, clouded as it is, cannot see what lies close at hand, how could it possibly see what lies far away? Spanning vast and boundless kalpas, one has still not glimpsed even a single corner within the three realms — how much less, then, the very borders that lie beyond them? It is like a thirsty deer chasing a mirage, or a mad dog snapping at thunder — how could either possibly attain what it seeks? Even should one come to understand the Hīnayāna, this is, in the end, still not the great path. Hence the Great Collection Sūtra says: "Those who hold the view of permanence say that cessation happens by different, successive thoughts; those who hold the view of annihilation say that cessation happens all in a single thought." Both fall into one of the two extremes, and neither accords with the middle way. And how much more so once the Buddha has passed from the world, when later generations grow ever duller of faculty, clinging to mere names and stirring up contention, each side declaring the other wrong, all of them falling into deviant views. Hence Nāgārjuna refutes the five aggregates as one or different, as simultaneous or sequential — all of them, like a mirage, an illusion, an echo, a phantom, ultimately unfindable. How much less, then, should one still cling to whether the sovereign mind and its mental factors arise simultaneously or in sequence? And yet every aggregate, within the three realms and beyond them alike, arises entirely from the mind. The Buddha told the monks: "One dharma gathers in every dharma — this is precisely the mind." The Treatise says: "Throughout the whole of the mundane world, there is only name and form; wishing to contemplate reality as it truly is, one need only contemplate name and form." That the mind is the very root of delusion — this is exactly the sense of it. Wishing to observe this closely, one must strike at its very root, like applying moxibustion directly to the site of the illness. Here, then, one should set aside the fathom in favor of the foot, set aside the foot in favor of the inch: setting aside the four aggregates of form and the rest, one contemplates the aggregate of consciousness alone. The aggregate of consciousness is precisely the mind itself.

Reading note: "Set aside the fathom in favor of the foot, set aside the foot in favor of the inch" is the treatise's own justification for a narrowing of scope that might otherwise look arbitrary: of the eighteen elements, only the mind is actually workable as a direct object of contemplation, since the mind is what the Buddha names as the single dharma that "gathers in every dharma." The painter simile, drawn from both the Sūtra on the Right Mindfulness of the Dharma and the Avataṃsaka, will recur through the entire treatise afterward as shorthand for this same point — the mind does not passively reflect the ten dharma-realms, it paints them.

The Ten Modes of Contemplation 十乘觀法

Chinese Source

觀心具十法門：一、觀不可思議境，二、起慈悲心，三、巧安止觀，四、破法遍，五、識通塞，六、修道品，七、對治助開，八、知次位，九、能安忍，十、無法愛也。既自達妙境；即起誓悲他；次作行填願，願行既巧；破無不遍；遍破之中，精識通塞；令道品進行；又用助開道；道中之位，己他皆識；安忍內外榮辱；莫著中道法愛。故得疾入菩薩位。譬如毘首羯磨造得勝堂，不疎不密，間隙容綖，巍巍昂昂，峙於上天，非拙匠所能揆則。又如善畫，圖其匡郭，寫像偪真，骨法精靈，生氣飛動，豈填彩人所能點綴？此十重觀法，橫竪收束，微妙精巧。初則簡境真偽，中則正助相添，後則安忍無著。意圓法巧，該括周備，規矩初心，將送行者到彼薩雲；非闇證禪師、誦文法師所能知也。蓋由如來積劫之所勤求，道場之所妙悟，身子之所三請，法譬之所三說，正在茲乎。

Ru-Yi Translation

Contemplating the mind is complete with ten modes: first, contemplating the inconceivable object; second, giving rise to the mind of compassion; third, skillfully settling calming-and-contemplation; fourth, comprehensively breaking through every dharma; fifth, distinguishing blockage from passage; sixth, cultivating the branches of the path; seventh, auxiliary remedies to open the way; eighth, knowing one's own stage; ninth, able to bear with adversity; tenth, free of attachment to the Dharma. Once one has directly reached the sublime object oneself, one gives rise to the vow of compassion for others; next, one carries out the practice that fulfills that vow, and once vow and practice are both skillfully joined, there is nothing left unbroken by this comprehensive breaking-through; in the very midst of this comprehensive breaking-through, one precisely distinguishes blockage from passage, so that the branches of the path may advance; one further applies auxiliary remedies to open the way; the stages along the path, one's own and others', are both fully known; one bears with honor and disgrace, inward and outward, with patience; and one clings to no attachment, not even to the Dharma of the middle way itself. Only thus is one able to enter the bodhisattva's own stage with speed. It is like Viśvakarman constructing the finest of halls: neither too sparse nor too dense, its every joint fitted precisely to the width of a single thread, towering aloft, standing against the very heavens — no clumsy craftsman could ever plan or measure such a thing. It is also like a skilled painter sketching out the outline, rendering the image true to life, its very bone-structure vivid and alive, its breath of life seeming to leap and move — how could a mere colorist, filling in paint, ever accomplish such a thing? These ten modes of contemplation, gathered together both horizontally and vertically, are subtle, sublime, and exquisitely refined: at the outset, they discern the true from the false among the objects; in the middle, they add the auxiliary to the principal; and at the close, they bear with adversity, free of any attachment at all. The intent is complete, the method skillful, comprehensive and thorough in every particular, laying down the very compass and square for the beginner, sending the practitioner on the way to the ocean of omniscience itself — this is not something a meditation teacher realized only in silent seclusion, or a Dharma-master who has merely memorized the texts, could ever come to know. It is precisely here that lies what the Tathāgata sought with such diligence across accumulated kalpas, what he awakened to so sublimely at the very seat of enlightenment, what Śāriputra himself begged three times to hear, and what was taught three times over, through both the Dharma and its similes together.

Reading note: The architectural and painting similes here are doing careful structural work: Viśvakarman's hall (the divine craftsman of Indra's own palace) stresses that the ten modes are load-bearing and interdependent, not decorative — remove one joint and the structure fails; the painter's outline stresses that the first mode, contemplating the inconceivable object, is the "bone-structure" the remaining nine only fill in and color. Fascicle 5 will complete only the first four of these ten modes for the first of the ten objects; the remaining six, and the other nine objects, are left for later fascicles — a scale that itself explains why this single mode, contemplating the inconceivable object, is about to become the treatise's longest and most demanding single passage.

Mode One: Contemplating the Inconceivable Object 觀不可思議境

Chinese Source

一、觀心是不可思議境者，此境難說，先明思議境，令不思議境易顯。思議法者，小乘亦說心生一切法，謂六道因果、三界輪環。若去凡欣聖，則棄下上出，灰身滅智，乃是有作四諦，蓋思議法也。大乘亦明心生一切法，謂十法界也。若觀心是有，有善有惡，惡則三品，三途因果也；善則三品，脩羅、人、天因果。觀此六品無常生滅；能觀之心，亦念念不住。又能觀、所觀悉是緣生；緣生即空，並是二乘因果法也。若觀此空、有，墮落二邊，沈空滯有，而起大慈悲入假化物。實無身，假作身；實無空，假說空而化導之，即菩薩因果法也。觀此法能度、所度，皆是中道實相之法，畢竟清淨。誰善、誰惡？誰有、誰無？誰度、誰不度？一切法悉如是，是佛因果法也。此之十法，邐迆淺深，皆從心出。雖是大乘無量四諦所攝，猶是思議之境，非今止觀所觀也。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, contemplating the mind as the inconceivable object: this object is difficult to explain in words, so the conceivable object shall first be clarified, so that the inconceivable object may thereby become easier to reveal. As for the conceivable dharma: even the Hīnayāna teaches that the mind gives rise to every dharma, meaning the causation of the six destinies, the wheel turning through the three realms. Should one abandon the ordinary and long for the sagely, casting off the lower to emerge into the higher, extinguishing the body and annihilating the intellect — this is the Four Noble Truths of the conditioned, and is itself already a conceivable dharma. The Mahāyāna, too, clarifies that the mind gives rise to every dharma, meaning the ten dharma-realms. Should one contemplate the mind as existing, existing as both good and evil — evil, in turn, having three grades, the causation of the three evil destinies; good having three grades, the causation of the asuras, humans, and heavenly beings. Contemplating these six grades as impermanent, arising and ceasing; and the very mind that contemplates them, too, never remaining fixed even for a single thought — and further, that both the contemplating mind and what is contemplated alike arise from conditions, and what arises from conditions is itself already empty — this is the dharma of causation belonging to the two lesser vehicles. Should one, contemplating this emptiness and this existence, fall into one of the two extremes, sinking into emptiness or stuck fast in existence, and thereupon give rise to great compassion, entering the provisional to transform beings — with, in reality, no body, and yet provisionally taking on a body; with, in reality, no emptiness, and yet provisionally speaking of emptiness in order to guide and transform others — this is the dharma of causation belonging to the bodhisattva. Contemplating this very dharma, both the one who delivers and the one delivered, as themselves, every one, the dharma of the true mark of the middle way, ultimately pure — who, then, is good, and who is evil? Who has, and who lacks? Who is delivered, and who is not? Every dharma is, entirely, just so — this is the dharma of causation belonging to the Buddha. These ten dharmas, spread out in shallow and deep succession, all issue from the mind alike. Though this is already what the Mahāyāna's own immeasurable Four Noble Truths gathers in, it remains, even so, a conceivable object — not what is contemplated by the calming-and-contemplation discussed here.

不可思議境者，如《華嚴》云：「心如工畫師，造種種五陰；一切世間中，莫不從心造。」種種五陰者，如前十法界五陰也。法界者三義，十數是能依，法界是所依，能所合稱，故言「十法界」。又此十法各各因，各各果，不相混濫，故言「十法界」。又此十法一一當體皆是法界，故言「十時界」云云。十法界通稱陰入界，其實不同：三途是有漏惡陰界入；三善是有漏善陰界入；二乘是無漏陰界入；菩薩是亦有漏亦無漏陰界入；佛是非有漏非無漏陰界入。《釋論》云：「法無上者，涅槃是。」即非有漏非無漏法也。《無量義經》云「佛無諸大陰界入」者，無前九陰界入也；今言有者，有涅槃常住陰界入也。《大經》云：「因滅無常色，獲得常色；受想行識亦復如是。」常樂重沓，即積聚義；慈悲覆蓋，即陰義。以十種陰界不同故，故名「五陰世間」也。

As for the inconceivable object: as the Avataṃsaka Sūtra says, "The mind is like a skillful painter, creating every kind of aggregate; throughout every world, there is nothing that is not created by the mind." "Every kind of aggregate" means precisely the five aggregates belonging to each of the ten dharma-realms named above. "Dharma-realm" has three senses: the ten realms themselves are what depends, and the dharma-realm is what is depended upon; combining what depends and what is depended upon, one speaks of "the ten dharma-realms." Further, these ten dharmas each have their own separate cause and their own separate fruit, never mingled or confused with one another, and this too is why they are called "the ten dharma-realms." Further still, each and every one of these ten dharmas is itself, in its own very substance, already the dharma-realm — hence they are also spoken of as "the ten realms of the moment." The ten dharma-realms are generally called, alike, "the aggregates, entries, and elements," and yet, in reality, they are not the same at all: the three evil destinies have the contaminated, evil aggregates, entries, and elements; the three wholesome destinies have the contaminated, wholesome aggregates, entries, and elements; the two lesser vehicles have the uncontaminated aggregates, entries, and elements; the bodhisattva has aggregates, entries, and elements that are both contaminated and uncontaminated at once; and the Buddha has aggregates, entries, and elements that are neither contaminated nor uncontaminated. The Treatise says: "That which is unsurpassed among dharmas is nirvāṇa" — meaning precisely the dharma that is neither contaminated nor uncontaminated. The Sūtra of Immeasurable Meanings says that "the Buddha has none of the great aggregates, entries, and elements," meaning that he lacks the first nine kinds of aggregates, entries, and elements; and where it is said that he does have them, this means he has the aggregates, entries, and elements of nirvāṇa's own eternal abiding. The Nirvāṇa Sūtra says: "By means of the extinguishing of impermanent form, one obtains eternal form; feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are exactly the same." The eternal and the blissful piling up together is exactly the sense of "accumulation"; compassion covering over and sheltering is exactly the sense of "shading." Because these ten kinds of aggregates and elements each differ from the rest, this is exactly why they are named "the world of the five aggregates."

Reading note: The distinction between "conceivable" and "inconceivable" objects is not a distinction between easy and hard doctrine — both versions given here already teach that mind gives rise to all ten dharma-realms. What makes the Hīnayāna and even this first Mahāyāna account still "conceivable" is that they present the ten realms as a sequence to be climbed, evil left behind for good, saṃsāra left behind for nirvāṇa. The reclassification about to follow — where the Buddha's own aggregates and a hell-being's aggregates share the very name "aggregate" while being utterly unlike each other, and where the "world of the five aggregates" is defined by their difference rather than their sequence — is the first sign that this passage is headed somewhere the "conceivable" framing cannot reach.

The World of Sentient Beings, and the World of Lands 眾生世間 · 國土世間

Chinese Source

攬五陰通稱眾生，眾生不同：攬三途陰，罪苦眾生；攬人天陰，受樂眾生；攬無漏陰，真聖眾生；攬慈悲陰，大士眾生；攬常住陰，尊極眾生。《大論》云：「眾生無上者，佛是。」豈與凡下同？《大經》云：「歌羅邏時名字異，乃至老時名字異。芽時名字異，乃至果時名字亦異。」直約一期十時差別，況十界眾生，寧得不異？故名「眾生世間」也。十種所居通稱「國土世間」者：地獄依赤鐵住；畜生依地、水、空住；修羅依海畔、海底住；人依地住；天依宮殿住。六度菩薩，同人依地住。通教菩薩惑未盡，同人、天依住；斷惑盡者，依方便土住。別圓菩薩惑未盡者，同人、天、方便等住；斷惑盡者，依實報土住。如來依常寂光土住。《仁王經》云：「三賢十聖住果報，唯佛一人居淨土。」土土不同，故名「國土世間」也。

Ru-Yi Translation

Gathering together the five aggregates is generally called "sentient being," and yet sentient beings differ from one another: gathering the aggregates of the three evil destinies together makes a suffering, guilty sentient being; gathering the aggregates of humans and heavenly beings together makes a pleasure-receiving sentient being; gathering the uncontaminated aggregates together makes a true and holy sentient being; gathering the aggregates of compassion together makes a great being, a bodhisattva sentient being; gathering the aggregates of eternal abiding together makes the most honored and ultimate sentient being. The Treatise says: "The unsurpassed among sentient beings is the Buddha" — how, then, could this be the same as an ordinary, base being? The Nirvāṇa Sūtra says: "At the kalala stage the name differs; and so on, up through old age, the name differs again. At the sprout stage the name differs; and so on, up through the fruit, the name differs again." This concerns only the differences across ten stages of a single lifetime — how much more, then, must sentient beings across the ten dharma-realms differ from one another? Hence this is named "the world of sentient beings." As for the ten kinds of dwelling-places, generally called "the world of lands": the hell-realm dwells upon red-hot iron; animals dwell on land, in water, and in the air; asuras dwell at the ocean's edge or at its very bottom; humans dwell upon the earth; heavenly beings dwell within their own palaces. The bodhisattva of the six pāramitās dwells upon the earth, the same as humans. The shared-teaching bodhisattva whose affliction is not yet exhausted dwells together with humans and heavenly beings; once affliction is exhausted, they dwell within a land of expedient means. The distinct- and perfect-teaching bodhisattva whose affliction is not yet exhausted dwells together with humans, heavenly beings, and those in the land of expedient means alike; once affliction is exhausted, they dwell within the land of true reward. The Tathāgata dwells within the land of eternally quiescent light. The Sūtra of Benevolent Kings says: "The three grades of worthy and the ten grades of sage dwell within a land of karmic reward; the Buddha alone dwells within the pure land." Each land differing from the rest in this way is exactly why this is named "the world of lands."

The Ten Suchnesses, I: Mark and Nature 十如是 · 相性

Chinese Source

此三十種世間，悉從心造。又十種五陰，一一各具十法，謂：如是相、性、體、力、作、因、緣、果、報、本末究竟等。先總釋，後隨類釋。總釋者，夫相以據外，覽而可別。《釋論》云：「易知故名為相。如水火相異，則易可知。」如人面色具諸休否，覽外相即知其內。昔孫、劉相顯，曹公相隱；相者舉聲大哭：「四海三分，百姓荼毒！」若言有相，闇者不知；若言無相，占者洞解。當隨善相者，信人面外具一切相也。心亦如是，具一切相。眾生相隱，彌勒相顯，如來善知，故遠近皆記。不善觀者，不信心具一切相。當隨如實觀者，信心具一切相也。

Ru-Yi Translation

These thirty kinds of world, in every case, are created by the mind. Further, each of these ten kinds of five aggregates is, each and every one, complete with ten dharmas, namely: such is the mark, such is the nature, such is the substance, such is the power, such is the activity, such is the cause, such is the condition, such is the effect, such is the retribution, and such is the ultimate consistency of root and branch. First a general explanation shall be given, and afterward an explanation category by category. As for the general explanation: "mark" is what is grasped from the outside, observed and thereby distinguished. The Treatise says: "It is called 'mark' because it is easily known — just as the marks of water and fire differ from one another, and so are easily told apart." Just as a person's own facial complexion already carries every sign of good or ill fortune, so that observing the outward mark reveals what lies within: in the past, when Sun Quan's and Liu Bei's own marks were plain to see, while Cao Cao's own mark remained hidden, a physiognomist cried aloud in great lamentation, "The realm shall be divided into three, and its people ground down in suffering!" If one says there is a mark, the unperceiving fail to notice it; if one says there is no mark, the diviner sees straight through to it all the same. One should follow the good physiognomist, and trust that a person's own outward face already carries every mark within it. The mind, too, is exactly the same, complete with every mark. In an ordinary sentient being, this mark remains hidden; in Maitreya, this mark is already plain to see; the Tathāgata, knowing this well, thereby gives predictions of buddhahood to those near and far alike. One who observes poorly does not believe the mind is complete with every mark; one should instead follow one who observes things as they truly are, and trust that the mind is complete with every mark.

如是性者，性以據內，總有三義。一、不改名性，《無行經》稱「不動性」，性即不改義也。又性名「性分」，種類之義，分分不同，各各不可改。又性是實性，實性即理性。極實無過，即佛性異名耳。不動性扶空；種性扶假；實性扶中。今明內性不可改，如竹中火性，雖不可見，不得言無；燧人乾草，遍燒一切。心亦如是，具一切五陰性，雖不可見，不得言無；以智眼觀，具一切性。世間人可笑，以其偏聞判圓經。《涅槃》明「佛知眾生有佛性」，判為極常；《法華》明「佛知一切法如是性」，判為無常。豈可以少知為常，多知為無常？又《法華》云：「佛知一切法，皆是一種一性。」此語亦少，何故判為無常？又有師判「法華十如」，前五如屬凡，是權；後五屬聖，為實。依汝所判，則凡無實，永不得成聖；聖無權，非正遍知。此乃專輒之說，誣佛慢凡耳。又《涅槃》明「一切眾生悉有佛性」，而言是常；《淨名》云「一切眾生即菩提相」，判是無常。若佛性、菩提相異者，可一常、一無常；若不異者，此判大謬。如占者見王相、王性，俱得登極；佛性、菩提相，何故不同？

As for "such is the nature": nature is what is grasped from within, and has, in general, three senses. First, "unchanging" is named nature — the Sūtra on the Practice of Non-Action calls this "the unmoving nature," nature being precisely the sense of the unchanging. Second, nature is named "the division of nature," the sense of a distinct category, each division differing from the next, and none of them able to be changed into another. Third, nature is the real nature, the real nature being precisely the nature of principle — the utmost real, beyond any further error, simply another name for the buddha-nature. The unmoving nature supports emptiness; the seed-nature supports the provisional; the real nature supports the middle. Here, it is clarified that the inward nature cannot be changed — just as the nature of fire already exists within bamboo, though it cannot be seen, one cannot say it does not exist; struck by flint against dry grass, it burns everything without exception. The mind, too, is exactly the same, complete with the nature of every one of the five aggregates — though it cannot be seen, one cannot say it does not exist; observed with the eye of wisdom, it is seen to be complete with every nature. It is laughable how people of the world judge a perfect sūtra on the basis of some partial hearing of it alone. The Nirvāṇa Sūtra clarifies that "the Buddha knows sentient beings possess the buddha-nature," and this is judged to describe the utmost permanence; the Lotus Sūtra clarifies that "the Buddha knows that every dharma has such-and-such a nature," and this is judged to describe impermanence instead. How could a lesser knowledge possibly be judged permanent, and a greater knowledge impermanent? Moreover, the Lotus Sūtra says: "The Buddha knows every dharma is, alike, of a single kind, a single nature" — this statement, too, is no less brief, so why should it be judged to describe impermanence? There is, further, a certain master who judges the Lotus Sūtra's own "ten suchnesses" so that the first five suchnesses belong to the ordinary being, as the provisional, while the last five belong to the sage, as the real. Following this master's own judgment, the ordinary being would then have no reality at all, and could never come to attain sagehood; and the sage would have no provisional means at all, and would thus fail to be truly and universally aware. This is simply an arbitrary, self-appointed claim, one that slanders the Buddha and disparages the ordinary being alike. There is, further, the Nirvāṇa Sūtra's clarification that "every sentient being, without exception, possesses the buddha-nature," said to be permanent; while the Vimalakīrti Sūtra's statement that "every sentient being is itself already the mark of bodhi" is judged to describe impermanence. If the buddha-nature and the mark of bodhi were genuinely different from each other, then one might indeed be permanent and the other impermanent; but if they are not different, this judgment is a grave error indeed. Just as a physiognomist, seeing the mark and the nature of a king, knows both alike that he will ascend to the throne — why, then, should the buddha-nature and the mark of bodhi be judged any differently from one another?
