Reading note: The ten suchnesses (十如是) are drawn from a single sentence of the Lotus Sūtra's "Skillful Means" chapter — mark, nature, substance, power, activity, cause, condition, effect, retribution, and the ultimate consistency of root and branch — and this passage is Zhiyi's own extended argument for reading all ten as a single continuous claim about every dharma, rather than the rival school's attempt to split them five-and-five between ordinary beings and sages. His objection is characteristically sharp: that reading would leave "the ordinary being with no reality at all" and "the sage with no provisional means at all," severing exactly the continuity between the two that the whole treatise, since fascicle 1's six identities, has been at pains to establish.

The Ten Suchnesses, II: Substance Through Ultimate Consistency 十如是 · 體力作因緣果報本末究竟等

Chinese Source

如是體者，主質故名「體」。此十法界陰俱用色心為體質也。如是力者，堪任力用也。如王力士，千萬技能，病故謂無，病差有用。心亦如是，具有諸力，煩惱病故，不能運動，如實觀之，具一切力。如是作者，運為建立名「作」。若離心者，更無所作。故知心具一切作也。如是因者，招果為因，亦名為「業」。十法界業，起自於心；但使有心，諸業具足，故名「如是因」也。如是緣者，緣名「緣由」；助業皆是緣義。無明、愛等能潤於業，即心為緣也。如是果者，剋獲為果。習因習續於前，習果剋獲於後，故言「如是果」也。如是報者，酬因曰「報」。習因、習果通名為「因」，牽後世報，此報酬於因也。如是本末究竟等者，相為本，報為末。本末悉從緣生，緣生故空；本末皆空，此就空為等也。又相但有字，報亦但有字，悉假施設，此就假名為等。又本末互相表幟，覽初相，表後報；覩後報，知本相；如見施知富，見富知施，初後相在，此就假論等也。又相無相，無相而相，非相非無相；報無報，無報而報，非報非無報，一一皆入如實之際，此就中論等也。

Ru-Yi Translation

As for "such is the substance": that which serves as the governing basis is named "substance" — the aggregates of all ten dharma-realms alike take form and mind together as their own substance and basis. As for "such is the power": this is the capacity to bear and function. Like a mighty strongman in the king's service, possessed of countless skills — when ill, he is said to have none of them; once his illness is cured, they are put to use once more. The mind, too, is exactly the same, complete with every power; on account of the sickness of affliction, it is unable to exert itself, but observed as it truly is, it is complete with every power. As for "such is the activity": exerting and establishing is named "activity." Apart from the mind, there is nothing further that acts at all — hence it is known that the mind is complete with every activity. As for "such is the cause": bringing about a fruit is named "cause," also named "karma." The karma of all ten dharma-realms arises from the mind alone; wherever mind is present, every karma is thereby already complete — hence this is named "such is the cause." As for "such is the condition": "condition" means that by which something comes about; whatever assists karma is, in every case, this same sense of condition. Ignorance, craving, and the like are able to moisten karma into growth — this, too, is the mind serving as condition. As for "such is the effect": attaining and obtaining is named "effect." The habituated cause continues on from what came before, and the habituated effect is what is obtained afterward — hence this is said to be "such is the effect." As for "such is the retribution": requiting a cause is named "retribution." The habituated cause and the habituated effect together are generally named "cause," drawing forth the retribution of a future life — this retribution is what requites that cause. As for "such is the ultimate consistency of root and branch": the mark serves as the root, and the retribution as the branch. Root and branch alike arise entirely from conditions; arising from conditions, they are thus empty — root and branch both being empty is what makes them "consistent" in terms of emptiness. Further, the mark has only a name, and the retribution, too, has only a name, both alike provisionally established — this is what makes them "consistent" in terms of the provisional name. Further, root and branch mutually signify one another: seeing the initial mark signifies the later retribution; observing the later retribution, one knows the initial mark — just as seeing generosity, one knows there is wealth, and seeing wealth, one knows there was generosity, the beginning and the end each present within the other — this is discussing their "consistency" in terms of the provisional. Further, the mark is without mark, markless and yet marked, neither marked nor unmarked; the retribution is without retribution, without retribution and yet retribution, neither retribution nor without retribution — each and every one entering into the very bounds of reality as it truly is — this is discussing their "consistency" in terms of the middle.

Reading note: The final suchness — "the ultimate consistency of root and branch" — is read here through all three truths at once, exactly like every other pivotal term across fascicles 3 through 5: consistent because both mark and retribution are equally empty, consistent because both are equally provisional names, and consistent because both, examined directly, are neither marked nor unmarked, neither retribution nor its absence. The physiognomy and strongman similes used across this whole ten-suchness passage share one underlying claim: dormant capacity is not absence. A strongman's skill is real even while illness prevents him from exerting it; the mind's own ten suchnesses are real for every one of the ten dharma-realms even where affliction currently keeps most of them from manifesting.

The Ten Suchnesses Across Four Categories 十如是 · 類解

Chinese Source

二、類解者，束十法為四類。三途以表苦為相，定惡聚為性，摧折色心為體，登刀入鑊為力，起十不善為作，有漏惡業為因，愛取等為緣，惡習果為果，三惡趣為報，本末皆癡為等。三善表樂為相，定善聚為性，升出色心為體，樂受為力，起五戒、十善為作，白業為因，善愛取為緣，善習果為果，人天有為報，應就假名初後相在為等也。二乘表涅槃為相，解脫為性，五分為體，無繫為力，道品為作，無漏慧行為因，行行為緣，四果為果，既後有田中不生，故無報云云。菩薩、佛類者，緣因為相，了因為性，正因為體，四弘為力，六度萬行為作，智慧莊嚴為因，福德莊嚴為緣，三菩提為果，大涅槃為報云云。因緣有逆順：順生死者，有漏業為因，愛取等為緣；逆生死者，以無漏正慧為因，行行為緣，俱損生破惑。順界外生死，亦以無漏慧為因，無明等為緣；若逆生死，即以中道慧為因，萬行為緣，俱損變易生死故。因緣既爾，餘者逆順，准此可知。若依聲聞，但九無十；若依大乘三佛義，佛有報身。若依斷惑盡義，則無後報。九之與十，斟酌可解。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, explanation by category: gathering the ten dharmas into four categories. For the three evil destinies: expressing suffering is the mark; fixed in the accumulation of evil is the nature; form and mind broken and crushed is the substance; climbing the mountain of blades and entering the cauldron is the power; giving rise to the ten unwholesome acts is the activity; contaminated evil karma is the cause; craving, grasping, and the rest are the condition; the fruit of habituated evil is the effect; the three evil destinies are the retribution; and delusion running through both root and branch alike is their consistency. For the three wholesome destinies: expressing bliss is the mark; fixed in the accumulation of good is the nature; form and mind rising and emerging is the substance; pleasant feeling is the power; giving rise to the five precepts and the ten wholesome actions is the activity; unblemished karma is the cause; wholesome craving and grasping are the condition; the fruit of habituated good is the effect; existence among humans and heavenly beings is the retribution; and this should be discussed as their consistency in terms of the provisional name, with beginning and end each present in the other. For the two lesser vehicles: expressing nirvāṇa is the mark; liberation is the nature; the five aggregates of the Dharma-body are the substance; freedom from bondage is the power; the branches of the path are the activity; uncontaminated practice of wisdom is the cause; the practice of practice is the condition; the four fruits are the effect; and since no further existence is sown in the field of future birth, there is no retribution at all. For the category of bodhisattva and Buddha: the conditioning cause is the mark; the illuminating cause is the nature; the correct cause is the substance; the four universal vows are the power; the six pāramitās and the ten thousand practices are the activity; the adornment of wisdom is the cause; the adornment of merit is the condition; the three kinds of bodhi are the effect; and the great nirvāṇa is the retribution. Cause and condition run both forward and in reverse: following the course of birth-and-death, contaminated karma is the cause, and craving and grasping are the condition; reversing the course of birth-and-death, uncontaminated correct wisdom is the cause, and the practice of practice is the condition, both together diminishing birth and breaking through delusion. Following the course of birth-and-death beyond the three realms, uncontaminated wisdom is likewise the cause, and ignorance and the rest are the condition; reversing that course, the wisdom of the middle way is the cause, and the ten thousand practices are the condition, both together diminishing the birth-and-death of mutable transformation. Cause and condition being thus, the forward and reverse course of the rest may be understood by the same pattern. Following the śrāvaka's own account, there are only nine suchnesses, lacking the tenth; following the Mahāyāna's own doctrine of the three buddha-bodies, the Buddha does possess a reward-body; following the sense in which affliction is exhausted entirely, there is no further retribution at all. Nine suchnesses or ten — this may be reconciled and understood with careful consideration.

Reading note: Running the same ten suchnesses through four very different categories — the three evil destinies, the three wholesome destinies, the two lesser vehicles, and the bodhisattva-and-Buddha together — is what makes the doctrine genuinely comprehensive rather than merely abstract: "such is the cause" names contaminated evil karma for a hell-being and the adornment of wisdom for a bodhisattva, the same formal category doing entirely different work depending on what fills it. The closing note on whether the tenth suchness (retribution) applies to a buddha at all — present under the Mahāyāna's three-body doctrine, absent once affliction is fully exhausted — is a small, honest acknowledgment that even Zhiyi's own scheme has an unresolved edge case rather than a tidy universal fit.

Three Thousand in a Single Thought 一念三千

Chinese Source

眾生世間既是假名無體，分別攬實法假施設耳。所謂惡道眾生相、性、體、力、究竟等云云；善道眾生相、性、體、力、究竟等；無漏眾生相、性、體、力、究竟等；菩薩、佛法界相、性、體、力、究竟等，准例皆可解。國土世間亦具十種法，所謂惡國土相、性、體、力等云云；善國土、無漏國土、佛菩薩國土相、性、體、力云云。夫一心具十法界，一法界又具十法界，百法界；一界具三十種世間；百法界即具三千種世間。此三千在一念心。若無心而已；介爾有心，即具三千。亦不言一心在前，一切法在後；亦不言一切法在前，一心在後。例如八相遷物，物在相前，物不被遷；相在物前，亦不被遷。前亦不可，後亦不可；秖物論相遷，秖相遷論物。今心亦如是，若從一心生一切法者，此則是縱；若心一時含一切法者，此即是橫。縱亦不可，橫亦不可；秖心是一切法，一切法是心。故非縱非橫，非一非異，玄妙深絕，非識所識，非言所言，所以稱為「不可思議境」，意在於此云云。

Ru-Yi Translation

The world of sentient beings, since it is only a provisional name without any substance of its own, is simply distinguished by gathering together real dharmas, provisionally established. This means: the mark, nature, substance, power, and ultimate consistency of a sentient being of the evil destinies; the mark, nature, substance, power, and ultimate consistency of a sentient being of the wholesome destinies; the mark, nature, substance, power, and ultimate consistency of an uncontaminated sentient being; and the mark, nature, substance, power, and ultimate consistency of the bodhisattva- and buddha-dharma-realms — each may be understood by the same pattern. The world of lands, too, is complete with these same ten dharmas: the mark, nature, substance, and power of an evil land; and, likewise, of a wholesome land, an uncontaminated land, and the land of buddhas and bodhisattvas.

Now, a single mind is complete with the ten dharma-realms, and each single dharma-realm is, in turn, complete with all ten dharma-realms, yielding a hundred dharma-realms; each one of these hundred realms is complete with all thirty kinds of world, and so the hundred dharma-realms are thereby complete with three thousand kinds of world. These three thousand lie within a single moment of thought. If there is no mind at all, then that is simply so; but the moment there is mind, however slight, it is at once complete with these three thousand. Nor should one say that the single mind comes first and every dharma comes after; nor should one say that every dharma comes first and the single mind comes after. It is like the eight marks that carry a thing through change: if the thing existed before the marks, it would not actually be carried through change by them; if the marks existed before the thing, they too would not actually carry it through change. Neither "before" nor "after" will do — one can only say that the thing is discussed in terms of the marks that carry it through change, and that the marks that carry it through change are discussed in terms of the thing itself. The mind, too, is now exactly the same: if all dharmas were said to be generated from the single mind, this would be a vertical relation; if the mind were said to contain every dharma all at the same instant, this would be a horizontal relation. Neither the vertical nor the horizontal will do — one can only say that the mind is itself every dharma, and every dharma is itself the mind. Hence it is neither vertical nor horizontal, neither one nor different, so profound and sublime that it defies every measure, not something known by ordinary consciousness, not something spoken by ordinary speech — and this is exactly why it is named "the inconceivable object." The meaning lies precisely here.

Reading note: This is the single most influential paragraph Zhiyi ever wrote — the source of what later Tiantai scholasticism would name yinian sanqian (一念三千), "three thousand in a single thought," the doctrine that every possible realm of existence is already fully present, entire, in the most ordinary passing moment of mind. The arithmetic (ten realms mutually containing ten realms is a hundred; a hundred realms each complete with ten suchnesses across three worlds is three thousand) is almost incidental to the real claim, which is about relation, not number: mind neither precedes the three thousand nor is generated alongside them as a separate item — "the mind is itself every dharma, and every dharma is itself the mind." The eight-marks simile, borrowed from Abhidharma metaphysics on how a conditioned thing is said to change, is doing exactly this work: asking whether the marks of change belong to the thing or the thing to the marks turns out to be the wrong question, and mind and the three thousand realms stand in exactly that same "neither vertical nor horizontal" relation to each other.

Does Mind, or Condition, Possess the Three Thousand? 一念三千 · 四句推撿

Chinese Source

問：心起必託緣，為心具三千法？為緣具？為共具？為離具？若心具者，心起不用緣；若緣具者，緣具不關心；若共具者，未共各無，共時安有？若離具者，既離心、離緣，那忽心具？四句尚不可得，云何具三千法耶？答：地人云：「一切解惑真妄，依持法性。法性持真妄；真妄依法性也。」《攝大乘》云：「法性不為惑所染，不為真所淨，故法性非依持。言依持者，阿黎耶是也。無沒無明盛持一切種子。」若從地師，則心具一切法；若從攝師，則緣具一切法。此兩師，各據一邊。若法性生一切法者，法性非心非緣。非心故而心生一切法者，非緣故亦應緣生一切法，何得獨言法性是真妄依持耶？若言法性非依持，黎耶是依持；離法性外，別有黎耶依持，則不關法性。若法性不離黎耶，黎耶依持即是法性依持，何得獨言黎耶是依持？又違經，經言：「非內非外，亦非中間，亦不常自有。」又違龍樹，龍樹云：「諸法不自生，亦不從他生，不共、不無因。」

Ru-Yi Translation

Question: since the mind arises only in dependence on conditions, does the mind alone possess the three thousand dharmas? Do the conditions alone possess them? Do both together possess them? Or are they possessed apart from both? If the mind alone possesses them, then the mind's own arising would need no conditions at all; if the conditions alone possess them, then the conditions' own possession of them would have nothing to do with the mind; if both together possess them, then before they came together, neither would possess them individually — how, then, could they possess them once combined? If they are possessed apart from both, then, being apart from both mind and condition, how could the mind suddenly possess them at all? Since not even one of these four propositions can be sustained, how could the three thousand dharmas possibly be possessed at all? Answer: the masters of the Dilun tradition say: "Every instance of understanding and delusion, of the real and the false, relies upon and is sustained by the Dharma-nature. The Dharma-nature sustains both the real and the false; the real and the false depend upon the Dharma-nature." The Compendium of the Mahāyāna says: "The Dharma-nature is neither defiled by delusion nor purified by the real, and so the Dharma-nature is not itself what sustains. What is called the 'sustaining basis' is the ālayavijñāna itself — an unsubmerged, ever-present ignorance that holds and sustains every seed." Following the Dilun masters, then, the mind alone possesses every dharma; following the Compendium's own master, then, the conditions alone possess every dharma. These two masters, however, each rest their claim on only one side of the matter. If the Dharma-nature itself gives rise to every dharma, then the Dharma-nature is neither mind nor condition; and if, being neither mind, it can still give rise to every dharma through the mind, then, being neither condition either, it ought equally to give rise to every dharma through conditions as well — how, then, could one claim, one-sidedly, that the Dharma-nature alone is what sustains both the real and the false? And if one says the Dharma-nature itself is not the sustaining basis, and the ālayavijñāna is instead the sustaining basis, then, apart from the Dharma-nature, there would be some separate ālayavijñāna serving as the sustaining basis, having nothing to do with the Dharma-nature at all. But if the Dharma-nature is not, in fact, apart from the ālayavijñāna, then the ālayavijñāna's own sustaining is itself already the Dharma-nature's own sustaining — so how, then, could one claim, one-sidedly, that the ālayavijñāna alone is what sustains? This, moreover, contradicts the sūtra, which says: "Neither within, nor without, nor in between the two, nor ever constantly existing of itself." It further contradicts Nāgārjuna, who says: "No dharma arises from itself, nor from another, nor from both together, nor without any cause at all."

更就譬檢，為當依心，故有夢？依眠，故有夢？眠法合心，故有夢？離心、離眠，故有夢？若依心有夢者，不眠應有夢。若依眠有夢者，死人如眠，應有夢。若眠心兩合而有夢者，眠人那有不夢時？又眠心各有夢，合可有夢？各既無夢，合不應有。若離心、離眠而有夢者，虛空離二，應常有夢。四句求夢尚不得，云何於眠夢見一切事？心喻法性，眠喻黎耶。云何偏據法性、黎耶生一切法？當知四句求心不可得，求三千法亦不可得。既橫從四句生三千法不可得者，應從一念心滅生三千法耶？心滅尚不能生一法，云何能生三千法耶？若從心亦滅亦不滅生三千法者，亦滅亦不滅其性相違；猶如水火二俱不立，云何能生三千法耶？若謂心非滅非不滅生三千法者，非滅非不滅非能、非所，云何能、所生三千法耶？亦縱亦橫求三千法不可得，非縱非橫求三千法亦不可得。言語道斷，心行處滅，故名「不可思議境」。大經云：「生生不可說；生不生不可說；不生生不可說；不生不生不可說。」即此義也。當知第一義中一法不可得，況三千法？世諦中一心尚具無量法，況三千耶？

Examining this further by way of a simile: does a dream depend on the mind? Does it depend on sleep? Does it arise because the dharma of sleep is joined with the mind? Or does it arise apart from both mind and sleep? If a dream depended on the mind alone, then one ought to dream even while not asleep. If it depended on sleep alone, then a dead person, being like one asleep, ought to dream as well. If a dream arose from the joining of sleep and mind together, how could a sleeping person ever have a moment without a dream? And further, if sleep and mind each already had a dream of their own individually, would joining them together really produce yet another dream? And if, individually, neither had any dream at all, their joining ought not to produce one either. If a dream arose apart from both mind and sleep, then empty space itself, apart from both, ought to dream constantly as well. Since not even these four propositions can account for a dream, how, then, could one see every event of one's life within a dream while asleep at all? The mind is a simile for the Dharma-nature; sleep is a simile for the ālayavijñāna. How, then, could one one-sidedly claim that the Dharma-nature or the ālayavijñāna alone gives rise to every dharma? One should know that the mind cannot be found through any of these four propositions, and the three thousand dharmas, too, cannot be found through them. Since the three thousand dharmas cannot be found to arise horizontally through any of these four propositions, should they instead be said to arise from a single thought of mind ceasing? But the ceasing of mind cannot even give rise to a single dharma — how, then, could it give rise to three thousand dharmas? If the three thousand dharmas are said to arise from a mind both ceasing and not ceasing at once, then "both ceasing and not ceasing" contradicts itself in its own nature — like water and fire, neither of which can stand together with the other — so how could this give rise to the three thousand dharmas? If it is said that a mind neither ceasing nor not ceasing gives rise to the three thousand dharmas, then, being neither ceasing nor not ceasing, it is neither an agent nor an object of action at all — how, then, could it give rise, as agent, to the three thousand dharmas as object? Sought both vertically and horizontally, the three thousand dharmas cannot be found; sought as neither vertical nor horizontal, the three thousand dharmas likewise cannot be found. The path of words is cut off; the sphere of the mind's own activity is extinguished — hence this is named "the inconceivable object." The Nirvāṇa Sūtra says: "Birth-of-birth cannot be spoken; birth-of-non-birth cannot be spoken; non-birth-of-birth cannot be spoken; non-birth-of-non-birth cannot be spoken." This is exactly that same sense. One should know that within the ultimate truth, not even a single dharma can be found — how much less, then, three thousand dharmas? And yet within the conventional truth, a single mind is already complete with immeasurable dharmas — how much more, then, these three thousand?

Reading note: Zhiyi stages this as a debate between two real contemporary schools — the Dilun masters, who located ultimate generative power in the Dharma-nature itself, and the Shelun (Compendium of the Mahāyāna) tradition, who located it instead in the ālayavijñāna, the "storehouse consciousness" — and refuses to adjudicate between them, showing instead that each position, taken as a complete explanation, collapses into the other or contradicts its own sources. The dream simile is not mere illustration; it is doing the argument's real work, since a dream is a case everyone already grants exhibits exactly the puzzle at issue — vivid, structured experience that cannot be pinned to the sleeper, the sleep, their combination, or their absence — making the far stranger claim about mind and the three thousand realms concrete before it is generalized back into the inconceivable.

Speaking the Unspeakable: The Four Siddhāntas 一念三千 · 四悉檀

Chinese Source

如佛告德女：「無明內有不？」「不也。」「外有不？」「不也。」「內外有不？」「不也。」「非內非外有不？」「不也。」佛言：「如是有。」龍樹云：「不自、不他、不共、不無因生。」大經：「生生不可說，乃至不生不生不可說；有因緣故，亦可得說。」謂四悉檀因緣也。雖四句冥寂，慈悲憐愍，於無名相中，假名相說。

Ru-Yi Translation

As when the Buddha said to the woman Guṇavatī: "Does ignorance exist within?" "No." "Does it exist without?" "No." "Does it exist both within and without?" "No." "Does it exist neither within nor without?" "No." The Buddha said: "It exists exactly in this way." Nāgārjuna says: "It arises neither from itself, nor from another, nor from both together, nor without any cause at all." The Nirvāṇa Sūtra says: "Birth-of-birth cannot be spoken, and so on through non-birth-of-non-birth, which cannot be spoken either — yet, on account of causes and conditions, it may indeed be spoken of after all," meaning precisely the causal conditions of the four siddhāntas. Though the four propositions are, in themselves, profoundly quiescent, compassion and pity move one, within what has neither name nor mark, to speak provisionally by means of name and mark all the same.

或作世界，說心具一切法，聞者歡喜。如言：「三界無別法，唯是一心造。」即其文也。或說緣生一切法，聞者歡喜。如言：「五欲令人墮惡道；善知識者是大因緣，所謂化導令得見佛。」即其文也。或言因緣共生一切，法聞者歡喜。如言：「水銀和真金，能塗諸色像。」即其文也。或言離生一切法，聞者歡喜。如言：「十二因緣非佛作，非天人、修羅作，其性自爾。」即其文也。此四句即世界悉檀，說心生三千一切法也。

Sometimes, by way of the worldly siddhānta, one teaches that mind is complete with every dharma, and the listener rejoices — as when it is said, "There is no separate dharma within the three realms; it is the single mind alone that creates them," this being exactly such a text. Sometimes one teaches that conditions give rise to every dharma, and the listener rejoices — as when it is said, "The five desires cause a person to fall into the evil destinies; a good spiritual friend is a great causal condition, meaning precisely being guided and transformed so as to come to see the Buddha," this being exactly such a text. Sometimes one teaches that cause and condition together give rise to every dharma, and the listener rejoices — as when it is said, "Mercury mixed with true gold is able to coat every kind of image," this being exactly such a text. Sometimes one teaches that every dharma arises apart from both cause and condition, and the listener rejoices — as when it is said, "The twelve links of dependent origination are made neither by the Buddha, nor by heavenly beings, humans, or asuras — their nature is simply so of itself," this being exactly such a text. These four propositions are precisely the worldly siddhānta, teaching that mind gives rise to the three thousand, every dharma.

云何為人悉檀？如言：「佛法如海，唯信能入。」「信則道源功德母，一切善法由之生。」「汝但發三菩提心，是則出家，禁戒具足。」聞者生信，即其文也。或說緣生一切法，如言：「若不值佛，當於無量劫墮地獄苦；以見佛故，得無根信，如從伊蘭出生栴檀。」聞者生信。或說合生一切法，如言：「心水澄清，珠相自現；慈善根力，見如此事。」聞者生信，即其文也。或說離生一切法，如言：「非內觀得是智慧，乃至非內外觀得是智慧。若有住著，先尼梵志小信尚不可得，況捨邪入正？」聞者生信，即其文也。是為為人悉檀四句，說心生三千一切法也。

What, then, is the siddhānta for the individual? As when it is said, "The Buddhadharma is like the ocean — only faith is able to enter it." "Faith is the very source of the path, the mother of merit — every wholesome dharma is born from it." "Simply give rise to the mind of the threefold bodhi, and this alone is leaving home, complete with every restraining precept" — hearing this, the listener gives rise to faith, this being exactly such a text. Sometimes one teaches that conditions give rise to every dharma, as when it is said, "Should one fail to encounter a buddha, one would fall, for immeasurable kalpas, into the suffering of hell; but on account of beholding the Buddha, one attains a faith rooted in nothing else at all, like sandalwood growing out of a poisonous eranda shrub" — hearing this, the listener gives rise to faith. Sometimes one teaches that the combination of both gives rise to every dharma, as when it is said, "Once the water of the mind grows clear and still, the mark of the pearl appears of itself; by the power of compassion's own wholesome root, a matter of this kind is seen" — hearing this, the listener gives rise to faith, this being exactly such a text. Sometimes one teaches that every dharma arises apart from both, as when it is said, "Not through inward contemplation is this wisdom attained, nor even through contemplation both inward and outward; should there be any attachment or dwelling at all, then not even the brahmacārin Śreṇika's own small measure of faith could be attained — how much less, then, leaving the deviant to enter the correct?" — hearing this, the listener gives rise to faith, this being exactly such a text. These are the four propositions of the siddhānta for the individual, teaching that mind gives rise to the three thousand, every dharma.

云何對治悉檀？說心治一切惡，如言：「得一心者，萬邪滅矣。」即其文也。或說緣治一切惡，如說：「得聞無上大慧明，心定如地不可動。」即其文也。或說因緣和合治一切惡，如言：「一分從思生，一分從師得。」即其文也。或說離治一切惡，「我坐道場時，不得一切法；空拳誑小兒，誘度於一切。」即其文也。是為對治悉檀，心破一切惡。云何第一義悉檀？心得見理，如言：「心開意解，豁然得道。」或說緣能見理，如言：「須臾聞之，即得究竟三菩提。」或說因緣和合得道，如：「快馬見鞭影即得正路」。或說離能見理，如言：「無所得即是得已，是得無所得。」是名第一義四句見理，何況心生三千法耶？佛旨盡淨，不在因、緣、共、離，即世諦是第一義也。

What, then, is the siddhānta of the antidote? Teaching that mind cures every evil, as when it is said, "One who attains a single, unified mind — every deviance is thereby extinguished," this being exactly such a text. Sometimes one teaches that conditions cure every evil, as when it is said, "Having attained the hearing of the unsurpassed great wisdom's own brightness, the mind grows fixed as the earth itself, unshakable," this being exactly such a text. Sometimes one teaches that the union of cause and condition cures every evil, as when it is said, "One portion arises from one's own reflection; one portion is obtained from one's own teacher," this being exactly such a text. Sometimes one teaches that curing arises apart from both, as when it is said, "When I sat at the very seat of enlightenment, I obtained not a single dharma at all; an empty fist deceives a small child, and by this very means, every being is drawn across," this being exactly such a text. This, then, is the siddhānta of the antidote, mind breaking through every evil. What, then, is the siddhānta of ultimate meaning? Mind attaining the direct seeing of principle, as when it is said, "The mind opens, understanding is resolved, and one attains the path all at once." Sometimes one teaches that conditions are able to see principle, as when it is said, "Hearing it for but a moment, one at once attains ultimate, complete bodhi." Sometimes one teaches that the union of cause and condition attains the path, as with, "A swift horse, seeing merely the shadow of the whip, arrives at once on the correct road." Sometimes one teaches that seeing principle arises apart from both, as when it is said, "Attaining nothing at all is itself already attainment — this is the attainment of attaining nothing." This is named the four propositions of ultimate meaning, seeing principle directly — how much more, then, could mind give rise to the three thousand dharmas by this same means? The Buddha's own intent, entirely pure and free of residue, does not lie fixed in cause, condition, both, or neither — the conventional truth is itself already the ultimate truth.

又四句俱皆可說，說因亦是，緣亦是，共亦是，離亦是。若為盲人說乳，若貝、若粖、若雪、若鶴，盲聞諸說，即得解乳，即世諦是第一義諦。當知終日說，終日不說；終日不說，終日說。終日雙遮，終日雙照；即破即立，即立即破。經論皆爾。天親、龍樹內鑒冷然，外適時宜，各權所據。而人師偏解，學者苟執，遂興矢石，各保一邊，大乖聖道也。若得此意，俱不可說、俱可說。若隨便宜者，應言無明法法性生一切法，如眠法法心則有一切夢事。心與緣合，則三種世間，三千相性，皆從心起。一性雖少而不無；無明雖多而不有。何者？指一為多，多非多；指多為一，一非少。故名此心為不思議境也。

And, further, all four propositions may equally be spoken: speaking of cause is correct, speaking of condition is correct, speaking of both together is correct, speaking of neither is correct. It is like explaining milk to a person born blind — whether one says it is like a conch shell, like rice flour, like snow, or like a crane, the blind person, hearing any of these various explanations, thereby comes to understand milk all the same — and just so, the conventional truth is itself already the ultimate truth. One should know that speaking the entire day is, at the same time, not speaking at all the entire day; and not speaking the entire day is, at the same time, speaking the entire day. Obstructing both extremes the entire day, and illuminating both the entire day; breaking through even as one establishes, establishing even as one breaks through — the sūtras and treatises are, every one, exactly this way. Vasubandhu and Nāgārjuna, inwardly perceiving with perfect clarity, outwardly adapted to the occasion, each provisionally taking their own particular stand — and yet later masters, understanding only one side, and students clinging obstinately to it, have gone on to raise arrows and stones against one another, each defending only their own single position, gravely at odds with the sagely path. Should one grasp this sense, then all four propositions alike cannot be spoken, and all four alike can be spoken. Should one speak according to whatever is fitting, one should say: ignorance, dharma by dharma, and the Dharma-nature give rise to every dharma together — just as the dharma of sleep, dharma by dharma, joined with the mind, gives rise to every event within a dream. Where mind and condition come together, the three kinds of world, the three thousand marks and natures, all arise from the mind. A single nature, though slight, is not therefore nonexistent; ignorance, though manifold, is not therefore truly existent. Why is this? Point to the one as many, and the many are not truly many; point to the many as one, and the one is not thereby diminished. Hence this mind is named the inconceivable object.

Reading note: The four siddhāntas — introduced already in fascicle 1 as Nāgārjuna's own framework for why the Buddha teaches differently depending on audience — here resolve the apparent contradiction between the previous section's exhaustive refutation (mind cannot be found to generate the three thousand through any of four propositions) and scripture's own repeated, confident assertions that it does. Both are true at different levels: the ultimate truth refuses every formulation, while the conventional truth positively requires all four, each one correct for a different listener — "explaining milk to a person born blind," where "conch shell," "rice flour," "snow," and "crane" are all equally partial and equally sufficient to convey real understanding. The rebuke of "later masters" who "raise arrows and stones against one another, each defending only their own single position" reads as pointed commentary on the very Dilun/Shelun dispute just staged two sections earlier — the treatise's own answer to that scholastic quarrel is not to pick a winner but to show both were only ever half right.

One Mind, Every Mind: Closing Mode One 一心一切心

Chinese Source

若解一心一切心，一切心一心，非一非一切；一陰一切陰，一切陰一陰，非一非一切；一入一切入，一切入一入，非一非一切；一界一切界，一切界一界，非一非一切；一眾生一切眾生，一切眾生一眾生，非一非一切；一國土一切國土，一切國土一國土，非一非一切；一相一切相，一切相一相，非一非一切；乃至一究竟一切究竟，一切究竟一究竟，非一非一切。遍歷一切，皆是不可思議境。若法性、無明合，有一切法陰界入等，即是俗諦；一切界入是一法界，即是真諦；非一非一切，即是中道第一義諦。如是遍歷一切法，無非不思議三諦云云。若一法一切法，即是因緣所生法，是為假名，假觀也；若一切法即一法，我說即是空，空觀也；若非一非一切者，即是中道觀。一空一切空，無假中而不空，總空觀也。一假一切假，無空中而不假，總假觀也。一中一切中，無空假而不中，總中觀也。即《中論》所說不可思議一心三觀。歷一切法亦如是。若因緣所生一切法者，即方便隨情，道種權智；若一切法一法，我說即是空，即隨智，一切智；若非一非一切，亦名中道義者，即非權非實，一切種智。例上，一權一切權，一實一切實，一切非權非實，遍歷一切，是不思議三智也。若隨情，即隨他意語；若隨智，即隨自意語；若非權非實，即非自非他意語；遍歷一切法無非漸、頓、不定不思議教門也。若解頓，即解心。心尚不可得，云何當有趣、非趣？若解漸，即解一切法趣心。若解不定，即解是趣不過。此等名異義同，軌則行人呼為三法，所照為三諦，所發為三觀，觀成為三智，教他呼為三語，歸宗呼為三趣。得斯意類，一切皆成法門。種種味，勿嫌煩云云。

Ru-Yi Translation

Should one understand that a single mind is every mind, and every mind is a single mind, neither one nor every one at all; that a single aggregate is every aggregate, and every aggregate a single aggregate, neither one nor every one; that a single entry is every entry, and every entry a single entry, neither one nor every one; that a single element is every element, and every element a single element, neither one nor every one; that a single sentient being is every sentient being, and every sentient being a single sentient being, neither one nor every one; that a single land is every land, and every land a single land, neither one nor every one; that a single mark is every mark, and every mark a single mark, neither one nor every one; and so on, up through a single ultimate consistency being every ultimate consistency, and every ultimate consistency a single ultimate consistency, neither one nor every one — passing through every one of these in turn, all of them alike are the inconceivable object. Should the Dharma-nature and ignorance combine, giving rise to every dharma, the aggregates, entries, elements, and the rest, this is the conventional truth; that every element and entry is itself a single dharma-realm is the real truth; and that they are neither one nor every one at all is the ultimate truth of the middle way. Passing through every dharma in this way, there is nothing that is not the inconceivable threefold truth. If a single dharma is every dharma, this is precisely the dharma arisen through causes and conditions, and this is the provisional name, the contemplation of the provisional; if every dharma is itself a single dharma, "I declare this to be emptiness," the contemplation of emptiness; if it is neither one nor every one, this is the contemplation of the middle way. A single emptiness is every emptiness, with nothing in the provisional or the middle that is not itself empty — the total contemplation of emptiness. A single provisional is every provisional, with nothing in emptiness or the middle that is not itself provisional — the total contemplation of the provisional. A single middle is every middle, with nothing in emptiness or the provisional that is not itself the middle — the total contemplation of the middle. This is precisely the inconceivable single mind's threefold contemplation spoken of in the Middle Treatise. Passing through every dharma is exactly the same. If every dharma arisen through causes and conditions is spoken of, this is the expedient means that follows sentiment, the provisional wisdom of the paths; if every dharma is itself a single dharma, "I declare this to be emptiness," this is what follows wisdom, the wisdom of all things; if it is neither one nor every one, also named the sense of the middle way, this is neither provisional nor real, the wisdom of all modes. By the same pattern as above: a single provisional teaching is every provisional teaching, a single real teaching is every real teaching, and the whole is neither provisional nor real — passing through every one of these in turn is the inconceivable threefold wisdom. If following sentiment, this is speech that follows another's own intent; if following wisdom, this is speech that follows one's own intent; if neither provisional nor real, this is speech neither one's own nor another's — passing through every dharma in this way, there is nothing that is not the inconceivable gate of teaching, gradual, sudden, and indeterminate. Should one understand the sudden, one understands the mind directly; and since the mind itself cannot even be found, how, then, could there be any convergence, or non-convergence, to speak of at all? Should one understand the gradual, one understands that every dharma converges upon the mind. Should one understand the indeterminate, one understands that this very convergence goes no further. These are different in name but the same in sense: as the very standard governing the practitioner, they are called the three dharmas; as what is illuminated, they are called the three truths; as what is given rise to, they are called the three contemplations; once the contemplation is accomplished, they are called the three wisdoms; as taught to others, they are called the three modes of speech; as the very source to which all things return, they are called the three convergences. Grasping this sense and its kind, everything without exception becomes a gateway of the Dharma. Do not resent the manifold flavors of this, however troublesome they may seem.

如如意珠，天上勝寶，狀如芥粟，有大功能；淨妙五欲，七寶琳琅；非內畜，非外入；不謀前後，不擇多少，不作麁妙，稱意豐儉；降雨穰穰，不添不盡。蓋是色法尚能如此，況心神靈妙，寧不具一切法耶？又三毒惑心，一念心起，尚復身、邊、利、鈍，八十八使乃至八萬四千煩惱。若言先有，那忽待緣？若言本無，緣對即應。不有不無，定有即邪，定無即妄。當知有而不有，不有而有。惑心尚爾，況不思議一心耶？又如眠夢見百千萬事，豁寤無一，況復百千？未眠，不夢不覺，不多不一；眠力故謂多，覺力故謂少。莊周夢為蝴蝶，翾翔百年；寤知非蝶，亦非積歲。無明法法性，一心一切心，如彼昏眠。達無明即法性，一切心一心，如彼醒寤云云。又行安樂行人，一眠夢初發心乃至作佛坐道場、轉法輪、度眾生、入涅槃；豁寤秖是一夢事。若信三喻，則信一心，非口所宣，非情所測。此不思議境，何法不收？此境發智，何智不發？依此境發誓乃至無法愛，何誓不具？何行不滿足耶？說時如上次第；行時一心中具一切心云云。

It is like the wish-fulfilling jewel, the supreme treasure of the heavens, its own shape no larger than a mustard seed, and yet possessed of vast power: pure and sublime, the five desires, the seven treasures shining bright — neither stored within, nor entering from without; taking no thought of before or after, choosing neither much nor little, producing neither the coarse nor the fine, exactly according with whatever is wished, whether abundant or spare; raining down its bounty in torrents, never added to, never exhausted. If even a mere material thing is already able to accomplish this much, how much more the mind's own numinous, sublime spirit — how could it possibly fail to be complete with every dharma? Consider, further, the mind confused by the three poisons: the very instant a single thought arises, it already gives rise to the views of a substantial self and of extremes, to sharp and dull afflictions alike, the eighty-eight proclivities, and on up to the eighty-four thousand afflictions in full. Should one say these existed beforehand, how could they possibly still depend on conditions? Should one say they were originally nonexistent, then, once conditions meet, they ought to correspond exactly. Neither existent nor nonexistent — to fix upon existence is itself already deviance; to fix upon nonexistence is itself already falsehood. One should know that they exist and yet do not exist, do not exist and yet exist. If even the deluded mind is already thus, how much more the inconceivable single mind itself? Consider, further, one who, asleep, dreams of a hundred thousand myriad events — waking suddenly, not a single one remains; how much less, then, a hundred thousand of them at all? Before falling asleep, there is neither dreaming nor waking, neither many nor one; it is the power of sleep that makes them seem many, and the power of waking that makes them seem few. Zhuangzi dreamed he was a butterfly, fluttering freely for what seemed a hundred years; waking, he knew he was no butterfly, nor had a hundred years actually passed. Ignorance, dharma by dharma, and the Dharma-nature — a single mind being every mind — is like that very darkness of sleep; realizing that ignorance is itself already the Dharma-nature, every mind being a single mind, is like that very moment of waking. Consider, further, the practitioner of the samādhi of ease and bliss: in a single sleep, dreaming of first giving rise to the resolve, and on through becoming a buddha, sitting at the very seat of enlightenment, turning the wheel of the Dharma, delivering sentient beings, and entering nirvāṇa — waking suddenly, this is simply the content of a single dream. Should one believe these three similes, one thereby believes in the single mind — something neither spoken by the mouth nor measured by ordinary feeling. This inconceivable object — what dharma does it fail to gather in? This object gives rise to wisdom — what wisdom does it fail to give rise to? Relying on this object to give rise to the vow, and on through freedom from attachment even to the Dharma itself — what vow does it fail to complete? What practice does it fail to fulfill? Spoken of, it follows the sequence given above; practiced, a single mind is complete with every mind at once.

Reading note: Mode One closes by folding its own governing vocabulary — the three truths, the three contemplations, the three wisdoms, the three modes of speech, the three convergences — into a single reciprocal formula repeated across every category the fascicle has built: aggregate, entry, element, sentient being, land, mark, and ultimate consistency. That Zhuangzi's own butterfly dream appears here, alongside the wish-fulfilling jewel and the Lotus Sūtra's own parable of a single night's dream containing an entire path to buddhahood, is a rare moment of the treatise reaching outside the Buddhist canon for its imagery — waking from the butterfly dream to find "he was no butterfly, nor had a hundred years actually passed" catches, better than any technical vocabulary could, exactly what it means to realize that "ignorance is itself already the Dharma-nature." This closes the treatise's single most demanding passage; the ten modes of contemplation now turn, for the first time, from the object of contemplation to the contemplating mind's own posture toward it.

Mode Two: Giving Rise to the True Bodhi-Mind 發真正菩提心

Chinese Source

二、發真正菩提心者，既深識不思議境，知一苦一切苦。自悲昔苦，起惑耽湎麁弊色聲，縱身口意作不善業。輪環惡趣縈諸熱惱，身苦心苦而自毀傷。而今還以愛繭自纏，癡燈所害，百千萬劫一何痛哉！設使欲捨三途，欣五戒十善，相心修福。如市易博換，翻更益罪，似魚入笱口、蛾赴燈中。狂計邪黠，逾迷逾遠，渴更飲鹹。龍須縛身，入水轉痛；牛皮繫體，向日彌堅。盲入棘林，溺墮洄澓。把刃把炬，痛那可言？虎尾蛇頭，悚焉悼慄；自惟若此，悲他亦然。假令隘路叛出怨國，備歷辛苦絕而復穌；往至貧里傭賃一日，止宿草庵不肯前進，樂為鄙事。不信不識，可悲可怪！思惟彼我，鯁痛自他。即起大悲興兩誓願：「眾生無邊誓願度，煩惱無數誓願斷。」眾生雖如虛空，誓度如空之眾生；雖知煩惱無所有，誓斷無所有之煩惱。雖知眾生數甚多，而度甚多之眾生；雖知煩惱無邊底，而斷無底之煩惱。雖知眾生如如佛如，而度如佛如之眾生；雖知煩惱如實相，而斷如實相之煩惱。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, giving rise to the true and correct bodhi-mind: having already deeply recognized the inconceivable object, one knows that a single suffering is every suffering. Grieving over one's own past suffering — giving rise to delusion, indulging in coarse, corrupting form and sound, letting body, speech, and mind run unchecked in unwholesome karma; turning through the wheel of the evil destinies, bound up in every burning affliction, suffering in body and suffering in mind, doing injury to oneself — and now, still wrapped in the very cocoon of one's own craving, harmed by the very lamp of one's own delusion, across a hundred thousand myriad kalpas — how great a pain is this! Suppose one wishes to cast aside the three evil destinies, and instead delights in the five precepts and the ten wholesome actions, cultivating merit with a mind still fixed on some mark — this is like trading in the marketplace, where one only adds to one's own offense in the exchange, like a fish swimming into the mouth of a trap, or a moth flying into a flame. Deranged calculation, deviant cunning — the further one goes, the further astray one strays, like drinking brine to quench one's thirst, only growing thirstier still. A dragon's whiskers binding the body only grow more painful once submerged in water; an ox-hide wrapped around the body only grows tighter still once turned toward the sun. Blind, one wanders into a thicket of thorns; drowning, one is drawn down into the whirling current. Grasping a blade, grasping a torch — how could the pain of it ever be told? A tiger's tail, a serpent's head — one trembles and shudders in terror at either. Reflecting that this is exactly my own condition, one grieves that it is exactly the condition of others as well. It is like fleeing along a narrow path out of an enemy land, enduring every hardship, on the very point of death and yet reviving — only to arrive at some poor village, hire oneself out for a single day's wage, lodge in a thatched hut, and refuse to go any further, content instead with so base an existence. Neither believing nor recognizing the truth — how pitiable, how strange this is! Reflecting on both others and oneself, one feels this pain lodged deep, for both self and other alike. And so one gives rise to great compassion, raising up the first two of the four universal vows: "Sentient beings are numberless; I vow to deliver them all. Afflictions are inexhaustible; I vow to extinguish them all." Though sentient beings are like empty space, one vows to deliver sentient beings as empty as space itself; though one knows that affliction has, in itself, nothing whatsoever, one vows to extinguish an affliction that has nothing whatsoever. Though one knows the number of sentient beings is exceedingly vast, one still delivers this exceedingly vast number of sentient beings; though one knows affliction has no bottom or boundary, one still extinguishes an affliction with no bottom or boundary. Though one knows sentient beings are, in their very suchness, the very suchness of the Buddha himself, one still delivers sentient beings who are the suchness of the Buddha; though one knows affliction is itself already the true mark of reality, one still extinguishes an affliction that is itself the true mark of reality.

何者？若但拔苦因，不拔苦果，此誓雜毒，故須觀空。若偏觀空，則不見眾生可度，是名著空者，諸佛所不化。若遍見眾生可度，即墮愛見大悲，非解脫道云云。今則非毒、非偽，故名為「真」；非空邊、非有邊，故名為「正」。如鳥飛空，終不住空；雖不住空，跡不可尋。雖空而度，雖度而空，是故名「誓與虛空共鬪」，故名「真正發菩提心」，即此意也。又識不可思議心，一樂心一切樂心。我及眾生昔雖求樂，不知樂因，如執瓦礫謂如意珠，妄指螢光呼為日月。今方始解，故起大慈，興兩誓願，謂：「法門無量誓願知，無上佛道誓願成」。雖知法門永寂如空，誓願修行永寂；雖知菩提無所有，無所有中，吾故求之。雖知法門如空無所有，誓願畫繢莊嚴虛空；雖知佛道非成所成，如虛空中種樹，使得華得果。雖知法門及佛果，非修非不修而修；非證非得，以無所證得而證而得。是名非偽、非毒，名為「真」；非空、非見愛名為「正」。如此慈悲誓願與不可思議境智，非前非後同時俱起。慈悲即智慧，智慧即慈悲。無緣無念，普覆一切；任運拔苦，自然與樂。不同毒害，不同但空，不同愛見。是名「真正發心菩提」義。自悲己、悲眾生義，皆如上說。觀心可解。

Why is this? Should one pull out only the cause of suffering, without pulling out its fruit as well, this vow is itself mixed with poison, and so the contemplation of emptiness is needed. Yet should one contemplate emptiness one-sidedly, one then fails to see any sentient being left to deliver at all — this is named one attached to emptiness, whom the buddhas cannot transform. Should one, on the other hand, see sentient beings everywhere as objects to be delivered without qualification, one falls into a great compassion still bound by craving and view, which is not the path of liberation at all. Here, then, the vow is neither poison nor falsehood, and is hence named "true"; neither the extreme of emptiness nor the extreme of existence, and is hence named "correct." It is like a bird flying through the sky, never actually alighting anywhere within it; and though it never alights, its own path leaves no trace to be followed either. Though empty, one still delivers; though delivering, one is still empty — and this is exactly why it is named "vowing to do battle with empty space itself," and exactly why it is named "giving rise to the true and correct bodhi-mind." This is precisely that same sense. Further, recognizing the inconceivable mind, a single bliss is every bliss. I myself, and every sentient being, though we formerly sought after bliss, did not know its true cause — like grasping a shard of rubble and calling it a wish-fulfilling jewel, or falsely pointing to a firefly's glow and calling it the sun or the moon. Only now does true understanding begin, and so one gives rise to great loving-kindness, raising up the remaining two of the four universal vows: "The Dharma-gates are boundless; I vow to master them all. The unsurpassed buddha-path; I vow to accomplish it." Though one knows the Dharma-gates are, forever, as quiescent as empty space, one vows to cultivate that very quiescence in practice; though one knows bodhi itself has nothing whatsoever, it is precisely within that very nothingness that one still seeks it. Though one knows the Dharma-gates are, like empty space, without anything at all, one vows to paint and adorn that very empty space; though one knows the buddha-path is not something accomplished by any accomplishing at all, it is like planting a tree within empty space itself, causing it to bear flowers and to bear fruit. Though one knows that neither the Dharma-gates nor the fruit of buddhahood are cultivated, and yet are not uncultivated either, one cultivates them all the same; neither realized nor attained, and yet, with nothing whatsoever realized or attained, one still realizes and attains them. This is named neither falsehood nor poison, and hence named "true"; neither emptiness, nor craving, nor view, and hence named "correct." Compassion, this very vow, and the wisdom of the inconceivable object arise together at once, neither before nor after the other. Compassion is itself wisdom; wisdom is itself compassion. Without any object, without any conceptual thought, it covers over everything universally; effortlessly it pulls out suffering, spontaneously it bestows bliss. It is unlike the poison of hatred; unlike mere emptiness alone; unlike compassion still bound by craving and view. This is the sense of "giving rise to the true and correct bodhi-mind." Grieving for oneself, and grieving for sentient beings — the sense of both is exactly as explained above. This may be understood through contemplating the mind.

Reading note: The image of the refugee who flees "an enemy land" through every hardship only to settle for a single day's wage in "some poor village," refusing to go any further, gives concrete shape to a pattern the treatise has named before under the Lotus Sūtra's own image of the phantom city: real suffering escaped, but the escape stopped short, mistaking a resting-place for the destination. The "vowing to do battle with empty space itself" formula captures the same tension already worked through in fascicle 2's treatment of "craving is itself the path": a vow to save beings who are ultimately empty must be genuinely double, holding compassion and emptiness together rather than letting either cancel the other — sentimental attachment on one side, sterile detachment on the other.

Mode Three: Skillfully Settling the Mind 善巧安心

Chinese Source

三、善巧安心者，善以止觀安於法性也。上深達不思議境，淵奧微密。博運慈悲，亘蓋若此。須行填願，行即止觀也。無明癡惑本是法性，以癡迷故，法性變作無明，起諸顛倒，善不善等。如寒來結水，變作堅水。又如眠來變心，有種種夢。今當體諸顛倒即是法性，不一不異。雖顛倒起滅，如旋火輪，不信顛倒起滅，唯信此心但是法性。起是法性起，滅是法性滅，體其實不起滅，妄謂起滅；秖指妄想悉是法性，以法性繫法性，以法性念法性，常是法性，無不法性時。體達既成，不得妄想亦不得法性。還源反本，法界俱寂，是名為「止」。如此止時，上來一切流轉皆止。觀者，觀察無明之心，上等於法性，本來皆空；下等一切妄想善惡，皆如虛空，無二無別。譬如劫盡，從地上至初禪，炎炎無非是火。又如虛空藏菩薩所現之相，一切皆空。如海慧初來，所現一切皆水。介爾念起，所念、念者無不即空；空亦不可得。如前火木能使薪然，亦復自然。法界洞朗，咸皆大明，名之為「觀」。止秖是智；智秖是止。不動止秖是不動智；不動智秖是不動止。不動智照於法性，即是觀智得安，亦是止安；不動於法性相應，即是止安，亦是觀安，無二無別。若俱不得安，當復云何？夫心神冥昧，椶利怳𭝏，汩起汩滅，難可執持；倏去倏來，不易關禁。雖復止之，馳疾颺炎；雖復觀之，闇逾漆墨。加功苦至，散惑倍隆。敵強力弱，鷸蚌相扼；既不得進，又不可退。當殉命奉道，薦以肌骨；誓巧安心，方便迴轉，令得相應成觀行位也。

Ru-Yi Translation

Third, skillfully settling the mind: skillfully using calming-and-contemplation to settle the mind within the Dharma-nature itself. Above, one has deeply fathomed the inconceivable object, so profound and subtle; and broadly carried compassion, so vast and all-covering. Now practice must be undertaken to fulfill that vow, and this very practice is calming-and-contemplation itself. Ignorance and deluded confusion are, at root, themselves already the Dharma-nature; on account of confusion and delusion, the Dharma-nature is transformed into ignorance, giving rise to every kind of inversion, wholesome and unwholesome alike — just as cold weather causes water to freeze, becoming solid ice; or just as sleep transforms the mind, giving rise to every kind of dream. Here, one should directly embody every inversion as itself already the Dharma-nature, neither one nor different from it. Though inversion arises and ceases like a whirling firebrand's circle of flame, one does not believe in this arising and ceasing of inversion, but believes only that this mind is, in every case, simply the Dharma-nature itself. Its arising is the Dharma-nature's own arising; its ceasing is the Dharma-nature's own ceasing; embodying it directly, there is, in reality, no arising or ceasing at all — one has only falsely supposed there to be arising and ceasing. Simply pointing directly at deluded thought as, in every case, already the Dharma-nature, binding the Dharma-nature to the Dharma-nature, being mindful of the Dharma-nature by means of the Dharma-nature itself — it is, constantly, the Dharma-nature, with never a moment that is not the Dharma-nature. Once this direct embodiment is accomplished, one no longer grasps at deluded thought, nor even grasps at the Dharma-nature either. Returning to the very source, turning back to the root, the dharma-realm, entire, is quiescent — this is named "calming." When calming of this kind is achieved, every flow and turning described above is thereby stilled. As for contemplation: contemplating the very mind of ignorance, above, it is equal to the Dharma-nature, originally, entirely empty; below, it is equal to every deluded thought, wholesome and unwholesome alike, all of it like empty space, with no two, no separation at all. It is like the ending of a kalpa, when, from the earth's own surface up to the first dhyāna heaven, blazing flame is nothing but fire throughout; or like the marks manifested by the bodhisattva Ākāśagarbha, every one of them, entirely, empty; or like when Sāgaramati first arrived, and everything manifested was, entirely, water. The very instant a thought stirs, both what is thought and the one who thinks it are, without exception, already empty — and this very emptiness, too, cannot be found. Just as fire and wood, spoken of before, can set kindling ablaze, so too does this happen of itself, effortlessly. The dharma-realm, penetrated through and radiant, is, altogether, brought to great clarity — this is named "contemplation." Calming is itself already wisdom; wisdom is itself already calming. The unmoving calming is itself already the unmoving wisdom; the unmoving wisdom is itself already the unmoving calming. The unmoving wisdom illuminating the Dharma-nature is itself the contemplative wisdom finding its own settling, and is likewise the calming finding its settling; being unmoved, in accord with the Dharma-nature, is itself the calming finding its settling, and is likewise the contemplation finding its settling — with no two, no separation at all. Should neither be settled even so, what, then, should be done? The spirit of the mind, dim and obscure, coarse and slippery, hazy and confused, arising and ceasing in a rush, difficult to hold and grasp; coming and going in an instant, not easily restrained or confined. Though one calms it, it still races off swiftly, flaring up like flame; though one contemplates it, the darkness only grows thicker than lacquer or ink. Applying effort with bitter diligence, the scattering delusion only intensifies twice over. The enemy strong, one's own strength weak, like the sandpiper and the clam locked together, gripping one another — one can neither advance nor retreat. At such a time, one should offer up one's very life in service of the path, presenting even flesh and bone; vowing to skillfully settle the mind, turning it about through expedient means, so that it may come into accord and reach the stage of contemplative practice.

安心為兩：一、教他，二、自行。教他又為兩：一、聖師，二、凡師。聖師有慧眼力，明於法藥；有法眼力，識於病障；有化道力，應病授藥，令得服行。如毱多知弟子應以信悟，令上樹；應以食悟，令服乳酪；應以呵責悟，化為女像。一一開曉無有毫差，不待時，不過時，言發即悟。佛去世後，如是之師甚為難得。盲龜何由上值浮孔？墜芥豈得下貫針鋒？難！難！二者、凡師，雖無三力，亦得施化。譬如良醫精別藥、病，解色、解聲、解脈，逗藥即差；有命盡者，亦不能起死。若不解脈，醫問病相，依語作方，亦挑脫得差。身子聖德亦復差機，凡夫具縛稱病導師。今不論聖師，正說凡師，教他安心也。他有二種：一、信行，二、法行。薩婆多明此二人位在見道。因聞入者，是為「信行」；因思入者，是為「法行」。曇無德云：「位在方便，自見法少，憑聞力多，後時要須聞法得悟，名為『信行』。憑聞力少，自見法多，後時要須思惟得悟，名為『法行』。」若見道中無相心利，一發即真，那得判信、法之別？然數據行成，論據根性，各有所以，不得相非。今師遠討源由，久劫聽學，久劫坐禪，得為信法種子；世世熏習則成根性，各於聞思開悟耳。若論根利鈍者，法行利，內自觀法故；信行鈍，藉他聞故。又信行利，一聞即悟故；法行鈍，歷法觀察故。或俱利俱鈍：信行人聞慧利，修慧鈍；法行人修慧利，聞慧鈍。己說前人根性利鈍竟，云何安心？師應問言：「汝於定慧，為志何等？」其人若言：「我聞佛說：『善知識者，如月形光，漸漸圓著；又如梯隥，漸漸增高。巧說轉人心，得道全因緣；志欣渴飲，如犢逐母。』」當知是則信行人也。若言：「我聞佛說：『明鏡體若不動，色像分明；淨水無波，魚石自現。欣捨惡覺，如棄重檐。』」當知是則法行人也。

Settling the mind divides into two: first, instructing others; second, one's own self-practice. Instructing others, in turn, divides into two: first, the sagely teacher; second, the ordinary teacher. The sagely teacher possesses the power of the wisdom-eye, clear about the Dharma-medicine; possesses the power of the Dharma-eye, discerning the sickness and its obstruction; and possesses the power of transformative guidance, matching the medicine exactly to the sickness, so that it is taken and put into practice. As when Upagupta, knowing that a certain disciple would awaken through faith, had him climb a tree; knowing another would awaken through food, had him partake of milk-curds; and knowing another would awaken through reproach, transformed himself into the image of a woman. Each and every one is opened to understanding without the slightest deviation, neither too soon nor too late — the very moment the word is spoken, awakening follows. Since the Buddha passed from the world, a teacher of this kind has become exceedingly difficult to find. How could a blind turtle ever chance to surface through a floating log's own single hole? How could a falling mustard seed ever happen to thread the very point of a needle below? Difficult indeed! Difficult indeed! Second, the ordinary teacher: though lacking these three powers, is still able to bestow instruction. It is like a skilled physician, precisely distinguishing medicine from sickness, understanding complexion, understanding voice, understanding the pulse — matching the medicine exactly, the patient recovers; though one whose life is truly at its end, even such a physician cannot raise from death. If the physician does not understand the pulse, but asks the patient about the symptoms and prescribes according to what is said, this too may, by chance, still bring about recovery. Even Śāriputra's own sagely virtue once misjudged a student's capacity — an ordinary being, still fully bound, may nonetheless be called a guide even while themselves still sick. Here, the sagely teacher is not what is discussed; what is properly discussed is the ordinary teacher, instructing others in settling the mind. Others are of two kinds: first, the practitioner of faith; second, the practitioner of dharma. The Sarvāstivādins explain that these two kinds of person are at the stage of seeing the path. One who enters through hearing is named "the practitioner of faith"; one who enters through reflection is named "the practitioner of dharma." The Dharmaguptakas say: "At the expedient stage, one who sees the dharma little of oneself, relying mostly on the power of hearing, and who, at a later time, must still hear the Dharma in order to awaken, is named 'the practitioner of faith.' One who relies little on the power of hearing, seeing the dharma mostly of oneself, and who, at a later time, must still reflect in order to awaken, is named 'the practitioner of dharma.'" But if, at the very stage of seeing the path, the markless mind is already sharp, arising all at once into the real — how, then, could one still distinguish between faith and dharma at all? Yet the Abhidharma masters base their account on how practice is accomplished, while the treatise-masters base theirs on innate capacity — each has its own reason, and neither should be dismissed as wrong. Here, tracing the matter back to its true source: through kalpas of listening and study, and kalpas of sitting in meditation, one comes to possess the seed of faith or of dharma; life after life, this habituation forms one's own capacity, and each accordingly awakens through hearing or through reflection. As for sharpness or dullness of capacity: the practitioner of dharma is sharp, since they observe the dharma inwardly, by themselves; the practitioner of faith is dull, since they depend on hearing from another. And yet the practitioner of faith is sharp in another sense, since a single hearing brings awakening at once; while the practitioner of dharma is dull in that same sense, since they must pass through the dharma by careful observation. Or both may be, in different respects, both sharp and dull at once: the practitioner of faith has sharp hearing-wisdom but dull cultivation-wisdom; the practitioner of dharma has sharp cultivation-wisdom but dull hearing-wisdom. Having now finished discussing the sharpness or dullness of a given person's own capacity, how, then, is the mind to be settled? The teacher should ask: "Toward concentration and wisdom, what is it you truly aspire to?" Should the person answer, "I have heard the Buddha say: 'A good spiritual friend is like the moon's own form and light, gradually growing full; or like a set of steps and rungs, gradually rising higher. Skillful speech turns a person's own mind, and attaining the path depends entirely on this causal condition; I long for it as eagerly as one parched with thirst longs to drink, like a calf pursuing its own mother'" — one should know this person is a practitioner of faith. Should the person instead answer, "I have heard the Buddha say: 'The substance of a bright mirror, when unmoving, reflects every form and image with perfect clarity; still water, without a single ripple, lets fish and stones appear of themselves. I delight in casting aside deluded thought, as one casts aside a heavy burden carried on the shoulders'" — one should know this person is a practitioner of dharma.

Reading note: The transformation formula opening Mode Three — "its arising is the Dharma-nature's own arising; its ceasing is the Dharma-nature's own ceasing" — is the practical payoff of everything Mode One established: since ignorance and the Dharma-nature were never actually two different things, calming does not need to suppress delusion, only stop mistaking it for something other than what it already is. The blind-turtle-and-floating-log simile, borrowed from the sūtras' own standard image for how rare a genuinely enlightened teacher is, sets up the entire remaining architecture of Mode Three: since no infallible teacher can be assumed, the "ordinary teacher" must instead rely on a diagnostic system — faith-practitioner or dharma-practitioner, sharp or dull in different respects — precisely because no single approach fits every student.

Eight Ways of Settling the Mind for the Practitioner of Faith 善巧安心 · 信行八番

Chinese Source

既知根性，於一人所，八番安心。咄！善男子！無量劫來飲狂散毒，馳逐五塵，升沈三界。猶如猛風吹兜羅𣭞；大熱沸鑊煮豆升沈。從苦至惱，從惱至苦，何不息心達本，以一其意？意若一者，何事不辦？苦集得一，則不輪迴；無明得一，不至於行，乃至不至老死。摧折大樹，畢故不造新；六蔽得一，則度彼岸，唯此為快。善巧方便，種種因緣，種種譬喻，廣讚於止，發悅其情。是名「隨樂欲以止安心」也。又善男子！如天亢旱，河池悉乾，萬卉焦枯，百穀零落；娑伽羅龍王七日構雲，四方注雨，大地霑洽，一切種子皆萌芽，一切根株皆開發，一切枝葉皆蔚茂，一切華果皆敷榮。人亦如是，以散逸故，應生善，不復生；已生善，還退失。禪定河乾，道品樹滅，萬善焦枯，百福殘悴，因華道果不復成熟。若能閑林一意，內不出，外不入，靜雲興也。發諸禪定，即是降雨也；功德叢林，煗頂方便，眼智明覺，信忍、順忍、無生、寂滅，乃至無上菩提，悉皆克獲。善巧方便，種種緣喻，廣讚於止，生其善根。是名「隨便宜以止安心」也。又善男子！夫散心者，惡中之惡。如無鉤醉象，踏壞華池；穴鼻駱駝，翻倒負馱；疾於掣電，毒逾蛇舌；重沓五翳，埃靄曜靈；睫近霄遠，俱皆不見。若能修定，如密室中燈能破巨闇；金錍抉膜，空色朗然，一指、二指、三指皆了。大雨能淹囂塵，大定能靜狂逸。止能破散，虛妄滅矣！善巧方便，種種緣喻，廣讚於止，破其睡散。是名「對治以止安心」也。又善男子！心若在定，能知世間生滅法相，亦知出世不生不滅法相。如來成道猶尚樂定，況諸凡夫？有禪定者，如夜見電光，即得見道，破無數億洞然之惡，乃至得成一切種智。善巧方便，種種緣喻，廣讚於止，即會真如。是名「隨第一義以止安心」也。

Ru-Yi Translation

Once the person's own capacity is known, there are eight methods of settling the mind for a single person. "Listen well, good son! Across immeasurable kalpas, you have drunk the poison of frenzy and dissipation, chasing after the five sense-objects, rising and sinking through the three realms — like a fierce wind scattering tufts of kapok cotton, or like a bean boiling in a great, roiling cauldron, now rising, now sinking. From suffering you reach vexation, and from vexation you reach suffering once more — why not still the mind and reach its very root, unifying your own intent? Once the intent is unified, what matter could remain unaccomplished? Suffering and its origin, once unified, no longer turn through the cycle of rebirth; ignorance, once unified, no longer reaches as far as formations, and so on, no longer reaching even as far as old age and death. Felling the great tree at a single stroke, finished with the old, one creates nothing new; the six defilements, once unified, are thereby crossed over to the far shore — this alone is true joy." Skillful expedient means, every kind of causal condition, every kind of simile — broadly praising calming in this way, one's own feeling is thereby delighted. This is named "settling the mind through calming, in accordance with one's own aspiration." Further, good son! It is like a great drought, when every river and pond runs dry, every plant scorched and withered, every grain crop fallen to ruin — until the nāga-king Sāgara gathers clouds for seven days, pouring rain down upon every direction, the great earth thoroughly soaked, and every seed sprouts, every root and stem opens forth, every branch and leaf grows lush, and every flower and fruit blossoms in abundance. A person is exactly the same: on account of dissipation, wholesomeness that ought to arise fails to arise, and wholesomeness already arisen falls back and is lost. The river of meditative concentration runs dry, the tree of the branches of the path withers away, every wholesome thing scorches and shrivels, every merit decays — the flower of practice and the fruit of the path fail to ripen. But should one, in the quiet of the forest, unify the intent, nothing going out, nothing coming in, the still clouds gather themselves. Giving rise to every kind of meditative concentration is itself the falling of the rain: the forest and grove of merit, the expedient means of warmth and summit, the clear awakening of eye and wisdom, the forbearance of faith, the forbearance of accord, the unborn, quiescent extinction, and on up to the unsurpassed bodhi itself — every one of these is thereby attained." Skillful expedient means, every kind of condition and simile — broadly praising calming in this way, one's own wholesome root is thereby generated. This is named "settling the mind through calming, in accordance with what is fitting." Further, good son! A scattered mind is the very worst among evils — like a drunken elephant with no hook to restrain it, trampling and ruining a garden of flowers; like a camel with a pierced nose, overturning and spilling its own load; swifter than a flash of lightning, more venomous than a serpent's tongue; the five obscurations piled up heavily, dust and mist dimming the very brightness of the sun — one cannot see even what lies close as one's own eyelash, let alone what lies far in the distant sky. But should one be able to cultivate concentration, it is like a lamp within a sealed chamber, able to break through the deepest darkness; like a golden probe removing a cataract from the eye, so that both emptiness and form grow bright and clear, and one, two, three fingers held before the eye can all be counted at once. A great rain is able to settle clamorous dust; a great concentration is able to still wild dissipation. Calming is able to break through scatteredness, and falsehood is thereby extinguished!" Skillful expedient means, every kind of condition and simile — broadly praising calming in this way, one's own drowsiness and scatteredness are thereby broken. This is named "settling the mind through calming, as an antidote." Further, good son! When the mind abides in concentration, it is able to know the marks of arising and ceasing throughout the mundane world, and is also able to know the unborn, unceasing marks that transcend the world. Even the Tathāgata, upon attaining the path, still delighted in concentration — how much more, then, should an ordinary being? One who possesses meditative concentration is like one who sees a flash of lightning at night, and thereby glimpses the path at once, breaking through countless hundreds of millions of blazing evils, and eventually accomplishing the wisdom of all modes." Skillful expedient means, every kind of condition and simile — broadly praising calming in this way, one thereby accords directly with suchness itself. This is named "settling the mind through calming, in accordance with the ultimate meaning."

其人若言：「我聞寂滅，都不入懷；若聞分別，聽受無厭。」即應為說：三惡燒然，駝驢重楚，餓鬼飢渴，不名為苦；癡闇無聞，不識方隅，乃是大苦。多聞分別樂，見法法喜樂，以善攻惡樂；無著阿羅漢，是名為最樂。從多聞人聞甘露樂，如教觀察，知道、非道，遠離坑坎，直去不迴。善巧方便，種種緣喻，廣讚於觀，發悅其情。是名「隨樂欲以觀安心」。又善男子！月開蓮華，日興作務；商應隨主，彩畫須膠。坯不遇火，無須臾用；盲不得導，一步不前。行無觀智，亦復如是。一切種智以觀為根本，無量功德之所莊嚴。善巧方便，種種緣喻，廣讚於觀，生其功德。是名「隨便宜以觀安心」。又善男子！智者識怨，怨不能害；武將有謀，能破強敵。非風何以卷雲？非雲何以遮熱？非水何以滅火？非火何以除闇？析薪之斧，解縛之刀，豈過智慧？善巧方便，種種緣喻，廣讚於觀，使其破惡。是名「對治以觀安心」。又善男子！井中七寶、闇室瓶盆要待日明。日既出已，皆得明了。須智慧眼，觀知諸法實相；一切諸法中，皆以等觀入。般若波羅蜜最為照明。善巧方便，種種緣喻，廣讚於觀，令得悟解。是名「第一義以觀安心」。如是八番為信行人說安心也。

Should the person say, "What I hear of quiescent extinction does not enter my own heart at all; but what I hear of careful distinctions, I listen to and receive without ever tiring" — one should then explain to them: the three evil destinies burning in flame, the camel and the donkey's own heavy beatings, the hunger and thirst of the hungry ghosts — none of these is named true suffering; rather, it is the darkness of delusion, deprived of learning, unable to recognize any direction at all, that is the truly great suffering. The bliss of broad learning and careful distinction, the bliss of seeing the Dharma and rejoicing in it, the bliss of using what is wholesome to attack what is unwholesome — the arhat free of attachment is named the very greatest bliss of all. Hearing the bliss of the ambrosial Dharma from one of broad learning, and observing accordingly as taught, one knows the path from what is not the path, remaining far removed from every pitfall, going straight forward without turning back. Skillful expedient means, every kind of condition and simile — broadly praising contemplation in this way, one's own feeling is thereby delighted. This is named "settling the mind through contemplation, in accordance with one's own aspiration." Further, good son! The moon causes the lotus to open; the sun spurs on every undertaking; the merchant must follow the caravan's own leader; painting requires glue to bind its pigments. An unfired clay pot, never having met with fire, has not the least use at all; the blind, lacking a guide, cannot take even a single step forward. Practice without the wisdom of contemplation is exactly the same. The wisdom of all modes takes contemplation as its very root, adorned by immeasurable merit. Skillful expedient means, every kind of condition and simile — broadly praising contemplation in this way, one's own merit is thereby generated. This is named "settling the mind through contemplation, in accordance with what is fitting." Further, good son! The wise recognize their own enemy, and the enemy is thereby unable to cause harm; a military commander with a strategy is able to defeat even a powerful foe. Without wind, how could clouds be swept away? Without clouds, how could the heat be screened off? Without water, how could fire be extinguished? Without fire, how could darkness be removed? The axe that splits firewood, the blade that cuts a binding rope — how could either surpass wisdom itself? Skillful expedient means, every kind of condition and simile — broadly praising contemplation in this way, one is thereby enabled to break through evil. This is named "settling the mind through contemplation, as an antidote." Further, good son! The seven treasures lying within a well, the vase and basin within a darkened room, all require the sun's own light to be seen. Once the sun has risen, everything becomes clear and distinct. The eye of wisdom is needed to contemplate and know the true mark of reality in every dharma; within every dharma without exception, one enters by means of contemplating their very equality. Prajñāpāramitā is the very foremost in illuminating all of this. Skillful expedient means, every kind of condition and simile — broadly praising contemplation in this way, understanding is thereby attained. This is named "settling the mind through contemplation, in accordance with the ultimate meaning." These, then, are the eight methods for settling the mind, taught to the practitioner of faith.

Reading note: The direct address — "Listen well, good son!" — shifts the register from doctrinal exposition to something closer to transcribed oral instruction, and it is worth noticing that seven of these eight vivid similes (the boiling bean, the drought ended by the nāga-king's rain, the drunken elephant, the lamp in a sealed chamber, the lightning-flash at night, the unfired clay pot, the recognized enemy) are addressed to feeling and imagination rather than argument. This is the treatise practicing exactly what fascicle 1 preached about the four siddhāntas: the same eight destinations — calming and contemplation, each aligned to aspiration, fitness, antidote, and ultimate meaning — reached through image and story rather than through the tetralemma logic that dominates the surrounding sections.

Eight Ways of Settling the Mind for the Practitioner of Dharma 善巧安心 · 法行八番

Chinese Source

其人若云：「我樂息心，默已復默，損之又損之，遂至於無為。不樂分別，坐馳無益。」此則法行根性，當為說止：汝勿外尋，但內守一；攀覺流動皆從妄生。如旋火輪，輟手則息；洪波鼓怒，風靜則澄。《淨名》云：「何謂攀緣？謂緣三界。何謂息攀緣？謂心無所得。」《瑞應》云：「其得一心者，則萬邪滅矣。」龍樹云：「實法不顛倒，念想觀已除，言語法皆滅。無量眾罪除，清淨心常一。如是尊妙人，則能見般若。」夫山中幽寂，神仙所讚；況涅槃澄淨，賢聖尊崇。《佛話經》云：「比丘在聚，身口精勤，諸佛咸憂；比丘在山，息事安臥，諸佛皆喜。」況復結跏束手，緘脣結舌，思惟寂相；心源一止，法界同寂，豈非要道？唯此為貴，餘不能及！善巧方便，種種因緣，種種譬喻，廣讚於止發悅其心。是名「隨樂欲以止安心」。其人若云：「我觀法相，秖增紛動，善法不明。」當為說止：止是法界平正良田，何法不備？上捨攀緣，即是檀；止體非惡，即是戒；止體不動，即是忍；止無間雜，即是精進；止則決定，即是禪；止法亦無，止者亦無，即是慧；因止會非止非不止，即是方便；一止一切止，即是願；止止愛，止止見，即是力；此止如佛止，無二無別，即是智。止具一切法，即是祕藏。但安於止，何用別修諸法？善巧方便，種種緣喻，令生善根，即是「隨便宜以止安心」也。若言「我觀法相，散睡不除」者，當為說止大有功能。止是壁定，八風惡覺所不能入。止是淨水，蕩於貪婬八倒；猶如朝露，見陽則晞。止是大慈，怨親俱愍，能破恚怒。止是大明呪，癡疑皆遣。止即是佛，破除障道，如阿伽陀藥遍治一切，如妙良醫呪枯起死。善巧方便，種種緣喻，令其破惡。是名「對治以止安心」。其人若言：「我觀察時不得開悟。」當為說止：止即體真，照而常寂；止即隨緣，寂而常照；止即不止止，雙遮雙照。止即佛母，止即佛父，亦即父即母。止即佛師、佛身、佛眼、佛之相好、佛藏、佛住處，何所不具？何所不除？善巧方便，種種緣喻，廣讚於止。是為「第一義以止安心」。

Ru-Yi Translation

Should the person instead say, "I delight in stilling the mind, silence upon silence, diminishing it and diminishing it further, until I reach the unconditioned itself; I take no delight in careful distinction, and sitting there, racing about in thought brings no benefit at all" — this, then, is the capacity of the practitioner of dharma, and one should explain calming to them: do not search outwardly, but simply hold to the one within; grasping and awareness, in their restless flowing, all arise from delusion alone. It is like a whirling firebrand's circle of flame — cease moving the hand, and it stops; like a great wave stirred up in fury — let the wind grow still, and it settles clear. The Vimalakīrti Sūtra says: "What is meant by clinging and pursuing? It means clinging to the three realms. What is meant by ceasing clinging and pursuing? It means the mind attains nothing at all." The Sūtra of Auspicious Manifestations says: "One who attains a single, unified mind — every deviance is thereby extinguished." Nāgārjuna says: "The real dharma is free of inversion — thought, notion, and observation are already removed, and the very dharma of language is, entirely, extinguished. Boundless offenses are thereby removed, the pure mind ever one; a person of such honor and sublimity is then able to see prajñā." The deep seclusion of the mountains is praised even by the immortals — how much more, then, should the clear purity of nirvāṇa be honored by the worthy and the sage? The Buddha's Own Discourse Sūtra says: "When a monk dwells amid the crowd, body and speech kept diligently occupied, every buddha alike grows concerned; when a monk dwells in the mountains, every affair stilled, resting in peace, every buddha alike rejoices." How much more, then, sitting cross-legged, hands folded, lips sealed and tongue stilled, reflecting on the mark of quiescence — the very source of the mind stilled as one, the dharma-realm alike quiescent — is this not the essential path? This alone is to be prized; nothing else can compare to it!" Skillful expedient means, every kind of causal condition, every kind of simile — broadly praising calming in this way, one's own heart is thereby delighted. This is named "settling the mind through calming, in accordance with one's own aspiration." Should the person say, "The more I contemplate the marks of dharmas, the more this only increases confusion and agitation, and wholesome dharmas grow no clearer" — one should then explain calming to them: calming is itself the level, fertile field of the dharma-realm — what dharma is not already complete within it? Relinquishing all grasping and pursuit, discussed above, is itself generosity; calming's own substance, free of evil, is itself the precepts; calming's own substance, unmoving, is itself forbearance; calming, without interruption or admixture, is itself diligent effort; calming, once settled, is itself dhyāna; calming as a dharma being itself nonexistent, and the one who calms likewise nonexistent, is itself wisdom; through calming, according with what is neither calming nor not-calming, is itself skillful means; a single calming being every calming is itself the vow; calming stilling craving, and calming stilling view, is itself power; this calming, being the very calming of the Buddha himself, with no two, no separation at all, is itself wisdom. Calming is complete with every dharma — it is itself the secret treasury. Simply settling within calming — what need is there to cultivate any other dharma separately?" Skillful expedient means, every kind of condition and simile, causing one's own wholesome root to be generated — this is "settling the mind through calming, in accordance with what is fitting." Should the person say, "I contemplate the marks of dharmas, and yet scatteredness and drowsiness are not removed" — one should then explain that calming has vast capacity indeed. Calming is a wall of concentration, which the eight winds and every evil thought cannot penetrate. Calming is pure water, washing away lust and the eight inversions — like the morning dew, which vanishes the moment the sun appears. Calming is great loving-kindness, showing compassion to friend and foe alike, able to break through anger and rage. Calming is the great illuminating mantra, dispelling delusion and doubt alike. Calming is itself the Buddha, breaking through every obstruction to the path, like the universal medicine that cures every ailment, like a skilled and wondrous physician whose very incantation revives the withered and raises the dead." Skillful expedient means, every kind of condition and simile, causing evil to be broken through — this is named "settling the mind through calming, as an antidote."
