Should the person say, "When I observe closely, I still fail to reach any awakening at all" — one should then explain calming to them: calming is itself embodying the real, illuminating even while ever quiescent; calming is itself according with conditions, quiescent even while ever illuminating; calming is itself the calming that is neither calming nor not-calming, obstructing both extremes and illuminating both at once. Calming is itself the mother of the Buddha, calming is itself the father of the Buddha, at once both father and mother together. Calming is itself the Buddha's own teacher, the Buddha's own body, the Buddha's own eye, the Buddha's own excellent marks, the Buddha's own treasury, the Buddha's own dwelling-place — what is there that it fails to be complete with? What is there that it fails to remove?" Skillful expedient means, every kind of condition and simile, broadly praising calming in this way — this is "settling the mind through calming, in accordance with the ultimate meaning."

彼人若言：「止狀沈寂，非我悅樂。」當為說觀，推尋道理。七覺中有擇覺分，八正中有正見，六度中有般若，於法門中為主、為導，乃至成佛。正覺、大覺、遍覺，皆是觀慧異名，當知觀慧最為尊妙。如是廣讚，是為「隨樂欲以觀安心」。若勤修觀，能生信戒、定、慧、解脫、解脫知見。知病識藥，化道大行，眾善普會，莫復過觀。是為「隨便宜以觀安心」。觀能破闇，能照道，能除怨，能得寶；傾邪山，竭愛海，皆觀之力。是為「隨對治以觀安心」。觀觀法時，不得能、所，心慮虛豁，朦朧欲開，但當勤觀開、示、悟、入。是為用「第一義以觀安心」。是為八番為法行人說安心也。

Should that person say, "Calming has the appearance of sinking into mere quiescence — this is not what truly delights me" — one should then explain contemplation to them, tracing out the reasoning behind it. Among the seven factors of enlightenment, there is the factor of discernment; among the eightfold noble path, there is right view; among the six pāramitās, there is prajñā — within every gateway of the Dharma, this serves as master and as guide, all the way through to the accomplishment of buddhahood itself. Correct awakening, great awakening, universal awakening — these are, every one, simply other names for the wisdom of contemplation; one should know that the wisdom of contemplation is the very most honored and sublime of all." Broadly praised in this way, this is "settling the mind through contemplation, in accordance with one's own aspiration." Should one diligently cultivate contemplation, one is able to give rise to faith, precepts, concentration, wisdom, liberation, and the knowledge and vision of liberation. Knowing the sickness, recognizing the remedy, the great practice of transformative guidance, every wholesome thing gathering together in abundance — nothing surpasses contemplation for this. This is "settling the mind through contemplation, in accordance with what is fitting." Contemplation is able to break through darkness, able to illuminate the path, able to remove one's own enemy, able to attain the treasure; toppling the mountain of deviance, exhausting the ocean of craving — all of this is the very power of contemplation. This is "settling the mind through contemplation, as an antidote." When contemplating a dharma through contemplation, one no longer grasps at any agent or object at all; the mind's own concern grows empty and expansive, hazy yet on the verge of opening — one should simply diligently contemplate opening, revealing, awakening, and entering. This is using "contemplation, in accordance with the ultimate meaning, to settle the mind." These, then, are the eight methods for settling the mind, taught to the practitioner of dharma.

Reading note: The two eight-fold sets are not simply mirror images — the practitioner of faith is offered calming and contemplation each praised for their own separate virtues, while the practitioner of dharma, already suspicious of "sitting there, racing about in thought," receives calming reinterpreted through all ten pāramitā-like categories at once (generosity, precepts, forbearance, effort, dhyāna, wisdom, skillful means, vow, power, wisdom again) before contemplation is introduced at all, precisely because this student needs to be shown that stillness is not passivity before being asked to add anything more active to it. The Buddha's Own Discourse Sūtra's contrast — monks in the crowd worrying every buddha, monks in the mountains delighting every buddha — is one of the treatise's more startling single lines, framing solitude not as escape but as itself the more advanced practice.

Shifting Capacity, Self-Practice, and Mutual Support: Up to 128 Methods 善巧安心 · 轉根 · 自行 · 相資

Chinese Source

復次，人根不定，或時迴轉。薩婆多明轉鈍為利；《成論》明數習則利。此乃始終論利鈍，不得一時辯也。今明眾生心行不定，或須臾而鈍，須叟而利，任運自爾，非關根轉，亦不數習。或作觀不徹，因聽即悟；或久聽不解，暫思即決。是故更論轉根安心。若法行轉為信行，逐其根轉，用八番悉檀而授安心。若信行轉成法行，亦逐根轉，用八番悉檀而授安心。得此意，廣略自在說之。轉、不轉，合有三十二安心也。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, a person's own capacity is not fixed, and may at times shift and turn. The Sarvāstivādins explain that dull capacity can be turned into sharp; the Satyasiddhi-śāstra explains that repeated practice makes it sharp. But these accounts discuss sharpness and dullness only from beginning to end, and cannot address the matter as it happens in the very moment. Here, it is clarified that a sentient being's own mental activity is itself unfixed — sometimes dull in one instant, sharp in the very next, simply so of itself, effortlessly, having nothing to do with any gradual turning of capacity, nor with repeated practice either. Sometimes contemplation, pursued without full penetration, is followed by sudden awakening upon merely hearing something further; sometimes prolonged hearing brings no understanding at all, and yet a brief moment of reflection resolves it at once. This is exactly why the settling of the mind must be discussed once more, in terms of this shifting capacity. Should the practitioner of dharma shift into a practitioner of faith, one follows this shift in capacity, applying the eight siddhāntas to bestow the settling of the mind accordingly. Should the practitioner of faith shift into a practitioner of dharma, one likewise follows this shift in capacity, applying the eight siddhāntas to bestow the settling of the mind accordingly. Grasping this sense, one may speak of it broadly or briefly, quite freely. Shifting or not shifting, this makes thirty-two methods of settling the mind in all.

自行安心者，當觀察此心，欲何所樂？若欲息妄，令念想寂然，是樂法行。若樂聽聞，徹無明底，是樂信行。樂寂者，如妄從心出，息心則眾妄皆靜。若欲照知，須知心原。心原不二，則一切諸法皆同虛空。是為「隨樂欲自行安心」。其心雖廣分別心及諸法，而信念精進毫善不生，即當凝停莫動；諸善功德因靜而生。若凝停時，薳更沈寂都無進忍，當校計籌量，策之令起。若念念不住如汗馬奔逸，即當以止對治馳蕩。若靜默無記與睡相應，即當修觀破諸昏塞。修止既久，不能開發，即應修觀；觀一切法無礙無異，怗怗明利，漸覺如空。修觀若久，闇障不除，宜更修止；止諸緣念，無能、無所，所我皆寂，空慧將生。是為自修法行八番善巧布厝令得心安云云。信行安心者，或欲聞寂定如須彌不畏八動，即應聽止。欲聞利觀破諸煩惱如日除闇，即應聽觀。聽觀多，如日焦芽，即應聽止，潤以定水。或聽定淹久，如芽爛不生，即應聽觀，令風日發動使善法現前。或時馳覺，一念叵住，即應聽止，以治散心。或沈昏濛濛坐霧，即當聽觀，破此睡熟。或聽止豁豁，即專聽止；或聞觀朗朗，即專聽觀。是為自修信行八番巧安心也。若法行心轉為信行，信行心轉為法行，皆隨其所宜，巧鑽研之。自行有三十二，化他亦三十二，合為六十四安心也。

As for settling the mind through one's own self-practice: one should observe this mind closely — what does it truly desire? If it wishes to still deluded thought, making every notion quiescent, this is the delight of the practitioner of dharma. If it delights in hearing and listening, penetrating to the very bottom of ignorance, this is the delight of the practitioner of faith. As for delighting in quiescence: since deluded thought issues from the mind itself, stilling the mind stills every delusion along with it. If one wishes to illuminate and know directly, one must know the very source of the mind; the source of the mind being non-dual, every dharma is then, alike, the same as empty space. This is "settling the mind through self-practice, in accordance with one's own aspiration." Though this mind may broadly distinguish itself and every other dharma, if faith, mindfulness, and diligence produce not even the slightest wholesome quality, one should then hold it fixed and still, without moving; every wholesome merit is born from stillness. But should this fixed stillness only sink further into deep torpor, with no forward progress or forbearance arising at all, one should weigh and measure the matter, spurring it back into motion. Should thought after thought fail to remain settled, racing off like a sweating horse in flight, one should then apply calming as the antidote to this restless racing. Should silent stillness, indeterminate, merge into sleep, one should then cultivate contemplation to break through this drowsy obstruction. Should calming, long cultivated, fail to open into further understanding, one should then cultivate contemplation instead, contemplating every dharma as unobstructed and undifferentiated, settled and sharply clear, gradually perceiving it as like empty space. Should contemplation, long cultivated, fail to remove this darkening obstruction, one should return once more to cultivating calming, calming every conditioned thought, with neither agent nor object, both the object and the self alike quiescent, so that the wisdom of emptiness is about to arise. These are the eight skillful arrangements for cultivating the practitioner of dharma's own self-practice, so that the mind is settled. As for settling the mind for the practitioner of faith: should one wish to hear of a quiescent concentration like Mount Sumeru, unafraid of the eight winds that stir it, one should then listen to teaching on calming. Should one wish to hear of a sharp contemplation that breaks through every affliction like the sun dispelling darkness, one should then listen to teaching on contemplation. Should listening to teaching on contemplation grow excessive, like the sun scorching a young sprout, one should then listen to teaching on calming, moistening it with the water of concentration. Should listening to teaching on calming linger too long, like a sprout rotting away, unable to grow, one should then listen to teaching on contemplation, letting the wind and sun stir it into motion, so that wholesome dharmas may manifest. Should awareness race about at times, unable to settle even for a single thought, one should then listen to teaching on calming, to treat this scattered mind. Should one sink into a hazy, foggy torpor, one should then listen to teaching on contemplation, to break through this heavy drowsiness. Should teaching on calming bring sudden clarity, one should then listen exclusively to teaching on calming; should teaching on contemplation bring bright clarity, one should then listen exclusively to teaching on contemplation. These are the eight skillful methods for the practitioner of faith's own self-cultivated settling of the mind. Should the mind of a practitioner of dharma shift into that of a practitioner of faith, or the mind of a practitioner of faith shift into that of a practitioner of dharma, in every case one follows whatever is fitting, skillfully refining the approach accordingly. Self-practice thus has thirty-two methods; instructing others likewise has thirty-two methods, making sixty-four methods of settling the mind altogether.

復次，信、法不孤立，須聞思相資。如法行者，隨聞一句「體寂湛然，夢妄皆遣」，還坐思惟，心生歡喜。又聞止已，還更思惟，即生禪定。又聞於止，還即思惟，妄念皆破。又聞止已，還更思惟，朗然欲悟。又聞觀已，還更思惟，心大歡喜。又聞觀已，還更思惟，生善、破惡、欲悟等，准前可知。此乃聽少思多，名為「法行」，非都不聽法也。信行端坐，思惟寂滅，欣踊未生；起已聞止，歡喜甘樂。端坐念善，善不能發；起已聞止，信戒精進倍更增多。端坐治惡，惡不能遣；起已聞止，散動破滅。端坐即真，真道不啟；起已聞止，豁如悟寂。是為信行，坐少聞多，非都不思惟。前作一向根性，今作相資根性。就相資中，復論轉、不轉，亦有三十二安心。化他相資亦有三十二安心，合六十四。合前為一百二十八安心也。夫心地難安，違苦順樂；今隨其所願，逐而安之。譬如養生，或飲或食，適身立命。養法身亦爾，以止為飲，以觀為食。藥法亦兩，或丸或散，以除冷熱。治無明病。以止為丸，以觀為散。如陰陽法，陽則風日，陰則雲雨；雨多則爛，日多則焦。陰如定，陽如慧；慧定偏者皆不見佛性。八番調和，貴在得意。一種禪師不許作觀，唯專用止，引偈云：「思思徒自思，思思徒自苦；息思即是道，有思終不覩。」又一禪師不許作止，專在於觀，引偈云：「止止徒自止，昏闇無所以；止止即是道，觀觀得會理。」兩師各從一門而入，以己益教他。學者則不見意，一向服乳，漿猶難得，況復醍醐？若一向作解者，佛何故種種說耶？天不常晴，醫不專散，食不恒飯；世間尚不爾，況出世耶？今隨根、隨病迴轉，自行、化他有六十四。若就三番止觀，則三百八十四。又一心止觀，復有六十四，合五百一十二。三悉檀是世間安心，世醫所治，差已復生；一悉檀是出世安心，如來所治，畢竟不發。世、出世法互相成顯。若離三諦，無安心處；若離止觀，無安心法。若心安於諦，一句即足；如其不安，巧用方便令心得安。一目之羅不能得鳥；得鳥者，羅之一目耳。眾生心行各各不同；或多人同一心行，或一人多種心行。如為一人，眾多亦然；如為多人，一人亦然。須廣施法網之目，捕心行之鳥耳。

Furthermore, faith and dharma do not stand entirely alone — hearing and reflection must support one another. As with the practitioner of dharma: hearing but a single phrase, such as "the substance is quiescent, deep and clear, every dream and delusion dispelled," they return to sitting in reflection, and the heart gives rise to joy. Or, having heard of calming, they return once more to reflection, and dhyāna is thereby born. Or, having heard of calming, they return at once to reflection, and every deluded thought is thereby broken. Or, having heard of calming, they return once more to reflection, and understanding dawns, on the very verge of awakening. Or, having heard of contemplation, they return once more to reflection, and the heart gives rise to great joy. Or, having heard of contemplation, they return once more to reflection, giving rise to wholesomeness, breaking through evil, on the verge of awakening, and so on — this may be understood by the same pattern as before. This, then, is hearing little and reflecting much, named "the practitioner of dharma" — not one who fails to listen to the Dharma at all. The practitioner of faith, sitting upright, reflecting on quiescent extinction, gives rise to no joy or delight at all; rising from sitting and hearing teaching on calming, joy and delight follow. Sitting upright, mindful of what is wholesome, wholesomeness fails to arise; rising and hearing teaching on calming, faith, precept, and diligence redouble further still. Sitting upright, treating what is unwholesome, the unwholesome fails to be dispelled; rising and hearing teaching on calming, agitation is broken through and extinguished. Sitting upright, seeking the real directly, the real path fails to open; rising and hearing teaching on calming, understanding dawns all at once, as though awakening to quiescence itself. This, then, is the practitioner of faith, sitting little and hearing much — not one who fails to reflect at all. What came before treated capacity as fixed in one single direction; here, capacity is treated as mutually supporting instead. Within this mutual support, shifting or not shifting, too, may be discussed, yielding thirty-two further methods of settling the mind. Instructing others through mutual support likewise yields thirty-two methods, making sixty-four in all. Combined with what came before, this makes one hundred and twenty-eight methods of settling the mind altogether. The very ground of the mind is difficult to settle, resisting suffering and pursuing pleasure; here, one follows the mind's own wish, and settles it accordingly. It is like nourishing life itself, through drink or through food, each fitting the body and sustaining life; nourishing the Dharma-body is exactly the same, taking calming as the drink and contemplation as the food. The dharma of medicine, too, is twofold, whether pill or powder, to remove cold or heat, treating the sickness of ignorance — taking calming as the pill and contemplation as the powder. It is like the dharma of yin and yang: yang is wind and sun; yin is cloud and rain — too much rain causes rot, too much sun causes scorching. Yin is like concentration; yang is like wisdom — one one-sidedly fixed on either wisdom or concentration alike fails to see the buddha-nature. Harmonizing these eight methods, the value lies in grasping their true sense. There is a certain meditation teacher who does not permit the practice of contemplation, using calming alone and exclusively, citing a verse: "Thinking upon thinking is merely thinking for its own sake; thinking upon thinking is merely suffering for its own sake. Stilling thought is itself the path; where thought remains, one never truly sees at all." There is another meditation teacher who does not permit the practice of calming, focusing on contemplation alone, citing a verse: "Calming upon calming is merely calming for its own sake, dim and dark, to no purpose at all; calming upon calming is itself the path; contemplation upon contemplation is what truly grasps principle." Each of these two teachers enters through only a single gate, and teaches others according to what has benefited themselves alone. Students, then, fail to grasp the true sense of it — drinking only milk, even a simple curd remains difficult to attain, how much less the finest ghee? If one were to form a single, fixed understanding only, why would the Buddha have taught in so many different ways at all? The sky is not always clear; a physician does not prescribe only a single powder; food is not always plain rice — even worldly matters are not so fixed, how much less, then, the world-transcending Dharma? Here, following capacity, following sickness, turning about accordingly, self-practice and instructing others together yield sixty-four methods. Applying this to the three successive contemplations, this yields three hundred and eighty-four; and the single mind's own calming-and-contemplation yields a further sixty-four, making five hundred and twelve in all. The first three siddhāntas are worldly methods of settling the mind, like a worldly physician's own treatment — cured, and yet liable to fall ill again; the fourth siddhānta alone is the world-transcending method of settling the mind, the Tathāgata's own treatment, never to recur once cured. The worldly and the world-transcending dharma mutually complete and reveal one another. Apart from the three truths, there is no place to settle the mind at all; apart from calming-and-contemplation, there is no method for settling it either. If the mind is already settled in the truth, a single phrase alone suffices; but should it not yet be settled, skillful means must be applied so that the mind may attain its settling. A net with only a single mesh cannot catch a bird; and yet what actually catches the bird is only ever a single mesh of that net. Sentient beings' own mental activity differs from one to the next — sometimes many people share the very same mental activity; sometimes a single person passes through many different kinds of mental activity in turn. What applies to a single person applies equally to many; what applies to many applies equally to a single person. One must broadly cast the net of the Dharma, mesh by mesh, in order to catch the very birds of the mind.

Reading note: The arithmetic climbing from eight methods to thirty-two, sixty-four, and finally five hundred and twelve is not numerology for its own sake — it is Zhiyi's own way of insisting that a fixed technique, however well-intentioned, eventually fails someone, and the two rival meditation teachers quoted by name (one permitting only calming, one only contemplation) are given as a cautionary tale of exactly this failure: each "teaches others according to what has benefited themselves alone." The closing image reframes the entire, dizzying combinatorial system in a single practical stroke: nobody needs all five hundred and twelve methods at once, any more than a bird is caught by an entire net rather than the single mesh it actually flies into — the vast apparatus exists only so that whichever single method a given mind actually needs will be there when it is needed.

Mode Four: Comprehensively Breaking Through Every Dharma 破法遍

Chinese Source

第四、明破法遍者，法性清淨，不合不散，言語道斷，心行處滅，非破非不破，何故言破？但眾生多顛倒，少不顛倒；破顛倒令不顛倒，故言「破法遍」耳。上善巧安心則定慧開發，不俟更破。若未相應，應用有定之慧而盡淨之，故言「破」耳。然破法須依門，經說門不同：或文字為門，《大品》明「四十二字門」是也；或觀行為門，《釋論》明「菩薩修三三昧緣諸法實相」是也；或智慧為門，《法華》云「其智慧門」是也；或理為門，《大品》明「無生法無來無去即是佛」也。依教門通觀，依觀門通智，依智門通理。理為門，復通何處？教、觀、智等諸門悉依於理；能依是門，所依何得非門？雖無所通，究竟遍通，是妙門也。三門置之，今但說教門。三藏四門，先破見，後破思，亦俱破云云。通教四門，亦先破見，後破思，亦俱破。但破四住，不得言「遍」也。別教四門，次第斷五住，斯乃竪遍、橫不遍，並非今所用。今不思議，一境一切境，一心一切心。橫竪諸法，悉趣於心；破心故，一切皆破，故言「遍」也。餘門破不遍，則不須說。圓教四門，皆能破遍，所謂：有門、無門、亦有亦無門、非有非無門。今且置三門，且依空無生門。無生門能通止觀到因、到果；又能顯無生，使門光揚。何者？止觀是行，無生門是教；依教修行，通至無生法忍，因位具足。《淨名》三十二菩薩各說入不二門，皆是菩薩從門入位，而無生為首。《大品》明阿字門，所謂「諸法初不生」，此證無生門通止觀到因，其義可見。止觀光揚無生門者，法不自顯，弘之在人。人能行行，法門光顯，使無生教縱橫無礙，觸處皆通，門義方成。譬如世人，門戶出入，有人、有位，門則榮顯。能譬既然，所譬可解。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fourth, clarifying comprehensively breaking through every dharma: the Dharma-nature is pure, neither combined nor dispersed, the path of words cut off, the sphere of the mind's own activity extinguished — neither broken nor unbroken. Why, then, speak of "breaking" at all? Simply because sentient beings are, for the most part, inverted, and only rarely free of inversion — breaking through inversion so that it becomes freedom from inversion is exactly why this is named "comprehensively breaking through every dharma." Above, once the mind is skillfully settled, concentration and wisdom already open and unfold, with no need for any further breaking-through. But should this accord not yet be reached, one must apply the wisdom already present in concentration to exhaust and purify it entirely — hence the word "breaking." Yet breaking through dharmas must proceed by way of a gate, and the sūtras name different gates: sometimes the written word serves as the gate, as the Larger Sūtra clarifies with its "forty-two letter gates"; sometimes contemplative practice serves as the gate, as the Treatise clarifies with "the bodhisattva cultivating the three samādhis, taking the true mark of every dharma as the object"; sometimes wisdom serves as the gate, as the Lotus Sūtra speaks of "its own gate of wisdom"; sometimes principle itself serves as the gate, as the Larger Sūtra clarifies that "the unborn dharma, neither coming nor going, is itself the Buddha." Relying on the gate of teaching, one penetrates through to contemplation; relying on the gate of contemplation, one penetrates through to wisdom; relying on the gate of wisdom, one penetrates through to principle. Principle itself serving as the gate — through to what further place does it then penetrate? Teaching, contemplation, wisdom, and every other gate all rely upon principle; and if that which relies is itself a gate, how could that which is relied upon fail to be a gate as well? Though it penetrates through to nothing further, its penetration is, in the end, everywhere complete — this is the sublime gate itself. Setting these three other gates aside for now, only the gate of teaching shall be discussed here. The Tripiṭaka teaching's own four gates first break through views, and afterward break through habituated thought, or break through both together; the shared teaching's own four gates likewise first break through views, and afterward break through habituated thought, or break through both together — but these break through only the four abodes of affliction, and cannot be said to be "comprehensive." The distinct teaching's own four gates break through the five abodes of affliction in sequence — this, then, is comprehensive vertically but not comprehensive horizontally, and neither of these is what is used here. Here, the inconceivable teaching holds that a single object is every object, a single mind is every mind; every dharma, both horizontally and vertically, converges entirely upon the mind — and since breaking through the mind breaks through everything at once, this is exactly why it is named "comprehensive." The remaining gates, which break through only incompletely, need not be further discussed. The perfect teaching's own four gates are, every one, able to break through comprehensively — namely, the gate of existence, the gate of nonexistence, the gate of both existence and nonexistence, and the gate of neither existence nor nonexistence. Here, the latter three gates shall be set aside for now, and only the gate of emptiness, the gate of the unborn, shall be relied upon. The gate of the unborn is able to carry calming-and-contemplation through to both cause and fruit; and is, further, able to reveal the unborn itself, causing this gate to shine forth in glory. Why is this? Calming-and-contemplation is the practice; the gate of the unborn is the teaching. Relying on the teaching to cultivate practice, one is carried through to the forbearance of the unborn dharma, the stage of cause fully complete. In the Vimalakīrti Sūtra, the thirty-two bodhisattvas each speak of entering the gate of non-duality — every one of them, a bodhisattva entering a stage by way of a gate, with the unborn taken as foremost among them. The Larger Sūtra clarifies the gate of the letter a, meaning precisely that "every dharma is, from the very outset, unborn" — this proves that the gate of the unborn carries calming-and-contemplation through to the cause, and the sense of this may be clearly seen. As for calming-and-contemplation causing the gate of the unborn to shine forth in glory: the Dharma does not reveal itself — its propagation depends upon the person. When a person is able to carry out the practice, the gate of the Dharma shines forth in glory, causing the unborn teaching to move without obstruction, in every direction, reaching wherever it touches — and only thus does the very sense of "gate" come to be fulfilled. It is like the way people of the world pass in and out through a gate — where there are people, and rank, the gate itself becomes glorious and renowned. The comparison being thus, what it compares to may be understood.

門通果者，《大經》云：「般涅言『不』，槃者言『生』；不生之義，名『大涅槃』。」又云：「定慧二法能大利益，乃至菩提。」《大品》云：「無生法無來無去，無生法即是佛。」《法華》云：「佛自住大乘，如其所得法，定慧力莊嚴，以此度眾生。」且引三經，果義明矣。止觀能顯果者，果不自顯，由行故果滿；果滿故，一切皆滿。巍巍堂堂如星中月，照十寶山，影臨四海。果亦如是，無上無上，功高十地，汲引四機。《金光明》中佛禮骨塔，即其義也。

As for the gate reaching the fruit: the Nirvāṇa Sūtra says, "'Pari' means 'not'; 'nirvāṇa' means 'born' — the sense of 'not born' is named 'the great nirvāṇa.'" It also says, "The two dharmas of concentration and wisdom are able to bring great benefit, all the way through to bodhi itself." The Larger Sūtra says, "The unborn dharma neither comes nor goes; the unborn dharma is itself the Buddha." The Lotus Sūtra says, "The Buddha himself abides in the Mahāyāna; according to the dharma he himself has attained, adorned with the power of concentration and wisdom, he thereby delivers sentient beings by this very means." Citing these three sūtras alone, the sense of the fruit is already made clear. As for calming-and-contemplation being able to reveal the fruit: the fruit does not reveal itself — through practice, the fruit is brought to fullness; and once the fruit is full, everything is thereby full as well. Towering and majestic, like the moon among the stars, illuminating the ten jeweled mountains, its reflection reaching the four seas — the fruit, too, is exactly the same: unsurpassed, unsurpassed again, its merit rising higher than the ten grounds themselves, drawing in beings of all four capacities. In the Sūtra of Golden Light, where the Buddha himself bows before a relic-stūpa, this is exactly that same sense.

Reading note: Mode Four opens with a question the treatise has raised before in different forms — if the Dharma-nature is already "neither broken nor unbroken," why does anything need breaking at all? — and answers it the same way each time: the asymmetry is not in reality but in sentient beings, who are "for the most part, inverted, and only rarely free of inversion." The choice to work through only one of the perfect teaching's four gates (existence, nonexistence, both, neither), and within that, only the gate of the unborn, is itself a demonstration of the "one gate is every gate" principle already established in the three-thousand-in-a-single-thought passage: a single gate, followed all the way through, is expected to carry the practitioner to both cause and fruit, exactly as a single mesh of a net catches the bird.

The Gate of the Unborn, Gathering Horizontally: The Nirvāṇa Sūtra's Tetralemma 破法遍 · 無生門橫攝 · 四句

Chinese Source

無生教門，竪攝因果，其義已彰；橫攝之意，今當說。《大品》云：「若聞無生門，則解一切義。初阿字攝四十一字，四十一字攝阿字，中間亦然。」橫竪備攝，其文如此。此意難見，更引《佛藏》示其相，次引《涅槃》釋其義，後說無生門破法遍。《佛藏》云：「劫火起時，菩薩一唾，火即滅；一吹，世界即成。」非是先滅後成，秖一唾中，即滅即成。彼經明外用，內合無生門，即破遍，即立遍，破立不須二念。若內無是德，則外無大用。寄外顯內，其相如是。須識觀心者，眾生一期將訖，即是劫盡；三毒、三災，火為語端。以止止之，如唾滅；以觀觀之，如吹成云云。《大經》釋義者，「不聞聞」一句有種種義。初云：「不生生、不生不生、生不生、生生。」按：此四句說無生門，攝自行因果、化他能所等法皆遍。不生生者：「安住世諦，初出胎時，名不生生。」今解：「世諦」者，無明共法性出生一切，隔歷分別，故名「世諦」。「安住」者，以止觀安於世諦，即是不可思議境；觀行位成，故名「安住」。以安住故，名「託聖胎」。初開佛知見，得無生忍，名「出聖胎」。不見無明世諦，故言「不生」。獲佛知佛見，故名為「生」。《論》云：「諸法不生而般若生。」即其義也。此說自行無生忍位，因義成也。經釋「不生不生」者：「不生不生，名大涅槃。」生相盡故，修道得故。今解：果由因剋，故言「修道得故」。斷德已圓，無明不生；智德已圓，般若不生，故言「不生不生」。此說自行寂滅忍，果義成也。因果既圓，即如《佛藏》所明一吹唾即滅即立，是其義焉。

Ru-Yi Translation

The unborn teaching's own gateway, gathering in cause and fruit vertically, has already had its sense made plain; the sense of gathering horizontally shall now be explained. The Larger Sūtra says: "Should one hear the gate of the unborn, one thereby understands every sense whatsoever. The very first letter a gathers in all forty-one letters, and the forty-one letters gather in the letter a — and it is exactly the same for every letter in between." Gathering in both horizontally and vertically in full — the text says exactly this. This sense is difficult to see, and so the Sūtra of the Buddha-Treasury shall first be cited to show its mark, then the Nirvāṇa Sūtra to clarify its meaning, and only afterward shall the gate of the unborn's own comprehensive breaking-through be explained. The Sūtra of the Buddha-Treasury says: "When the fire of the kalpa's end arises, a single spit from the bodhisattva extinguishes it at once; a single breath from him, and the world is formed once more." This is not extinguishing first and forming afterward — within that single spit alone, extinguishing and forming happen at once. That sūtra clarifies the outward function, while inwardly according with the gate of the unborn: breaking through comprehensively and establishing comprehensively at once, breaking and establishing requiring no second thought between them. Were there no such virtue within, there could be no such great function shown without — the outward is borrowed to reveal the inward, and this is exactly its own mark. One who wishes to recognize this through contemplating the mind should know: as a sentient being's own single lifetime draws to its close, this is itself the ending of a kalpa; the three poisons and the three calamities, fire foremost among the words for them. Calming it with calming is like that very spit extinguishing the fire; contemplating it with contemplation is like that very breath forming the world anew.

As for the Nirvāṇa Sūtra's own exegesis of this sense: the single phrase "hearing without hearing" carries manifold senses within it. It first says: "not-born-born, not-born-not-born, born-not-born, born-born." Examining this: these four propositions expound the gate of the unborn, gathering in, comprehensively, both the cause and fruit of one's own practice and the agent and object of transforming others alike. "Not-born-born" means: "settled within the conventional truth, at the very moment of first emerging from the womb, this is named not-born-born." Here it is explained: "conventional truth" means ignorance, together with the Dharma-nature, giving rise to everything, distinguished and differentiated one from another — hence it is named the "conventional truth." "Settled" means using calming-and-contemplation to settle within the conventional truth, which is itself already the inconceivable object; the stage of contemplative practice being accomplished, this is hence named "settled." On account of being thus settled, this is named "entrusted to the sage's own embryo." The first opening of the buddha's own knowledge and vision, attaining the forbearance of the unborn, is named "emerging from the sage's own embryo." Not seeing the conventional truth of ignorance is hence called "unborn"; obtaining the buddha's own knowledge and vision is hence named "born." The Treatise says: "Every dharma is unborn, and yet prajñā is born" — this is exactly that same sense. This explains the stage of one's own practice reaching the forbearance of the unborn, the sense of the cause thereby accomplished. The sūtra's exegesis of "not-born-not-born" says: "Not-born-not-born is named the great nirvāṇa. The mark of birth is exhausted entirely, attained through the cultivation of the path." Here it is explained: the fruit is brought about through the cause, hence it is said, "attained through the cultivation of the path." The virtue of severance already perfected, ignorance is no longer born; the virtue of wisdom already perfected, prajñā, too, is no longer born — hence it is said, "not-born-not-born." This explains one's own practice reaching the forbearance of quiescent extinction, the sense of the fruit thereby accomplished. Cause and fruit both already perfected, this is exactly what the Sūtra of the Buddha-Treasury clarifies as that single breath and spit, extinguishing and establishing at once — this is exactly that same sense.

經釋「生不生」者：「世諦死時名生不生。」今解：「世諦」者，無明是其根本，既破無明故言「世死」；世死故名「生不生」。此釋初句。初句上緣於理，智德成故，言「不生生」。此句下破於惑，斷德成故，言「生不生」。不生名雖同，事理大異。初句詺智慧開發為「生」；此句詺結業起動為「生」。生名雖同，而縛脫大異，莫迷名惑旨，須精識之！須精識之！初句如唾中吹，此句如吹中唾；唾吹一時，不可前後也。經重釋此句云：「四住菩薩名生不生，生自在故。」今解：先「生不生」，說自行之惑滅；重釋「生不生」，明化道之興。何者？菩薩斷四住時，破結業生，即能自在生，況斷五住耶？以劣顯勝，彌彰化道；二乘斷惑沈空，不能如此，故標菩薩也。惑滅顯唾；化興顯吹也。經釋「生生」者：「一切有漏念念生故。」今解：此句明化用之所耳。菩薩何意不生而生？良由一切有漏眾生相續不斷，是故菩薩而起大悲，示自在生而度脫之。是為無生門攝自行因果、化他能所，皆悉具足矣。

The sūtra's exegesis of "born-not-born" says: "At the very moment the conventional truth dies, this is named born-not-born." Here it is explained: "conventional truth" means that ignorance is its very root; ignorance having already been broken through, this is hence called "the world dying"; and the world having died, this is hence named "born-not-born." This explains the first proposition. The first proposition, above, took principle as its object, the virtue of wisdom thereby accomplished, and so is said to be "not-born-born." This proposition, below, breaks through affliction, the virtue of severance thereby accomplished, and so is said to be "born-not-born." Though the word "born" is the same in name, the actual matter and the underlying principle differ greatly between the two. The first proposition names the opening and unfolding of wisdom as "born"; this proposition names the arising and stirring of karmic bondage as "born." Though the name "born" is the same, bondage and liberation differ enormously between the two — do not let confusion over the name obscure the actual sense; this must be precisely discerned! This must be precisely discerned! The first proposition is like the breath within the spit; this proposition is like the spit within the breath — spit and breath happen at a single instant, and cannot be placed one before the other. The sūtra further re-explains this same proposition, saying: "The bodhisattva who has severed the four abodes of affliction is named born-not-born, since birth is now self-mastered." Here it is explained: the earlier "born-not-born" explained the extinguishing of one's own affliction through practice; this further re-explanation of "born-not-born" clarifies the flourishing of the way of transforming others. Why is this? When the bodhisattva severs the four abodes of affliction, breaking through the very birth caused by karmic bondage, they are thereby already able to be born with self-mastery — how much more, then, once the fifth abode as well is severed? Using the lesser to reveal the greater, the way of transformation is thereby made all the more evident; the two lesser vehicles, severing affliction only to sink into emptiness, are unable to do this, and this is exactly why the bodhisattva alone is singled out here. The extinguishing of affliction reveals the spit; the flourishing of transformation reveals the breath. The sūtra's exegesis of "born-born" says: "Because every contaminated dharma is born, thought after thought." Here it is explained: this proposition clarifies precisely the place where the very function of transformation operates. Why does the bodhisattva, though unborn, still take birth? Simply because every contaminated sentient being continues on, uninterrupted, and so the bodhisattva gives rise to great compassion on this very account, manifesting a birth of self-mastery in order to deliver them. This, then, is the gate of the unborn gathering in, comprehensively, both the cause and fruit of one's own practice and the agent and object of transforming others — every one of these complete within it.

Reading note: Zhiyi's own warning — "do not let confusion over the name obscure the actual sense; this must be precisely discerned!" repeated twice for emphasis — flags exactly the trap this passage is built to catch: the word "born" (生) recurs across all four propositions, but names something different each time — wisdom unfolding in the first, karmic bondage stirring in the second, self-mastered rebirth in the third, ordinary contaminated rebirth in the fourth. The bodhisattva who has severed only the first four of five proclivities, "using the lesser to reveal the greater," is a small but characteristic move: even an incomplete attainment is cited by name specifically because it makes the fuller claim about a buddha's own self-mastery easier to grasp by contrast.

The Six Propositions: Breaking Through Both Conceivable and Inconceivable Affliction 破法遍 · 六句

Chinese Source

四住菩薩者，《地持》云：「從初發心住至十地，束為六住：一、種性住，二、解行住，三、淨心住，四、行道迹住，五、決定住，六、究竟住。」「種性住」者，若人無有種性，雖生善道，數退數進，不得在菩薩六人數中；若種性處成就，無有退失，數數增進，得是一人也。「解行人」是初地方便。「淨心住」是入初地，得出世間心，離凡夫我相障，故名「淨心住」。「行道迹住」者，從二地至七地，住修道也。「決定住」者，八地、九地也；已得報行，不還不退，故名「決定」。「究竟住」者，第十地，學行窮滿，故言「究竟住」也。經稱「四住名生不生」者，正是行道迹住。從二地止，正是入假化他之位，處處現生而非實生，將別顯圓。初出胎時即能利他，化生自在，於圓義亦應無失。

Ru-Yi Translation

As for the "bodhisattva of the four abodes": the Yogācārabhūmi says, "From the very first abiding of giving rise to the resolve up through the ten grounds, these are gathered together into six abidings: first, the abiding of the seed-nature; second, the abiding of understanding and practice; third, the abiding of the purified mind; fourth, the abiding of following the traces of the path; fifth, the abiding of certainty; sixth, the abiding of ultimacy." As for "the abiding of the seed-nature": should a person lack this seed-nature, though born into a wholesome destiny, they repeatedly regress and repeatedly advance, and cannot be counted among the bodhisattva's own six kinds of person; but should the place of this seed-nature already be accomplished, with no regression or loss at all, advancing further and further, this is counted as the first of the six kinds of person. "The person of understanding and practice" is at the expedient stage prior to the first ground. "The abiding of the purified mind" is entering the first ground itself, attaining the world-transcending mind, free of the ordinary being's own obstruction of the mark of self — hence it is named "the abiding of the purified mind." "The abiding of following the traces of the path" spans from the second ground through the seventh, abiding in the cultivation of the path. "The abiding of certainty" is the eighth and ninth grounds; the practice of reward already attained, never turning back, never regressing, hence it is named "certainty." "The abiding of ultimacy" is the tenth ground, the study and practice of it brought to their utmost fullness, hence it is called "the abiding of ultimacy." Where the sūtra names "the bodhisattva of the four abodes" as "born-not-born," this refers precisely to the abiding of following the traces of the path. From the second ground onward, this is precisely the stage of entering the provisional to transform others, manifesting birth in place after place and yet without any real birth at all — using the distinct teaching to reveal the perfect. From the very first moment of emerging from the womb, one is already able to benefit others, transforming and giving birth with complete self-mastery — and with regard to the perfect teaching's own sense, this too ought to suffer no loss at all.

經又六句：「不生生亦不可說，生生亦不可說，生不生亦不可說，不生不生亦不可說，生亦不可說，不生亦不可說。」按：此六句明無生門破法遍。若破思議惑，用前四句；若破不思議惑，用後二句。何者？思議惑雖多，不出界內、外。界外惑附體生，故言「不生生」；界內惑是枝末，故言「生生」。此惑紛綸，並是所化之境，為此境故，施自在生。所化既不可得，何處有能化？能所俱亡，是故「不生生、生生俱不可說」。若破思議解，此解雖多，不出界內、外。界內解，止遣分段，故言「生不生」；界外解，雙遣分段、變易，故言「不生不生」。此解淺深，故有種種自行因果。理尚非一，寧有種種？今遍唾破，故言「生不生不可說，不生不生亦不可說」。若破不可思議惑者，秖是無明。無明故生，生故無明；無明不可得，生亦不可得。今皆唾破，故言「生不可得」。若破不思議解者，秖是圓解。圓解始終，判出因果。理不遍圓亦非始終，那有因果？今皆唾破，故言「不生不可得」。將彼經意，釋無生門破法遍者，其義分明。

The sūtra, further, gives six propositions: "not-born-born, too, cannot be spoken; born-born, too, cannot be spoken; born-not-born, too, cannot be spoken; not-born-not-born, too, cannot be spoken; born cannot be spoken; not-born cannot be spoken." Examining this: these six propositions clarify the gate of the unborn's own comprehensive breaking-through. To break through conceivable affliction, the first four propositions are used; to break through inconceivable affliction, the latter two are used. Why is this? Conceivable affliction, though manifold, never exceeds the bounds of within and beyond the three realms. Affliction beyond the three realms arises attached to the very substance of things, hence it is called "not-born-born"; affliction within the three realms is a mere branch and twig, hence it is called "born-born." This affliction, tangled and confused, is, in every case, the very object of what is to be transformed, and it is precisely for the sake of this object that a birth of self-mastery is bestowed. Since what is to be transformed cannot ultimately be found, where, then, could there be any agent of transformation either? Agent and object both vanish together, and this is exactly why "not-born-born and born-born alike cannot be spoken." To break through conceivable understanding: though this understanding, too, is manifold, it never exceeds the bounds of within and beyond the three realms. Understanding within the three realms merely dispels segmented birth-and-death, hence it is called "born-not-born"; understanding beyond the three realms dispels both segmented and mutable birth-and-death together, hence it is called "not-born-not-born." This understanding differs in shallowness and depth, and so manifold causes and fruits of one's own practice result from it. Yet principle itself is never anything but singular — how, then, could there truly be manifold understanding at all? Here, this is comprehensively broken through with a single spit, hence it is said, "born-not-born cannot be spoken; not-born-not-born, too, cannot be spoken." To break through inconceivable affliction: this is simply ignorance itself. On account of ignorance, birth occurs; on account of birth, ignorance persists — ignorance cannot ultimately be found, and birth, too, cannot ultimately be found. Here, both are broken through with a single spit, hence it is said, "born cannot be found." To break through inconceivable understanding: this is simply the perfect teaching's own understanding. The perfect teaching's own understanding, from beginning to end, distinguishes cause from fruit — yet principle itself neither fails to be perfect nor has any beginning or end at all, so how, then, could there truly be any cause or fruit? Here, both are broken through with a single spit, hence it is said, "not-born cannot be found." Taking up that sūtra's own intent to explain the gate of the unborn's own comprehensive breaking-through — the sense of this is now made plain.

Reading note: Notice the escalation across these two rounds of tetralemma: the first four propositions distinguish conceivable affliction from conceivable understanding, still working within categories a practitioner can track and progress through by stages (the six abidings, the ten grounds); the final two propositions collapse even that distinction, breaking through inconceivable affliction and inconceivable understanding "with a single spit" each, since ignorance and birth turn out to define each other circularly, and the perfect teaching's own understanding "neither fails to be perfect nor has any beginning or end at all." The passage stages its own argument for why sudden, comprehensive breaking-through is ultimately required, not merely stylistically preferred over gradual analysis.

Not Speaking a Single Word, and the Twelve Links Establishing Everything 破法遍 · 楞伽不說一字 · 十因緣法

Chinese Source

又《楞伽》云：「我從得道夜至涅槃夜不說一字。」佛因二法作如此說，謂：緣自法及本住法。「自法」者，彼如來所得，我亦得之；無增無減，離言說、妄想、文字、二趣。釋曰：「緣自法」是證聖真諦實性也。「離言說、妄想」者，不可思議也。「離文字」者，離假名也。「離二趣」者，離說、所說，想、所想，名、所名也。「本住法」者，謂古先聖道，法界常住。如道趣城，道為人行，非行者作道；城由道至，非至者作城。經曰：「士夫見平坦道，即隨入城，受如意樂。我及先佛，法界常住，亦復如是。是故二夜不說一字。」當知二法決定，非口言、分別所能變異。本法者，如理也；自法者，證實。此義與《大經》四不可說意同。生生不可說者，本法不可說也；生隨順緣生，本法不可說也。生不生不可說者，即自斷法不可說也。不生生不可說者，即自智法不可說也。不生不生不可說者，即是究竟自證法不可說也。後二句，一結生不可說，結本法不可說也；一句結不生不可說，結自證法不可說也。

Ru-Yi Translation

The Laṅkāvatāra Sūtra, further, says: "From the very night I attained the path to the very night of my parinirvāṇa, I have not spoken a single word." The Buddha, on account of two dharmas, spoke in this way: namely, the dharma taken as his own object, and the dharma that abides originally. As for "his own dharma": what the Tathāgata himself attained, I too have attained — neither increasing nor decreasing, free of verbal expression, of deluded thought, of written word, and of the twofold movement. This may be explained: "taking his own dharma as object" is realizing the sage's own real truth, the true nature of reality itself. "Free of verbal expression, of deluded thought" is the inconceivable itself. "Free of written word" is being free of the provisional name. "Free of the twofold movement" is being free of speaking and what is spoken, of conceiving and what is conceived, of naming and what is named. As for "the dharma that abides originally": this refers to the sagely path of old, the dharma-realm abiding forever. It is like a road leading to a city — the road exists for a person to travel upon, and it is not the traveler who creates the road; the city is reached by means of the road, and it is not the one who arrives who creates the city. The sūtra says: "A person seeing the level road follows it and enters the city, receiving every joy just as they wish. I myself, and every buddha before me, alike find the dharma-realm abiding forever, exactly the same way. This is exactly why, across those two nights, not a single word was spoken." One should know that these two dharmas are fixed and certain, not something that speech or discrimination could ever alter. "The original dharma" is suchness itself; "his own dharma" is the realization of the real. This same sense accords with the four propositions of the Nirvāṇa Sūtra that "cannot be spoken." "Born-born cannot be spoken" is the original dharma that cannot be spoken; birth following and according with conditions is the original dharma that cannot be spoken. "Born-not-born cannot be spoken" is precisely the dharma of one's own severance that cannot be spoken. "Not-born-born cannot be spoken" is precisely the dharma of one's own wisdom that cannot be spoken. "Not-born-not-born cannot be spoken" is precisely the dharma of ultimate self-realization that cannot be spoken. Of the latter two propositions given earlier, one concludes "born cannot be spoken," concluding that the original dharma cannot be spoken; the other concludes "not-born cannot be spoken," concluding that the dharma of self-realization cannot be spoken.

《大經》云「十因緣法為生作因，亦可得說」者，今解：此即無生門遍立之義；亦如《佛藏》所明一吹遍成也。「十因緣」者，從無明支乃至有支，立諸法也。「立」有三義：一、立眾生，二、立機緣，三、立聲教。「立眾生」者，過去二因，現在五果，更互因緣而立五陰，假名眾生也。「立根機」者，過去或修行析行、體行、漸行、頓行，以行為業，無明潤之，致今五果。於此陰果更起本習，或起析愛取有，或起體愛取有，或起漸愛取有，或起頓愛取有。取有起故，得為機緣也。「立聲教」者，析愛取有起故，感三藏教，是為生生不可說；十因緣法為生生作因，亦可得說，說生生也。體愛取有，感於通教，是為生不生不可說；十因緣法為生不生作因，亦可得說，說生不生也。漸愛取有，感於別教，是為不生生不可說；十因緣法為不生生作因，亦可得說，說不生生也。頓愛取有，感於圓教，是為不生不生不可說；十因緣法為不生不生作因，亦可得說，說不生不生也。眾生若立，一切惑法因果立，一切所化立；機、教若立，一切解行因果立，一切能化立。是為無生門一立一切立。故《大品》云：「若聞阿字門，則解一切義。」《佛藏》云：「一吹一切悉成。」此之謂也。

The Nirvāṇa Sūtra says, "The dharma of the twelve links of dependent origination serves as the cause for birth, and this too may indeed be spoken of." Here it is explained: this is precisely the sense of the gate of the unborn establishing comprehensively — exactly as the Sūtra of the Buddha-Treasury clarifies with its single breath forming everything comprehensively. As for "the twelve links": from the link of ignorance through to the link of existence, every dharma is thereby established. "Establishing" has three senses: first, establishing sentient beings; second, establishing capacity and condition; third, establishing verbal teaching. As for "establishing sentient beings": the two causes of the past and the five fruits of the present, mutually serving as cause and condition for one another, establish the five aggregates, provisionally named "sentient being." As for "establishing capacity": in the past, one may have cultivated the practice of analysis, the practice of embodying, the practice of gradual progress, or the practice of sudden attainment, taking this practice as karma, moistened by ignorance, and thereby bringing about the present five fruits. Upon this fruit of the aggregates, the original habituation arises once more, giving rise to craving and grasping toward existence through analysis, or through embodying, or through gradual progress, or through sudden attainment. Since grasping toward existence has thus arisen, this becomes the very capacity and condition. As for "establishing verbal teaching": since craving and grasping toward existence through analysis has arisen, this attracts the Tripiṭaka teaching — this is "born-born cannot be spoken"; the dharma of the twelve links serving as the cause of "born-born" may indeed be spoken of, and speaking of it is speaking of "born-born." Craving and grasping toward existence through embodying attracts the shared teaching — this is "born-not-born cannot be spoken"; the dharma of the twelve links serving as the cause of "born-not-born" may indeed be spoken of, and speaking of it is speaking of "born-not-born." Craving and grasping toward existence through gradual progress attracts the distinct teaching — this is "not-born-born cannot be spoken"; the dharma of the twelve links serving as the cause of "not-born-born" may indeed be spoken of, and speaking of it is speaking of "not-born-born." Craving and grasping toward existence through sudden attainment attracts the perfect teaching — this is "not-born-not-born cannot be spoken"; the dharma of the twelve links serving as the cause of "not-born-not-born" may indeed be spoken of, and speaking of it is speaking of "not-born-not-born." Should sentient beings be established, then every dharma of affliction, its cause and its fruit, is thereby established, and every object of transformation is thereby established as well; should capacity and teaching be established, then every cause and fruit of understanding and practice is thereby established, and every agent of transformation is thereby established as well. This, then, is the gate of the unborn: establishing one thing establishes everything. Hence the Larger Sūtra says: "Should one hear the gate of the letter a, one thereby understands every sense whatsoever." The Sūtra of the Buddha-Treasury says: "A single breath, and everything is thereby formed comprehensively." This is exactly what is meant here.

Reading note: The Laṅkāvatāra's own claim that the Buddha "did not speak a single word" across his entire teaching career is not mysticism for its own sake — Zhiyi reads it as making the same structural point as everything else in Mode Four: the road to the city is not created by the traveler, and the Dharma-realm's own eternal abiding is not created by the Buddha's speech either, only pointed to. The twelve links of dependent origination, elsewhere the treatise's paradigm case of samsaric bondage, are here read as establishing the Four Teachings scheme itself — analysis, embodying, gradual, and sudden practice each attracting its own matching teaching — which quietly folds fascicle 3's entire doctrinal architecture back into this same single chain of causation.

Thirty-Two Gates, and the Moon That Neither Waxes Nor Wanes 破法遍 · 三十二門 · 月喻

Chinese Source

如《地持》四種成熟，謂：聲聞種性、緣覺種性、佛種性、菩薩種性。無此四性，以善趣熟之。佛種性即此圓機，菩薩種性即此別機。彼文云：「菩薩種子，有佛、無佛，堪能次第斷煩惱障及智障。」豈非別機？聲聞種性當開之：別異善根，即三藏機；退大取小種性，即通機。彼四成熟，即此四種機緣義也。問：上六句是無生門，一破一切破；十因緣法是無生門，一立一切立。上四句是無生門，亦破亦立；亦應有第四句，非破非立不？答：《大經》十九卷初云：「十事功德不可思議，聞者驚怪，非難非易、非內非外、非相非非相、非方非圓、非尖非斜等。」即是第四句「非破非立」之文義。

Ru-Yi Translation

It is like the Yogācārabhūmi's own four kinds of ripening capacity: the śrāvaka's own seed-nature, the pratyekabuddha's own seed-nature, the buddha's own seed-nature, and the bodhisattva's own seed-nature. Lacking these four natures, one is ripened instead through mere wholesome destinies. The buddha's own seed-nature is precisely this perfect teaching's own capacity; the bodhisattva's own seed-nature is precisely this distinct teaching's own capacity. That text says: "The bodhisattva's own seed, whether a buddha has appeared in the world or not, is able to sever the obstruction of affliction and the obstruction of the intellect in sequence" — is this not exactly the distinct teaching's own capacity? The śrāvaka's own seed-nature should further be opened out: a wholesome root distinct and different from others is exactly the Tripiṭaka teaching's own capacity; a seed-nature that retreats from the great vehicle and takes up the small is exactly the shared teaching's own capacity. Those four kinds of ripening are exactly this same sense of the four kinds of capacity and condition. Question: the six propositions given above are the gate of the unborn, breaking one thing breaking everything; the twelve links of dependent origination are the gate of the unborn, establishing one thing establishing everything. The four propositions given even before that are the gate of the unborn, both breaking and establishing at once — should there not also be a fourth proposition, neither breaking nor establishing? Answer: the Nirvāṇa Sūtra, at the opening of its nineteenth fascicle, says: "The ten matters of merit are inconceivable, and the listener is astonished — neither difficult nor easy, neither within nor without, neither marked nor unmarked, neither square nor round, neither pointed nor slanted," and so on. This is exactly the textual sense of the fourth proposition, "neither breaking nor establishing."

問：若無生門攝一切法者，則無復諸門也。答：無生門亦攝諸門，諸門亦攝無生門。欲依智德義便，故言無生門。此應四句：生門、無生門、亦生亦無生門、非生非無生門。一一門各有四門，四四十六門。若依斷德義便，應有滅門、不滅門、亦滅亦不滅門、非滅非不滅門。一一門各有四門，四四十六門。合三十二門。《大經》舉十五日月光增，正喻智德；十六日月光減，正喻斷德。月無增、無減，約白論增，約黑論減。實相無智、無斷，約照論智，約寂論斷。若無生門攝一切法高極，此竪攝一切法也。若無生門攝諸法廣遍者，即無生門橫攝一切法也。問：無生門，門稱無生，其境、惑、智、斷等，悉應稱為無生，那忽言「無生生、生生、生自在故」？答：此還助顯無生門。無生忍發，故言「無生生」；明其所化，故言「生生」；明其應用，故言「生自在」。還是無生門，即唾，故言「無生」；即吹，故言「無生生」等，彌顯無生門攝法遍耳。約《大經》釋「門」義竟云云。

Question: if the gate of the unborn gathers in every dharma, would there then be no further gates left at all? Answer: the gate of the unborn also gathers in every other gate, and every other gate also gathers in the gate of the unborn. It is simply for the sake of convenience, in terms of the virtue of wisdom, that it is named "the gate of the unborn." This should form four propositions: the gate of birth, the gate of the unborn, the gate of both birth and the unborn, and the gate of neither birth nor the unborn. Each and every one of these gates, in turn, has its own further four gates, four times four making sixteen gates in all. Following the convenience of the virtue of severance, there should likewise be the gate of extinction, the gate of non-extinction, the gate of both extinction and non-extinction, and the gate of neither extinction nor non-extinction — each and every one of these, too, having its own further four gates, four times four making sixteen gates in all. Combined, this makes thirty-two gates altogether. The Nirvāṇa Sūtra cites the fifteenth day's moon, its light waxing, as a simile precisely for the virtue of wisdom; and the sixteenth day's moon, its light waning, as a simile precisely for the virtue of severance. The moon itself neither waxes nor wanes — discussed in terms of its brightness, one speaks of waxing; discussed in terms of its darkness, one speaks of waning. The true mark of reality itself has neither wisdom nor severance — discussed in terms of illumination, one speaks of wisdom; discussed in terms of quiescence, one speaks of severance. Where the gate of the unborn gathers in every dharma to its very utmost height, this is the gate of the unborn gathering in every dharma vertically; where the gate of the unborn gathers in every dharma to its broadest extent, this is the gate of the unborn gathering in every dharma horizontally. Question: the gate is named "the unborn" — its object, its affliction, its wisdom, its severance, and the rest, ought then, every one, to be named "the unborn" as well. How, then, could it suddenly speak of "the unborn born," "born-born," and "birth with self-mastery"? Answer: this, too, only further helps to reveal the gate of the unborn. The forbearance of the unborn arising is hence called "the unborn born"; clarifying what it transforms is hence called "born-born"; clarifying its own responsive function is hence called "birth with self-mastery." This remains, throughout, the gate of the unborn: as the spit, it is hence called "the unborn"; as the breath, it is hence called "the unborn born," and so on — this only further reveals how thoroughly the gate of the unborn gathers in the comprehensive breaking-through of every dharma. This concludes the explanation of the sense of "gate" in terms of the Nirvāṇa Sūtra.

Reading note: The waxing-and-waning moon closes this whole extended treatment of the "gate of the unborn" with the same move that has organized the entire fascicle: wisdom and severance, like waxing and waning, are not two different properties competing for the same reality, but two different descriptions of a single unchanging moon, read differently depending on which face is turned toward the light. Thirty-two gates sounds like proliferation, but the logic driving it is the reverse — each new gate is generated only to demonstrate that it, too, collapses back into the single gate of the unborn already chosen, "gathering in every other gate" rather than standing beside it as an alternative.

Entering Emptiness from the Provisional: The Affliction of Views 從假入空破法遍 · 見惑

Chinese Source

次、明破法遍者，為三：一、無生門，從始至終盡其源底，竪破法遍；二、歷諸法門，當門從始至終盡其源底，橫破法遍；三、橫竪不二，從始至終盡其源底，非橫非竪破法遍。竪則論高；橫則論廣。竪來入橫，無橫而不高；橫來入竪，無竪而不廣。《法華》云：「其車高廣。」橫竪不二，則非橫非竪，故云：「是法平等，無有高下。」一、無生門破法遍者，又為三：一、從假入空破法遍；二、從空入假破法遍；三、兩觀為方便，得入中道第一義諦破法遍。如此三觀實在一心，法妙難解，寄三以顯一耳。《大論》云：「三智實在一心，為向人說，令易解故，分屬三人。」《華嚴》亦有二意：宣說菩薩歷劫修行，彼為鈍根也；初發心時便成正覺，所有慧身不由他悟，彼是利根也。《法華》唯一意：「正直捨方便，但說無上道。」今欲借別顯總，舉次而論不次，故先三義解釋也。從假入空破法遍，又為三：先從見假入空，次從思假入空，後四門料簡。後見假入空，又為二：先明見假，次明空觀。見惑附體而生，還能障體。如炎依空，而動亂於空；似夢因眠，夢昏於眠。夢若不息，眠不得覺；此惑不除，體不得顯。然見則見理，見實非惑。見理時能斷此惑；從解得名，名為見惑耳。見惑有四：一、單四見，二、複四見，三、具足四見，四、無言見。

Ru-Yi Translation

Next, clarifying comprehensively breaking through every dharma, this is threefold: first, the gate of the unborn, from beginning to end, reaching all the way to its very source and depths — breaking through every dharma comprehensively, vertically; second, passing through every gate of the Dharma, each gate in turn, from beginning to end, reaching all the way to its very source and depths — breaking through every dharma comprehensively, horizontally; third, the vertical and the horizontal as not two, from beginning to end, reaching all the way to its very source and depths — breaking through every dharma comprehensively, neither horizontally nor vertically. The vertical discusses height; the horizontal discusses breadth. The vertical, entering into the horizontal, finds nothing horizontal that is not also lofty; the horizontal, entering into the vertical, finds nothing vertical that is not also broad. The Lotus Sūtra says, "This carriage is both lofty and broad." The vertical and the horizontal as not two is thus neither horizontal nor vertical — hence it is said, "This dharma is equal throughout, with nothing higher, nothing lower." First, the gate of the unborn's own comprehensive breaking-through of every dharma is, again, threefold: first, comprehensively breaking through every dharma by entering emptiness from the provisional; second, comprehensively breaking through every dharma by entering the provisional from emptiness; third, taking both contemplations as expedient means, entering the middle way, the ultimate truth, comprehensively breaking through every dharma. These three contemplations, in reality, reside within a single mind — the dharma is sublime, difficult to explain, and so three are provisionally distinguished only to reveal the one. The Treatise says: "The three kinds of wisdom, in reality, reside within a single mind; spoken of to different people, in order to make it easier to understand, they are distributed among three persons." The Avataṃsaka Sūtra, too, carries two senses: proclaiming that the bodhisattva cultivates practice across many kalpas is spoken for those of dull faculty; that at the very moment of first giving rise to the resolve, correct awakening is already accomplished, this very wisdom-body requiring no awakening from another, is spoken for those of sharp faculty. The Lotus Sūtra carries only a single sense: "Directly and without deviation, casting aside every expedient means, it teaches only the unsurpassed path." Here, we shall borrow the distinct in order to reveal the total, raising a sequence in order to discuss what is, in fact, not sequential at all — and so these three senses shall first be explained in turn. Comprehensively breaking through every dharma by entering emptiness from the provisional is, again, threefold: first, entering emptiness from the provisional by way of the affliction of views; second, entering emptiness from the provisional by way of the affliction of habituated thought; and finally, a fourfold consideration of these four gates together. The first, entering emptiness from the provisional by way of the affliction of views, is, again, twofold: first, clarifying the affliction of views itself; second, clarifying the contemplation of emptiness. The affliction of views arises attached to the very substance of things, and is thereby able to obstruct that substance in turn — like a mirage that depends on empty space, and yet disturbs and confuses that very space; like a dream that arises because of sleep, and yet darkens that very sleep. If the dream is not stilled, one cannot awaken from the sleep; if this affliction is not removed, the substance cannot be revealed. And yet seeing is itself seeing principle — seeing the real is not, in itself, affliction at all. At the very moment principle is seen, this affliction is thereby severed; it takes its name from the understanding it obstructs, and so is named "the affliction of views." The affliction of views is of four kinds: first, the single fourfold view; second, the compound fourfold view; third, the complete fourfold view; fourth, the wordless view.

「單四見」者，執有、執無、執亦有亦無、執非有非無。於一有見，復起利鈍，謂有於我，我與有俱，恒起我心，與我相應，即是「我見」；以計我故，能生「邊見」；以我、邊故，破世、出世因果，即是「邪見」；執此為道，望通涅槃，名為「戒取」；謂此為實，餘皆妄語，不受餘見，名為「見取」；是己法者「愛」；非己法故「瞋」；我解他不解生「慢」；不識有見中苦集為「癡」；猶豫不決為「疑」。如是十使歷欲界四諦：「苦」下具十；「集」下有七，除身、邊、戒取；「道」下有八，除身、邊；「滅」下有七，除身、邊、戒取；合三十二使。歷色界四諦，有二十八；無色亦爾，例除一瞋。合有八十八使。餘三見亦各具八十八使。若歷六十二見，見見各具八十八使。倒浪瀾漫，不可稱數；邪網彌密，障於體理。

As for "the single fourfold view": clinging to existence, clinging to nonexistence, clinging to both existence and nonexistence, clinging to neither existence nor nonexistence. Within the single view of existence alone, sharp and dull further arise: holding that a self exists, this self bound up together with existence, ever giving rise to the mind of "I," corresponding to this "I" — this is precisely "the view of a self"; on account of conceiving of a self, "the extreme view" is thereby able to arise; on account of the view of a self and of extremes, breaking through the causation of both the mundane and the world-transcending, this is precisely "the deviant view"; clinging to this as the path, hoping thereby to reach nirvāṇa, this is named "clinging to precept and vow"; holding this to be real, and everything else mere idle talk, refusing to accept any other view, this is named "clinging to views"; that which belongs to oneself becomes "craving"; that which does not belong to oneself becomes "aversion"; that I understand while others do not gives rise to "pride"; failing to recognize the suffering and its origin contained within the view of existence is "delusion"; and lingering in indecision is "doubt." These ten proclivities, spread across the Four Noble Truths within the desire realm: under "suffering," all ten are complete; under "origin," seven are present, excluding the view of a self, the extreme view, and clinging to precept and vow; under "the path," eight are present, excluding the view of a self and the extreme view; under "cessation," seven are present, excluding the view of a self, the extreme view, and clinging to precept and vow — combined, this makes thirty-two proclivities. Spread across the Four Noble Truths within the form realm, there are twenty-eight; and it is exactly the same within the formless realm as well, excluding, by the same pattern, aversion alone. Combined, this makes eighty-eight proclivities in all. Each of the remaining three views likewise has its own eighty-eight proclivities. Spread across the sixty-two views, each and every view has its own eighty-eight proclivities — inverted waves spreading without measure, beyond all reckoning; the net of deviance grows ever denser, obstructing the very substance of principle.

Reading note: The dream simile returns here in a sharper, more clinical register than its earlier appearance in Mode One — a view "disturbs and confuses" the very substance it depends on, exactly as a dream "darkens" the sleep it depends on, and neither can be addressed by attacking the substance directly, only by dissolving the affliction attached to it. The arithmetic that follows (ten proclivities distributed unevenly across the Four Noble Truths, eighty-eight per view, eighty-eight again for each of sixty-two views) is not merely decorative precision; it is the treatise's own way of making concrete just how "the net of deviance grows ever denser" than intuition alone would suggest — a single mistaken view of existence already implicates dozens of interlocking afflictions before any of the other views are even considered.

Compound, Complete, and Wordless Views 見惑 · 複四見 · 具足四見 · 絕言見

Chinese Source

世講者，謂：「有」是見，「無」非是見；「亦有亦無」是見，「非有非無」非是見。此語違經負心。經云：「依止此諸見，具足六十二。」如汝解者，數則欠少。《中論》破自、他性，有是自性；對有說無，無是他性。若有若無皆是性，何意無非是見？又此無既非證理之無，寧得非見？諸外道本劫本見，末劫末見，介爾計謂是事實，餘妄語；增見長非，吾我毒盛；捉頭拔髮，構造生死。如長爪雖不受一切法，而受於不受，不識苦集；佛以一責，墮二負處。高著外道尚未免見，云何底下謬謂為是？今判此並屬單四見攝也。「複四見」者，謂：有有、有無；無有、無無；亦有有無、亦無有無；非有有無、非無有無。此是複四見，於一一見具八十八使。若六十二見，見見又具八十八使、百八等，如上說。「具足四見」者，有見具四者，謂：有「有」、有「無」、有「亦有亦無」、有「非有非無」。無具四者：無「有」、無「無」、無「亦有亦無」、無「非有非無」。亦有亦無具四者：亦有亦無「有」、亦有亦無「無」、亦有亦無「亦有亦無」、亦有亦無「非有非無」。非有非無具四者：非有非無「有」、非有非無「無」、非有非無「亦有亦無」、非有非無「非有非無」。是名具足四見。一句具八十八使；如是六十二見，見見具八十八使、百八等，如前說。「絕言見」者，單四見外，一絕言見；複四句外，一絕言見；具足四句外，一絕言見。一一見皆起八十八使、六十二見、百八等，如前說。如是等，約外道法，生如是等見也。又約佛法生見者，三藏四門生四見，通教四門生四見，別教四門生四見，圓教四門生四見。又一種四門外，各有絕言見。如是一一見中，各各起八十八使、六十二見、百八等惑，如前說。

Ru-Yi Translation

There are worldly lecturers who claim: "Existence" is a view, but "nonexistence" is not a view; "both existence and nonexistence" is a view, but "neither existence nor nonexistence" is not a view. This claim contradicts the sūtras, and betrays the true sense of the mind. The sūtra says, "Relying upon these very views, there are sixty-two in full" — following this claimant's own explanation, the number would then fall short. The Middle Treatise refutes both self-nature and other-nature: existence is a self-nature; and, spoken in opposition to existence, nonexistence is an other-nature. If both existence and nonexistence are alike a "nature," why should nonexistence alone fail to be a view? And this "nonexistence," moreover, is not the nonexistence of one who has actually realized principle at all — how, then, could it possibly fail to be a view? Every heterodox path holds its own view of the primordial kalpa and its own view of the final kalpa, calculating in the slightest instant that this alone is real, and everything else mere idle talk — magnifying view, extending wrongdoing, the poison of "I" and "mine" growing ever more potent, seizing the very head, tearing out the very hair, constructing birth-and-death for oneself. Even Dīrghanakha, though he accepted no dharma whatsoever, still accepted the very fact of "not accepting," and so failed to recognize suffering and its origin — the Buddha, with a single rebuke, brought him to grief on both counts at once. Even the most exalted heterodox practitioners have still not escaped view — how, then, could those far more debased claim to be free of it through mere mistaken supposition? Here, this is all judged to belong to the single fourfold view. As for "the compound fourfold view": existence-of-existence, existence-of-nonexistence; nonexistence-of-existence, nonexistence-of-nonexistence; both-existence-and-nonexistence-of-existence, both-existence-and-nonexistence-of-nonexistence; neither-existence-nor-nonexistence-of-existence, neither-existence-nor-nonexistence-of-nonexistence. This is the compound fourfold view, and each and every one of these views is complete with its own eighty-eight proclivities. Spread across the sixty-two views, each and every view is, again, complete with its own eighty-eight proclivities, its own one hundred and eight, and so on, exactly as explained above. As for "the complete fourfold view": the view of existence complete with four means: existence-of-existence, existence-of-nonexistence, existence-of-both-existence-and-nonexistence, existence-of-neither-existence-nor-nonexistence. Nonexistence complete with four means: nonexistence-of-existence, nonexistence-of-nonexistence, nonexistence-of-both, nonexistence-of-neither. Both existence and nonexistence complete with four means: both-of-existence, both-of-nonexistence, both-of-both, both-of-neither. Neither existence nor nonexistence complete with four means: neither-of-existence, neither-of-nonexistence, neither-of-both, neither-of-neither. This is named the complete fourfold view. Each single proposition is complete with its own eighty-eight proclivities; spread across the sixty-two views in this way, each and every view is complete with its own eighty-eight proclivities, its own one hundred and eight, and so on, exactly as explained before. As for "the wordless view": beyond the single fourfold view, there is one further wordless view; beyond the compound fourfold view, there is one further wordless view; beyond the complete fourfold view, there is one further wordless view. Each and every one of these views, too, gives rise to its own eighty-eight proclivities, its own sixty-two views, its own one hundred and eight, and so on, exactly as explained before. Views of this kind, in every case, are given rise to in terms of the heterodox paths' own doctrines. And, discussed in terms of the Buddhadharma's own generation of views: the Tripiṭaka teaching's own four gates give rise to four views; the shared teaching's own four gates give rise to four views; the distinct teaching's own four gates give rise to four views; the perfect teaching's own four gates give rise to four views. And, beyond each single set of four gates, there is, in each case, a further wordless view. Within each and every one of these views, likewise, there arises its own eighty-eight proclivities, its own sixty-two views, its own one hundred and eight, and so on, exactly as explained before.

Reading note: The rebuttal of the "worldly lecturers" who exempt "nonexistence" from being a view at all is characteristic of the treatise's own precision about what counts as a genuine escape from view versus a merely disguised instance of it: Dīrghanakha, cited by name, is the classic case — refusing to accept any position whatsoever, he still, unavoidably, accepted the position of not accepting, and the Buddha caught him "on both counts at once." What might look like scholastic overkill in cataloguing single, compound, complete, and wordless views, each generating its own eighty-eight afflictions across the Tripiṭaka, shared, distinct, and perfect teachings alike, is actually a closing of every possible exit: there is no fifth position, however subtle or however "beyond words," that escapes being itself a further view.

The Threefold Provisional: Composite Arising, Continuity, Mutual Relativity 三假 · 因成假 · 相續假 · 相待假

Chinese Source

復次，見惑非但隨解得名，亦當體受稱，稱之為假。假者，虛妄顛倒，名之假耳。例前亦應言：單四假、複四假、具足四假，一一各有絕言之假。依於佛法復有十六假，一一如前說。又於一一假中復有三假，謂：因成假、相續假、相待假。法塵對意根生一念心起，即因成假；前念後念次第不斷，即相續假；待餘無心，知有此心，即相待假。上「因成」約外塵、內根；「相續」但約內根；「相待」竪待滅無之無，又橫待三無為之無心也。開善云：「因兼二假，或亦過之。」明第三假起時，因上兩假，故言「因兼」；上假未除，後假復起，故言「過之」。此就心明三假也。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, the affliction of views is not only named according to the understanding it obstructs — it should also be named according to its own very substance, called "the provisional." "Provisional" means false, deceptive, and inverted — this alone is why it is named "provisional." Following the same pattern as before, one should likewise speak of the single fourfold provisional, the compound fourfold provisional, and the complete fourfold provisional, each with its own further wordless provisional. In terms of the Buddhadharma, there are, further, sixteen kinds of the provisional, each explained exactly as before. And within each and every one of these provisional constructs, there are, further, three kinds of the provisional: the provisional of composite arising, the provisional of continuity, and the provisional of mutual relativity. A dharma-object meeting the mental faculty, giving rise to a single thought of mind, is the provisional of composite arising; the preceding thought and the following thought continuing on in sequence, unbroken, is the provisional of continuity; depending on some further absence of mind in order to know that this present mind exists is the provisional of mutual relativity. Of these, "composite arising" concerns the outward object and the inward faculty together; "continuity" concerns the inward faculty alone; "mutual relativity" depends, vertically, on the very absence that comes with cessation, and, horizontally, on the mindless state of the three kinds of the unconditioned. Kaishan says: "The third provisional carries within it both of the first two, and may even go beyond them." This clarifies that, at the moment the third provisional arises, it depends upon the first two already present — hence it is said to "carry within it"; and the earlier provisional constructs have not yet been removed when this later one arises in turn — hence it is said to "go beyond them." This explains the three provisional constructs in terms of the mind.

又約色明三假，先世行業託生父母，得有此身，即因成假；從胎相續，迄乎皓首，即相續假；以身待不身，即相待假。又約依報亦具三假。如四微成柱；時節改變相續不斷；此柱待不柱，長、短、大、小等也。此是三藏經中隨事三假，委釋如論師。但此名通用，不獨在小乘，大乘亦名三假。附無明起，如幻如化，但有名字，實不可得。鏡中能成之四微尚不可得，況所成之幻柱？柱尚不可得，況歷時節「相續」，以幻化長短「相待」，寧復可得？舉易況難而明十喻。「即色是空，非色滅空。」即此義也。是名大乘隨理三假。又《釋論》明三種有：相待有、假名有、法有。「相待有」者，長因短有，短亦因長；此、彼亦爾。物東則以此為西，在西則東；一物未異，而有東西之別。有名無實，是為相待有。「假名有」者，如酪色、香、味、觸，四事因緣和合，故假名為酪。雖有，不同因緣之有；雖無，不如兔角、龜毛之無。但以因緣和合故有，假名為酪。又如極微色、香、味、觸，故有毛分，毛分故有毳，毳故有㲲，㲲故有衣，是為假名有。「法有」者，即是色、香、味、觸，四微和合，故名法有。

Furthermore, discussed in terms of form, the three provisional constructs are as follows: a former life's own karmic practice, entrusted to father and mother, gives rise to this present body — this is the provisional of composite arising; continuing on from the womb all the way to white-haired old age — this is the provisional of continuity; the body depending on what is not the body — this is the provisional of mutual relativity. Discussed, further, in terms of the dependent retribution of one's own surroundings, the same three provisional constructs are likewise complete: as when the four subtle elements form a pillar; as the seasons change while continuity remains unbroken; as this pillar depends on what is not a pillar, on long or short, large or small, and the rest. This is the threefold provisional applied to phenomenal matters as given in the Tripiṭaka's own sūtras, explained in detail by the treatise-masters. Yet this same term is used generally, not confined to the Hīnayāna alone — the Mahāyāna, too, names its own threefold provisional. Attached to and arising from ignorance, like an illusion, like a phantom, having only a name, in reality unfindable at all: even the four subtle elements that compose the mirror's own reflection cannot ultimately be found — how much less, then, the illusory pillar they compose? If even the pillar itself cannot be found, how much less, then, its own "continuity" traced across the seasons, or its own "mutual relativity" of illusory length and shortness? Citing what is easy in order to illustrate what is difficult, this clarifies the ten similes. "Form is itself already emptiness; it is not that form is extinguished into emptiness" — this is exactly that same sense. This is named the Mahāyāna's own threefold provisional, discussed in terms of principle. The Treatise, further, clarifies three kinds of existence: relative existence, provisional-name existence, and dharma-existence. As for "relative existence": long exists because of short, and short, too, exists because of long; and it is exactly the same for this and that. Of a single object, moving eastward makes it "west," while being in the west makes it "east" — the object itself has not changed at all, and yet there is a distinction between "east" and "west." Having the name without the substance — this is relative existence. As for "provisional-name existence": as with the color, scent, flavor, and texture of yogurt — these four matters, coming together through causes and conditions, are provisionally named "yogurt." Though it exists, it does not exist in the same way that the causes and conditions themselves exist; though it does not exist, it is not nonexistent the way a rabbit's horn or a turtle's fur is nonexistent — it exists only because causes and conditions have come together, and this is provisionally named "yogurt." Further, as with the most minute color, scent, flavor, and texture: on their account there is fine hair; on account of the fine hair there is down; on account of the down there is felt; on account of the felt there is a garment — this is provisional-name existence. As for "dharma-existence": this is precisely color, scent, flavor, and texture, the four subtle elements combined together, hence named "dharma-existence."

《論》又云「三假施設」，與三假云何？答：別義不論，今通會之。法假施設，如「因成」；受假施設，如「相續」；名假施設，如「相待」。《論》云：「五眾等法是法波羅聶提。」五眾和合故名眾生；如根、莖、枝、葉，故有樹名，是「受波羅聶提」。用是名字取二法相，說是二種，是「名波羅聶提」。故知三假義同也。《瓔珞經》亦有三假之文。《大品》云：「有緣思生，無緣思不生。」即「因成」意。《大經》云：「如讀誦法，雖念念滅，亦能從一《阿含》至一《阿含》；猶如飲食，雖念念滅，亦能初飢後飽。」「相續」意也。《淨名》云：「說法不相待，一念不住故。」當知三假之名大小通用，非但小乘名生死法以為見為假；如前說大乘亦名生死為見為假。所謂三藏四門生四見，見見有三假、六十二見、百八煩惱等云云。通教四門生四見，見見具三假、六十二見、百八煩惱等。別教四門生四見，見見具三假、六十二見、百八煩惱等。圓教四門生四見，見見具三假、六十二見、百八煩惱等。如來教門示人無諍法，消者成甘露，不消成毒藥。實語是虛語，生「語見」故，故於四門、十六門，起見、起假云云。

The Treatise, further, speaks of "the three provisional establishments" — how do these relate to the three provisional constructs? Answer: their distinct senses shall not be discussed separately here; here, they shall simply be reconciled together in general. The provisional establishment of dharma is like "composite arising"; the provisional establishment of reception is like "continuity"; the provisional establishment of name is like "mutual relativity." The Treatise says: "The five aggregates and the like are the very dharma of prajñapti." The five aggregates coming together is hence named "sentient being"; just as root, stem, branch, and leaf together give the name "tree" — this is "the prajñapti of reception." Using this very name to grasp the mark of these two dharmas, and speaking of these two kinds, is "the prajñapti of name." Hence it is known that the sense of the threefold provisional is the same throughout. The Yingluo Sūtra, too, contains this same threefold provisional in its own text. The Larger Sūtra says: "With a condition present, thought arises; without a condition, thought does not arise" — this is exactly the sense of "composite arising."
