The Nirvāṇa Sūtra says: "It is like the recitation of a text: though it ceases, thought after thought, it is nonetheless still able to proceed from one Āgama to the next; it is like eating and drinking: though these, too, cease, thought after thought, one is still able to go from hunger at the start to fullness at the end" — this is the sense of "continuity." The Vimalakīrti Sūtra says: "Teaching the Dharma depends on nothing else at all, since not a single thought remains fixed" — one should know that the terms of the threefold provisional are used generally across both the lesser and the great vehicles alike, and it is not only the Hīnayāna that names the dharma of birth-and-death as "view" and as "the provisional" — as already explained, the Mahāyāna, too, names birth-and-death as "view" and as "the provisional" in exactly the same way. This means: the Tripiṭaka teaching's own four gates give rise to four views, and each and every view is complete with the threefold provisional, the sixty-two views, the one hundred and eight afflictions, and so on. The shared teaching's own four gates give rise to four views, each and every view complete with the threefold provisional, the sixty-two views, the one hundred and eight afflictions, and the rest. The distinct teaching's own four gates give rise to four views, complete in exactly the same way. The perfect teaching's own four gates give rise to four views, complete in exactly the same way as well. The Tathāgata's own gate of teaching shows people a dharma free of contention — for one who digests it, it becomes ambrosia; for one who fails to digest it, it becomes poison. Truthful words become deceptive words, giving rise to "the view of language" itself — and this is exactly why, across these four gates and sixteen gates, view arises, and the provisional arises, in every case.

Reading note: The threefold provisional gives the treatise a technical vocabulary for exactly how something can be "false, deceptive, and inverted" without being simply nonexistent: composite arising (a body formed of father, mother, and karmic cause), continuity (that same body persisting from womb to white hair), and mutual relativity (calling a direction "east" only because something else is "west," with the object itself unchanged) are three distinct mechanisms by which a provisional name comes to feel solid. The closing move — insisting this vocabulary is "used generally... not confined to the Hīnayāna alone" — matters for the same reason fascicle 3 fought so hard against splitting the ten suchnesses between ordinary beings and sages: the Mahāyāna does not get a different, more sophisticated illusion to dispel, only a subtler grip on exactly the same one.

The Contemplation of Emptiness: Refuting Composite Arising 明空觀 · 破因成假 · 十八空

Chinese Source

今亦如是，為鈍根故，廣破單、複，訖至無言說見；通用龍樹四句破令盡淨。若一念心起即具三假，三假如前說。當觀此一念為從心自生心？為對塵生心？為根塵共生心？為根塵離生心？若心自生者，前念為根，後念為識；為從根生心？為從識生心？若根能生識，根為有識故生識？根為無識故生識？根若有識，根識則並，又無能生、所生。根若無識而能生識，諸無識物不能生識，根既無識，何能生識？根雖無識而有識性，故能生識者，此之識性是有？是無？有已是識，並在於根，何謂為性？根無識性，不能生識。又識性與識，為一、為異？若一，性即是識，無能、無所；若異，還是他生，非心自生。如是推求，畢竟知心不從自生。若言心不自生，塵來發心故有心生。引經云：「有緣思生，無緣思不生。」若爾，塵在意外，來發內識，則心由他生。今推此塵為是心故生心？為非心故生心？塵若是心，則不名塵，亦非意外，則同自生。又二心並，則無能所。塵若非心，那能生心？如前破。若塵中有生性，是故生心；此性為有？為無？性若是有，性與塵並，亦無能所；若無，無不能生。如是推求，知心畢竟不從塵生。

Ru-Yi Translation

Here it is exactly the same: for the sake of the dull-faculty practitioner, the single and compound views are broken through at length, all the way to the wordless view — using, throughout, Nāgārjuna's own fourfold refutation to break through and exhaust them entirely. Should a single thought of mind arise, it is already complete with the threefold provisional, exactly as explained above. One should contemplate this single thought: does it arise from the mind itself, generating mind? Does it arise facing a sense-object, generating mind? Does it arise from faculty and object together, generating mind? Or does it arise apart from both faculty and object, generating mind? If the mind arises from itself: the preceding thought would be the faculty, and the following thought the consciousness — does mind, then, arise from the faculty, or arise from the consciousness? If the faculty is able to generate consciousness, does the faculty generate consciousness because it already possesses consciousness, or because it lacks consciousness? If the faculty already possesses consciousness, then faculty and consciousness stand together already, with no agent or object of generation left at all; if the faculty, lacking consciousness, is still able to generate consciousness, then every object lacking consciousness ought to be able to generate consciousness as well — and since the faculty already lacks consciousness, how could it possibly generate consciousness at all? Should one say the faculty, though lacking consciousness, still possesses the nature of consciousness, and so is able to generate it — is this very nature of consciousness existent, or nonexistent? If existent, it is already consciousness itself, already present within the faculty — what, then, would there be left to call a mere "nature"? If the faculty lacks the nature of consciousness, it cannot generate consciousness at all. And is this nature of consciousness the same as consciousness, or different from it? If the same, this nature is itself already consciousness, with no agent or object of generation; if different, this is once again generation from another, not the mind generating itself. Examined in this way, one comes, in the end, to know that mind does not arise from itself. Should one say that mind does not arise from itself, but that a sense-object comes and stimulates the mind, and so mind arises — citing the sūtra's own words: "With a condition present, thought arises; without a condition, thought does not arise." If this is so, then the sense-object, existing outside the mental faculty, comes to stimulate the inward consciousness, and mind thereby arises from another. Now examine this sense-object: does it generate mind because it is itself already mind, or because it is not mind at all? If the sense-object is itself already mind, it could not properly be called a "sense-object" at all, nor could it exist outside the mental faculty — and this would then be the same as generation from itself. And, further, two minds standing together would leave no agent or object of generation either. If the sense-object is not mind at all, how could it possibly generate mind? — refuted exactly as before. Should there instead be some nature of generation present within the sense-object, and mind thereby generated on that account — is this nature existent, or nonexistent? If this nature is existent, then this nature stands together with the sense-object already, with no agent or object of generation either; if nonexistent, then nonexistence cannot generate anything at all. Examined in this way, one comes to know that mind, in the end, does not arise from the sense-object.

若根塵合故有心生者，根塵各各有心故合生心？各各無心故合生心？若各各有合，則兩心生，墮自他性中；若各各無，合時亦無。譬如鏡、面，各有像故合生像？各無像故合生像？若各有像，應有兩像；若各無像，合不能生。若鏡、面合為一而生像者，今實不合，合則無像；若鏡、面離故生像者，各在一方，則應有像，今實不爾。根塵離合，亦得如是。如是推求，知心畢竟不從合生。又根塵各有心性，合則心生者，當檢此性為有？為無？如前破云云。若根塵各離而有心生者，此是無因緣生，為有此離？為無此離？若有此離，還從緣生，何謂為離？若無此離，無何能生？若言此離有性，性為有？為無？若性是有，還從緣生，不名為離；若性是無，無何能生？如是推求，知心畢竟不從離生。《中論》云：「諸法不自生，亦不從他生，不共不無因，是故說無生。」即此意也。若推因成假四句，求生不得，執性即薄。但有名字，名為心生。名不在內、外、中間，亦不常自有。是字不住，不住有四句；亦不不住，不住無四句。故無住之心雖有心名字，名字即空。若四句推性不見性，是世諦破性，亦名「性空」；若四句推名不見名，是真諦破假，亦名「相空」。性相俱空者，是為總相從假入空觀也。故《中論》曰：「諸法不自生。」如此用觀者，與《中論》意同也。若根檢不得心，即是「內空」；塵檢無心，即是「外空」；根塵合檢不得，即「內外空」；離檢不得，即是「空空」；四性檢不得，即是「性空」；四句檢不得，即是「相空」；若就塵檢無十方分，即是「大空」；求最上所以不得，即是「第一義空」；四句因緣不得，即「有為空」；因有為說無為，既不得有為，亦不得無為，即「無為空」；四句求心生元不得，即「無始空」；四句求心滅不可得，即「散空」；四句求心生滅不可得，亦不得心不生不滅，即「畢竟空」；三界無別法，唯是一心作，今求心不可得，即「一切空」；觀心無心，觀空無空，即「無所得空」；觀有見三假不可得，即「有法空」；觀無見三假不可得，即「無法空」；觀亦有亦無見三假不可得，即「無法有法空」。如此觀者，即與《大品》意同，是為十八種從假入空觀也。

Should mind arise because faculty and object come together — does it arise because each already individually possesses mind, or because each individually lacks mind? If each already individually possesses mind, then their coming together would generate two minds, falling into the fault of both self-generation and other-generation at once; if each individually lacks mind, then even their coming together would still lack mind as well. It is like a mirror and a face: does the reflected image arise because each already individually possesses an image, or because each individually lacks one? If each already possesses an image, there ought to be two images; if each lacks one, their coming together cannot generate one either. If the mirror and the face come together as one and thereby generate the image, then, in reality, they have not actually come together at all — for were they to truly come together, there would be no image at all; and if the image instead arises because the mirror and the face remain apart, each in its own separate place, then an image ought to appear even so — yet, in reality, this is not the case at all. The faculty and the sense-object, coming together or remaining apart, may be examined in exactly the same way. Examined thus, one comes to know that mind, in the end, does not arise from their coming together either. And should one say that faculty and object each already possess the nature of mind, so that mind arises once they come together — one should examine whether this nature is existent or nonexistent, refuted exactly as before. Should mind arise because faculty and object remain apart from one another — this would be generation without any cause or condition at all: does this "apartness" itself exist, or not? If this apartness exists, it, too, arises from some condition — how, then, could it truly be called "apartness" at all? If this apartness does not exist, then nonexistence cannot generate anything either. Should one say this apartness itself possesses some nature — is this nature existent, or nonexistent? If this nature is existent, it, too, arises from some condition, and so could not properly be named "apartness" at all; if this nature is nonexistent, then nonexistence cannot generate anything either. Examined in this way, one comes to know that mind, in the end, does not arise from apartness either. The Middle Treatise says: "No dharma arises from itself, nor from another, nor from both together, nor without any cause at all — this is exactly why it is said to be unborn." This is precisely that same sense. Having thus examined all four propositions of the provisional of composite arising and found that arising cannot be found through any of them, one's grasping at any fixed nature grows correspondingly thin — there remains only a bare name, called "the mind's own arising." This name resides neither within, nor without, nor in between; nor does it exist constantly, of itself, either. This word does not remain fixed — and its very not remaining fixed cannot be pinned down through any of the four propositions either; nor is it, on the other hand, "not remaining fixed" in some fixed sense, since this too cannot be pinned down through any of the four propositions. Hence the mind, which abides nowhere at all, though it carries the mere name "mind," is, in that very name, already empty. Where the four propositions examine this nature and find no nature to be seen, this is the conventional truth breaking through this nature, also named "the emptiness of nature"; where the four propositions examine this name and find no name to be seen, this is the real truth breaking through this provisional construct, also named "the emptiness of the mark." Nature and mark both alike empty — this is the general contemplation of entering emptiness from the provisional. Hence the Middle Treatise says, "No dharma arises from itself" — using contemplation in this way accords exactly with the Middle Treatise's own intent. Where the faculty is examined and mind cannot be found, this is "inward emptiness"; where the sense-object is examined and no mind is found, this is "outward emptiness"; where faculty and object combined are examined and nothing is found, this is "emptiness both inward and outward"; where apartness is examined and nothing is found, this is "the emptiness of emptiness itself"; where the four natures are examined and none is found, this is "the emptiness of nature"; where the four propositions are examined and nothing is found, this is "the emptiness of the mark"; where the sense-object is examined and found to lack any of the ten directions, this is "great emptiness"; where the very utmost is sought and cannot be found, this is "the emptiness of ultimate meaning"; where the four propositions of causation cannot be found, this is "the emptiness of the conditioned"; and since the unconditioned is spoken of only in relation to the conditioned, once the conditioned itself cannot be found, the unconditioned, too, cannot be found — this is "the emptiness of the unconditioned"; where the four propositions seek the very origin of the mind's own arising and cannot find it, this is "the emptiness without beginning"; where the four propositions seek the mind's own ceasing and cannot find it, this is "the emptiness of dispersal"; where the four propositions seek both the arising and the ceasing of mind and can find neither, nor can they find any "neither arising nor ceasing" either, this is "ultimate emptiness"; since the three realms have no separate dharma, being created by the single mind alone, and this very mind, sought after, cannot be found, this is "the emptiness of everything"; contemplating mind and finding no mind, contemplating emptiness and finding no emptiness either, this is "the emptiness of attaining nothing"; contemplating the threefold provisional of the view of existence and finding it unfindable, this is "the emptiness of what has existence as a dharma"; contemplating the threefold provisional of the view of nonexistence and finding it unfindable, this is "the emptiness of what has nonexistence as a dharma"; and contemplating the threefold provisional of the view of both existence and nonexistence and finding it unfindable, this is "the emptiness of both existence-as-dharma and nonexistence-as-dharma." Contemplating in this way accords exactly with the Larger Sūtra's own intent — this, then, is the eighteen kinds of contemplation of entering emptiness from the provisional.

Reading note: This fourfold refutation — self, other, both, and neither — is Nāgārjuna's own signature argument form from the Middle Treatise's opening verse, and Zhiyi runs it here with unusual patience against a single thought's own arising, treating each of the four options (mind from itself, mind from a sense-object, mind from their combination, mind from neither) as seriously as a live philosophical hypothesis before dismantling it. The eighteen kinds of emptiness that follow are not eighteen different emptinesses but eighteen different vantage points on the same single finding, cross-referenced against inward, outward, ultimate, conditioned, and beginningless — proof that a single rigorous refutation of one thought's own arising is already sufficient to ground the entire technical vocabulary of Prajñāpāramitā emptiness.

Refuting Continuity, and Refuting Mutual Relativity 明空觀 · 破相續假 · 破相待假

Chinese Source

若不悟者，轉入相續假破之。何以故？雖因成四破不得心生，今現見心念念生滅相續不斷，何謂不生？此之念念，為當前念滅，後念生？為前念不滅，後念生？為前念亦滅亦不滅，後念生？為前念非滅非不滅，後念生？若前念不滅後念生：此則念自生念，兩生相並，亦無能所。若前念有生性，生於後念，此性為有？為無？有則非性；無則不生如前。若前念滅後念生者：前不滅生，名為自性；今由滅生，不滅望滅，豈非他性？他性滅中有生故生？無生故生？有生是生，生滅相違，乃是生生，何謂滅生？若滅無生，無何能生？若滅有生性，性破如前。若前念亦滅亦不滅後念生者：若滅，已屬滅；若不滅，已屬不滅；若不滅合滅能生，即是共生。共生自相違，相違何能生？又若各各有生，即有二過；各各無生，合亦不生。若滅不滅中有生性者，為有？為無？若性定有，何謂滅不滅？若性定無，亦何謂滅不滅？此不免斷、常之失，還墮共過。若前念非滅非不滅而後念心生者：為有此非滅非不滅？為無此非滅非不滅？若有，則非無因；若無，無因不能生。若無因有生性者，此性即因，何謂無因？若無，無不能生。如是四句推相續假，求心不得。無四性實，執心即薄，但有心名字。是字不住內、外、兩中間，亦不常自有。相續無性，即世諦破性，名為「性空」；相續無名，即真諦破假，名為「相空」。性相俱空，乃至作十八空，如前說。是名「從假以入空觀」。

Ru-Yi Translation

Should one not yet awaken through this, one turns instead to breaking through the provisional of continuity. Why is this? Because though the four propositions of composite arising fail to find any generated mind, one now, in fact, still sees the mind arising and ceasing, thought after thought, in unbroken continuity — how, then, could this be called "unborn"? This continuity, thought after thought — does the preceding thought cease and the following thought arise? Does the preceding thought not cease, and the following thought arise regardless? Does the preceding thought both cease and not cease, and the following thought arise? Or does the preceding thought neither cease nor not cease, and the following thought arise? If the preceding thought does not cease and the following thought arises: this would be thought generating thought by itself, two acts of generation standing side by side, with no agent or object of generation either. Should the preceding thought possess some nature of generation, generating the following thought thereby — is this nature existent, or nonexistent? If existent, it is no longer merely a "nature" at all; if nonexistent, then nonexistence cannot generate anything, refuted as before. If the preceding thought ceases and the following thought arises: the preceding thought's own not-ceasing-and-generating would be named self-nature; but here, since it is generation through ceasing, ceasing set against not-ceasing — is this not, then, other-nature instead? Within this "other-nature's" own ceasing, does generation occur because generation is present, or because generation is absent? If generation is present, this is already generation — and generation contradicts ceasing, so this would simply be generation-generating, not what could properly be called ceasing-generating at all. If generation is absent in this ceasing, then absence cannot generate anything either. Should this ceasing possess some nature of generation, this nature is refuted exactly as before. If the preceding thought both ceases and does not cease, and the following thought arises thereby: insofar as it ceases, this already belongs to ceasing; insofar as it does not cease, this already belongs to not-ceasing; and if not-ceasing combines with ceasing to generate, this is simply co-generation — but co-generation already contradicts itself internally, so how could contradiction itself generate anything at all? And further, should each individually already possess generation, this incurs two faults at once; should each individually lack generation, their combination, too, fails to generate. Should this "both ceasing and not-ceasing" possess some nature of generation — is it existent, or nonexistent? If this nature is fixedly existent, how could it still be called "both ceasing and not-ceasing" at all? If this nature is fixedly nonexistent, how, too, could it be called "both ceasing and not-ceasing"? This cannot escape the fault of either annihilationism or eternalism, and falls back once more into the fault of co-generation. If the preceding thought neither ceases nor does not cease, and the following mind arises thereby: does this "neither ceasing nor not-ceasing" exist, or not? If it exists, this is no longer generation without a cause at all; if it does not exist, then a nonexistent cause cannot generate anything. Should this nonexistent cause possess some nature of generation, this nature is itself already the cause — so how could this still be called "without a cause"? And if it does not exist, then nonexistence cannot generate anything either. Examining the provisional of continuity by means of these four propositions in this way, generation is nowhere to be found. Since none of the four natures is real, one's grasping at mind grows correspondingly thin, leaving only the bare name "mind." This name resides neither within, nor without, nor in between the two; nor does it exist constantly, of itself, either. Continuity possessing no fixed nature is the conventional truth breaking through this nature, named "the emptiness of nature"; continuity possessing no fixed name is the real truth breaking through this provisional construct, named "the emptiness of the mark." Nature and mark both alike empty, and so on, up through the eighteen kinds of emptiness, exactly as explained before. This is named "the contemplation of entering emptiness from the provisional."

若不得入者，猶計有心待於無心，相待惑起，此與上異。因成，取根塵兩法和合為因成；相續，竪取意根前後為相續。竪望生滅，此是別滅，別滅則狹。今相待假，待於通滅，此義則寬。通滅者，如三無為，雖不併是滅而得是無生。待虛空無生而說心生，即是相待假。上既不悟，復因上惑共起此惑，故言「因兼」；上惑猶在，復起此惑，故言「過之」。又因兼者，無生法塵待意根生，亦是因成；因上假心來續相待，即是相續，故言「因兼」。過之者，上兩假不於通滅起惑，今約通起，豈非過之？釋既異舊，而借彼語示相待假相耳。今檢此心，為待無生心生？為待有生心生？為待亦生亦無生而心生？為待非生非不生而心生？若待無生而生心者：有此無生？無此無生？若有生可待，還是待有，何謂待無？有有相待，即是自生。若無此無生，無何所待？若秖待此無無而生心者，一切無無亦應生心。無望於有，無即是他生也。又無生雖無而有生性，待此性故而知有心。此性為已生？為未生？若已生生，即是於生，何謂為性？性若未生，未生何能生？若待生而心生者：生還待生，長應待長；既無此義，何得心生？若待生無生故有心生，如待長短，得有於長。此墮二過。各有，則二生並；各無，全不可得，如前。若待非生非無生而有心生者，《論》云：「從因緣生尚不可，何況無因緣？」又此無因為有？為無？若有，還是待有；若無，還是待無；何謂無因？若言有性，性為有？為無？性若是有，為生？非生？若生，已是生，何謂為性？若無生，云何能生？如是四句推相待假，求心生不可得，執心即薄，不起性實，但有名字。名字之生，生則非生。是字不在內、外、中間，亦不常自有；是字無所有。求性不可得，世諦破性，是名「性空」。求名不可得，真諦破假，是名「相空」。復次，此性相中求陰入界不可得，即是「法空」；性相中求人我知見不可得，名「眾生空」；乃至作十八空，如前說。是名「從假入空」，慧眼得開，見第一義。非但有見三假惑除，一切見惑無不清淨，正智現前。是名無生門通於止觀，亦是止觀成無生門。若不悟者，當善用止觀巧破見假，信、法迴轉，成方便道，伏於有見；無量煩惱悉皆被伏。伏故名「善有漏五陰」也。以被伏故，有見不起，度入無見計中，如後破。

Should one not yet enter through this either, one still supposes there to be a mind depending on a mindless state, and so the affliction of mutual relativity arises — differing in this way from what came before. Composite arising took faculty and object, the two dharmas combined, as its own basis; continuity took the mental faculty alone, before and after, as its own basis. Continuity, considered against arising and ceasing, is a particular kind of cessation — a particular cessation is thus narrow in scope. Here, the provisional of mutual relativity depends instead on cessation in general, and this sense is thereby broader. "Cessation in general" refers to the three kinds of the unconditioned: though not, every one of them, strictly cessation, they nonetheless amount to the unborn. Depending on the unborn state of empty space in order to speak of mind's own arising is precisely the provisional of mutual relativity. Since the earlier two provisional constructs were not yet awakened through, and this present affliction arises further, depending on those earlier afflictions, this is said to "carry within it" what came before; and since those earlier provisional constructs still remain even as this one arises in addition, this is said to "go beyond them." Further, "carrying within it" means: the unborn dharma-object, depending on the mental faculty to arise, is itself already composite arising; and the provisional mind built upon this, continuing on in relation to it, is itself already continuity — hence it is said to "carry within it." "Going beyond them" means: the earlier two provisional constructs did not give rise to affliction in relation to cessation in general, while this one now arises precisely in relation to that general cessation — is this not, then, "going beyond them"? Though this explanation differs from the earlier account, it borrows that same language to show the mark of the provisional of mutual relativity here. Now examine this mind: does it arise depending on the unborn state? Does it arise depending on some existent state of birth? Does it arise depending on both birth and the unborn at once? Or does it arise depending on neither birth nor non-birth? If mind arises depending on the unborn state: does this unborn state exist, or not? If there is some birth for it to depend upon, this is once again depending on existence — how, then, could it be called "depending on the unborn"? Existence depending on existence is simply self-generation. If this unborn state does not exist, then there is nothing at all for it to depend upon. Should mind be said to arise simply depending on this very absence of birth, then every instance of absence whatsoever ought to generate mind as well. Nonexistence, set against existence, is itself already generation from another. And should this unborn state, though itself nonexistent, still possess some nature of generation, and mind is thereby known to exist by depending on this very nature — has this nature already generated, or not yet generated? If it has already generated, this is already generation itself — what, then, would be left to call a mere "nature"? If this nature has not yet generated, how could what has not yet generated possibly generate anything at all? If mind arises depending on some existent birth: generation would then depend on generation, just as length depends on length — but there is no such thing as length depending on length, so how could mind possibly arise this way? If mind arises depending on birth as set against the unborn, this is like length depending on shortness in order to be called "long" — this falls into two faults at once: should each already individually possess this, two acts of generation would stand together; should each individually lack it, nothing at all could be found, exactly as before. If mind arises depending on what is neither birth nor the unborn: the Treatise says, "Generation from causes and conditions is already untenable — how much more, then, generation without any cause or condition at all?" And is this absence of cause itself existent, or nonexistent? If existent, this is once again depending on existence; if nonexistent, this is once again depending on nonexistence — how, then, could it be called "without a cause" at all? Should one say it possesses some nature — is this nature existent, or nonexistent? If this nature is existent, does it generate, or not? If it generates, this is already generation itself — what, then, is left to call a "nature"? If it does not generate, how could it possibly generate anything at all? Examining the provisional of mutual relativity by means of these four propositions in this way, the mind's own arising cannot be found; one's grasping at mind grows correspondingly thin, no fixed nature arising at all, leaving only the bare name. This name's own "arising" is, in its very arising, not truly arising at all. This word resides neither within, nor without, nor in between; nor does it exist constantly, of itself, either — this word has nothing whatsoever to it. Seeking this nature and finding it unobtainable, the conventional truth breaks through this nature — this is named "the emptiness of nature." Seeking this name and finding it unobtainable, the real truth breaks through this provisional construct — this is named "the emptiness of the mark." Furthermore, seeking the aggregates, entries, and elements within this nature and mark and finding them unobtainable, this is "the emptiness of dharmas"; seeking the knowledge and view of a person or a self within this nature and mark and finding them unobtainable, this is named "the emptiness of sentient beings" — and so on, up through the eighteen kinds of emptiness, exactly as explained before. This is named "entering emptiness from the provisional," the eye of wisdom thereby opened, seeing the ultimate truth directly. Not only is the affliction of the threefold provisional belonging to the view of existence removed — every affliction of view whatsoever becomes, without exception, pure, and correct wisdom manifests before one. This is the gate of the unborn penetrating through to calming-and-contemplation, and is likewise calming-and-contemplation accomplishing the gate of the unborn. Should one not yet awaken through this, one should skillfully apply calming-and-contemplation to break through the affliction of views with skill, the practitioner of faith and the practitioner of dharma each turning about accordingly, forming the expedient path, subduing the view of existence — every boundless affliction is thereby, entirely, subdued. Because it is thus subdued, this is named "wholesome, contaminated aggregates." Because it is subdued, the view of existence no longer arises, and one passes over into the calculation belonging to the view of nonexistence instead, to be refuted below.

Reading note: Where composite arising asked whether a single thought's arising could be pinned to self, other, both, or neither, continuity asks the same question of the relation between one thought and the next, and mutual relativity asks it of the relation between existence and its own absence — three progressively subtler places the mind might still be smuggling in a fixed reference point. That each of the three, run through the identical fourfold refutation, arrives at the same eighteen kinds of emptiness is the argument's real payoff: it is not that three different problems happen to share a solution, but that "self-generation," "generation from another," and the rest name the same handful of ways any claim to fixed generation can fail, regardless of what is being examined.

Refuting the View of Nonexistence, and the Meditator's Own Trap 明空觀 · 破無見

Chinese Source

夫破見之由聞思不定。若上根人聞觀於生，知生無生，破執得悟；中根執輕，成伏見方便，善有漏五陰；下根執重，猶懷取著，聞破生不得生，謂無生是實，更起無生見。又當總別破之。總破者，如《大品》云：「識無生尚不可得，何況識生？」又「識生尚不可得，何況識無生？」生與無生俱不可得。《楞伽經》中又廣破無生見。然無生之理非識所知，云何謂情？捨有緣無，如步屈蟲，又似獼猴。不應虛妄執此見著。是為總破。

Ru-Yi Translation

The reason breaking through view depends on hearing or reflection is not fixed. Should a person of superior faculty hear this teaching on generation, know that generation is itself unborn, break through their own attachment, and awaken thereby — good. A person of middling faculty, whose attachment is comparatively light, forms the expedient path of subduing views, wholesome and contaminated aggregates. A person of inferior faculty, whose attachment runs heavy, still clings and grasps — hearing that generation, once broken through, cannot be found, they take "the unborn" itself to be real, and give rise to a further view of the unborn instead. This, too, must be refuted, both in general and in particular. As for the general refutation: the Larger Sūtra says, "Consciousness of the unborn cannot ultimately be found — how much less, then, consciousness of the born?" And also: "Consciousness of the born cannot ultimately be found — how much less, then, consciousness of the unborn?" Both the born and the unborn alike cannot ultimately be found. The Laṅkāvatāra Sūtra, too, refutes the view of the unborn at length. Yet the principle of the unborn is not something ordinary consciousness can know at all — how, then, could it be reduced to mere sentiment? Casting aside existence only to cling to nonexistence is like an inchworm, or like a monkey swinging from branch to branch — one should not falsely cling to this view either. This is the general refutation.

別破者，行人用止觀破因成三假，不得性相，泯然入定，不見內外，亦無前後，無相形待。寂然定住，或豁亡身心一切都淨，便發此無心，自謂得無生止觀，定慧已成，而起見著。著此空想，諸佛不化。何故不化？觀心推畫，發一分細定，生一分空解。此是空見法塵與心相應，何關無生？《釋論》簡外道、佛法二俱觀空，云何有異？外道愛著觀空智慧，即是向者所發空塵，謂為涅槃。即有能觀者，能觀者便成身見；身見故，即有利鈍十使乃至八十八等，生死浩然，如前說。如是罪過皆由空塵而起，障真失道，豈會涅槃？是名外道觀空。佛弟子觀無生，若發空心，空心生時即知是愛，何者？生名愛法，愛法即是無明，無明生我見等八十八使，一一皆具三假之惑，終不執謂是真無生。云何三假？良由上來有見三假被伏，度入無見。無生法塵對意根，一念空心生，即因成假；以生心滅故，無生心生，是相續假；豁爾無生待於有生，是相待假。當推此無生心生，為意根生？為法塵生？為合？為離？若意根生者，為根生？為識生？若根生，為根中有識故生識？為無識故生識？若根有識，為是根？為非根？識若是根，則無能所；根若無識，何能生識？若根有生識之性，此性為有？為無？性若有者，識性與識為一？為異？若一，性即是識；若異，異何能生？自生中檢心不可得，具如上說。若由塵起無生心者，塵為有心？為無心？若有心，則無能所；若無，無不能生。又塵為一？為異？一則無能所；異則不能生。檢他心不可得，具如上說。若根塵合，有無生心生者，此有二過，如前說云云。又離根、離塵，有無生心生者，從因緣尚不可得，何況無因？如前。當知無生之心，不自、不他、不共、不離，無四性；無四性故名「性空」。性空即無心，而言心者，但有名字。名字不在內、外，是名「相空」。乃至十八空，如上說。是為從假入空見第一義。非但無見假破，上惑下障一切皆除，得正智慧。若未去者，勤用止觀善巧修習，信、法迴轉，成方便道，伏於苦集；所有陰界入等八十八使皆悉被伏。以被伏故，名「善有漏」也。勤修力故，無見中假不復得起，度入有無假中，如後破云云。

As for the particular refutation: a practitioner uses calming-and-contemplation to break through the threefold provisional of composite arising, failing to find nature or mark, and dissolves entirely into concentration, seeing neither within nor without, with neither before nor after, no mark set against any other. Abiding quiescent in concentration, or suddenly forgetting body and mind entirely, everything utterly purified, one thereby gives rise to this mindless state, supposing oneself to have attained the calming-and-contemplation of the unborn, concentration and wisdom already accomplished — and thereupon gives rise to attachment to this very view. Clinging to this notion of emptiness, the buddhas cannot transform such a person. Why can they not transform them? Because this contemplation of the mind, in its own calculation, has merely brought forth a fraction of subtle concentration, giving rise to a fraction of understanding of emptiness — this is simply a dharma-object of emptiness-as-view corresponding with the mind, and has nothing whatsoever to do with the true unborn at all. The Treatise distinguishes the heterodox path's own contemplation of emptiness from the Buddhadharma's own — how, then, do they differ? The heterodox path craves and clings to the wisdom of contemplating emptiness — this is exactly that same dharma-object of emptiness brought forth just now, taken to be nirvāṇa. Since there is thereby still one who contemplates, this contemplator becomes, in turn, the view of a substantial self; and on account of this view of a self, the ten proclivities, sharp and dull alike, and their full eighty-eight, are present as well, birth-and-death spreading out vast, exactly as explained before. Every fault of this kind arises entirely from this dharma-object of emptiness, obstructing the real and losing the path — how could this possibly accord with nirvāṇa at all? This is named the heterodox path's own contemplation of emptiness. Should the Buddha's own disciple, contemplating the unborn, give rise to a mind of emptiness, the very moment this mind of emptiness arises, one should recognize it at once as craving. Why is this? "Arising" is itself already named the dharma of craving; the dharma of craving is itself already ignorance; ignorance gives rise to the view of a self and its full eighty-eight proclivities, each and every one already complete with the affliction of the threefold provisional — and so one never, in the end, clings to this as the true unborn at all. How, then, does the threefold provisional apply here? Simply because the threefold provisional belonging to the view of existence, already discussed above, has been subdued, and one has passed over into the view of nonexistence instead. The unborn dharma-object meeting the mental faculty, a single thought of empty mind arising, is precisely composite arising; the mind of generation having ceased, the mind of the unborn arising in its place, is continuity; and this sudden unborn state, depending on some existent state of generation, is mutual relativity. One should examine this very arising of the mind of the unborn: does it arise from the mental faculty? From the dharma-object? From their combination? Or apart from both? If it arises from the mental faculty: does it arise from the faculty itself, or from consciousness? If from the faculty, does it arise because the faculty already contains consciousness, or because it lacks consciousness? If the faculty already contains consciousness, is this "the faculty," or "not the faculty"? If consciousness is itself the faculty, there is no agent or object of generation left; if the faculty lacks consciousness, how could it possibly generate consciousness at all? Should the faculty possess some nature capable of generating consciousness, is this nature existent, or nonexistent? If existent, is this nature of consciousness the same as consciousness, or different from it? If the same, this nature is itself already consciousness; if different, how could something different possibly generate anything at all? Examining self-generation in this way, mind cannot be found — exactly as explained above in full. Should the unborn mind arise on account of the dharma-object instead: does this dharma-object already possess mind, or not? If it already possesses mind, there is no agent or object of generation; if it lacks mind, then lack cannot generate anything. And is this dharma-object the same as mind, or different from it? If the same, there is no agent or object; if different, it cannot generate anything either. Examining generation from another in this way, mind cannot be found — exactly as explained above in full. Should the mind of the unborn arise because faculty and object combine, this incurs the same two faults already explained above. And should the mind of the unborn arise apart from both faculty and object — generation from a cause and condition is already untenable, how much more, then, generation without any cause at all, exactly as before. One should know that the mind of the unborn arises neither from itself, nor from another, nor from both together, nor apart from both — lacking all four natures; and lacking these four natures, this is named "the emptiness of nature." The emptiness of nature is itself the absence of any fixed mind, and yet, spoken of as "mind," this remains only a bare name. This name resides neither within nor without — this is named "the emptiness of the mark." And so on, up through the eighteen kinds of emptiness, exactly as explained above. This is entering emptiness from the provisional, seeing the ultimate truth directly. Not only is the affliction belonging to the view of nonexistence broken through — every affliction above and every obstruction below is, without exception, removed, and correct wisdom is attained. Should one not yet be free of it, one should diligently and skillfully apply calming-and-contemplation, the practitioner of faith and the practitioner of dharma each turning about accordingly, forming the expedient path, subduing suffering and its origin — every one of the eighty-eight proclivities belonging to the aggregates, entries, and elements is thereby, entirely, subdued. Because it is subdued, this is named "wholesome and contaminated." Through the power of this diligent cultivation, the provisional belonging to the view of nonexistence no longer arises, and one passes over into the provisional belonging to both existence and nonexistence together, to be refuted below.

Reading note: This is the treatise's most pointed warning against a specific meditative failure mode: a practitioner who genuinely dissolves the sense of self and object in deep concentration, "everything utterly purified," may still mistake that very dissolution for realization — precisely because "arising" itself, even the arising of a mind that feels empty, is still, in its bare occurrence, "the dharma of craving." The distinction Zhiyi draws from the Treatise between heterodox and Buddhist contemplations of emptiness turns on a single test: does a contemplator still remain, subtly reified as "the one who has attained emptiness"? If so, the very posture of having transcended view is, itself, already a view — the same trap the inchworm-and-monkey simile named a moment before, swinging from one branch (existence) to what only looks like solid ground (nonexistence) but is merely another branch.

Refuting Both Existence and Nonexistence, and Neither Existence nor Nonexistence 明空觀 · 破亦有亦無見 · 破非有非無見

Chinese Source

次、破亦有亦無見三假者：行人善用止觀伏無見惑，無假不起。或進一分定慧，豁發亦有亦無與心相應，即便謂言：「若無心者，誰知無生？無生是無，知即是有。」發此心時，受是亦有亦無見，謂是事實，堅著不可捨，不知過患。如長爪自謂有道，實是苦集，不能識故。佛點示之，即便得悟。發見之人亦復如是，迷此見毒，不識正真；若聞指示，執心颯解。云何指示？《大品》五受皆不受，汝云何受是亦有亦無法塵，豈非受陰？緣此像貌，行用此法了別此法，四陰宛然。如此受、想皆名污穢，是見依色陰。又意根受是亦有亦無法塵，即是界；根塵相涉，即是入。是名苦也。又我能行、能受、能知此法假名，即起「我見」；我見既生，即有「邊見」；若撥因果是「邪見」；計此為道是「戒取」；計為涅槃是「見取」；違「瞋」，順「喜」，我解「慢」他；不識苦集即「癡」，後當大「疑」。如是等十使，歷三界，具八十八，違於實道，順於生死，悉於亦有亦無見心中生。又此見心即備三假，例前可知。今破此見三假者，還用四句，一一例前可解。如是破已，三假四句陰入皆無實性，即是「性空」；但有名字，名字即空，是名「相空」。性相既空，乃至十八空，如上說，即是入第一義，正智現前。若不入者，善用悉檀，信、法迴轉，巧修止觀，伏於諸見，令成方便，善有漏法。亦有亦無見雖伏不起，仍度入非有非無見中，如後破。

Ru-Yi Translation

Next, as for breaking through the threefold provisional belonging to the view of both existence and nonexistence: a practitioner, having skillfully applied calming-and-contemplation to subdue the affliction of the view of nonexistence, no longer gives rise to that provisional construct at all. Yet should a further fraction of concentration and wisdom advance, suddenly bringing forth a view of both existence and nonexistence together, corresponding with the mind, one might at once suppose: "If there is truly no mind at all, who could know the unborn? The unborn is itself nonexistence — and yet the very knowing of it is itself existence." The very moment this thought arises, one receives this as the view of both existence and nonexistence, holding it to be real, clinging to it firmly, unable to let it go, unaware of its own faults. It is like Dīrghanakha, who supposed himself already to possess the path, when this was, in reality, still suffering and its origin — simply because he failed to recognize it as such. The Buddha, with a single pointed remark, brought him to awakening at once. One who gives rise to this view is exactly the same: confused by the very poison of this view, failing to recognize what is truly correct — yet, upon hearing it pointed out, the grasping mind dissolves at a stroke. How is it pointed out? The Larger Sūtra holds that none of the five kinds of reception are truly received — how, then, could you receive this dharma-object of both existence and nonexistence as real? Is this not itself already the aggregate of feeling? Taking hold of this very image, applying this dharma, discriminating this dharma — the four aggregates are already, vividly, present. Feeling and perception of this kind are both named defilement, and this view depends upon the aggregate of form as well. And the mental faculty receiving this dharma-object of both existence and nonexistence is itself already the element; faculty and object interacting is itself already the entry. This, then, is suffering. Further, that "I" am able to act, able to receive, able to know this provisionally named dharma at once gives rise to "the view of a self"; once the view of a self has arisen, "the extreme view" follows; sweeping aside cause and effect is "the deviant view"; taking this to be the path is "clinging to precept and vow"; taking it to be nirvāṇa is "clinging to views"; opposition brings "aversion," accord brings "delight," my own understanding against another's ignorance gives rise to "pride"; failing to recognize suffering and its origin is "delusion"; and, afterward, great "doubt" follows. These ten proclivities, spread across the three realms, complete in their full eighty-eight, running contrary to the real path and according instead with birth-and-death, all arise, entirely, from within this very mind holding the view of both existence and nonexistence. And this view-mind, too, is already complete with the threefold provisional, understood by the same pattern as before. Breaking through this view's own threefold provisional now, one uses, once again, the same four propositions — each and every one to be understood by the same pattern as before. Having thus broken through it, the threefold provisional and the four propositions, the aggregates and entries, are all, alike, without any real nature — this is "the emptiness of nature"; having only a bare name, and this name itself already empty, this is named "the emptiness of the mark." Nature and mark both already empty, and so on, up through the eighteen kinds of emptiness, exactly as explained above — this is entering the ultimate truth directly, correct wisdom manifesting before one. Should one not yet enter through this, one should skillfully apply the siddhāntas, the practitioner of faith and the practitioner of dharma each turning about accordingly, skillfully cultivating calming-and-contemplation, subduing every view, forming the expedient path, a wholesome, contaminated dharma. Though the view of both existence and nonexistence is thus subdued and no longer arises, one still passes over into the view of neither existence nor nonexistence, to be refuted below.

次、破非有非無見者：上勤用方便伏有無見，豁然更發，離有無心。所以者何？心若定有，不可令無；心若定無，不可令有，云何乃謂亦有亦無？若不定有，則非有；若不定無，則非無。非有者，非生也；非無者，非滅也。出於有無之表，是名「中道」，與《中論》同。何以故？前有見，是因緣生法；無見，是即空；亦有亦無，是即假；今是即中。堅著此心，計以為實，是人能起無量過患。何以故？汝謂此心為實者，乃以虛語為實語；生語見故，故非真實。若真實者，此心應是常、樂、我、淨。此心生滅，故非常；受此心，故非樂；不自在，故非我；污穢，故非淨。我心生故，是「身見」；身見有無，未免非有非無，如屈步蟲，是名「邊見」；謂非有非無見以為中道，通諸生死，是愚癡論，非道非字謂是道字，是名「戒取」；謂非有非無心為涅槃，具陰界入利鈍等使，是名「見取」；謂非有非無以為正法，乃破一切世間因果，故名「非有」；破一切出世間因果，故名「非無」。破正見威儀尚不當世間道理，云何能當出世道理？「寧起我見如須彌山，不惡取空。」不正為正，是名「邪見」。若順歎，則「愛」；違毀，則「瞋」；不識此心毒草、藥王，則「癡」；自擅陵他，則「慢」；後當大「疑」。略過有十，廣不可盡。如是等過，皆從非有非無見心中出。又一一過悉具三假，如前云云。若破此見假，還用前四句止觀逐而破之，如前云云。

Next, as for breaking through the view of neither existence nor nonexistence: having diligently applied expedient means above to subdue the views of both existence and of both existence-and-nonexistence together, one suddenly gives rise to a further mind free of both existence and nonexistence. Why is this? If the mind were fixedly existent, it could not be made nonexistent; if the mind were fixedly nonexistent, it could not be made existent — how, then, could it be called "both existence and nonexistence" at all? If it is not fixedly existent, it is thus not-existent; if it is not fixedly nonexistent, it is thus not-nonexistent. "Not-existent" means unborn; "not-nonexistent" means unceasing. Emerging beyond the very surface of existence and nonexistence, this is named "the middle way," and accords with the Middle Treatise. Why is this? The earlier view of existence is the dharma arisen through causes and conditions; the view of nonexistence is precisely this same dharma as empty; the view of both existence and nonexistence is precisely this same dharma as provisional; and this present view is precisely this same dharma as the middle. Clinging firmly to this mind, taking it to be real, such a person is thereby able to give rise to boundless faults. Why is this? In holding this mind to be real, one takes false speech for true speech, giving rise to "the view of language" itself, and so it is not truly real at all. Were it truly real, this mind ought to be permanent, blissful, possessed of a self, and pure. Yet this mind arises and ceases, and so is not permanent; it is itself received, and so is not bliss; it lacks self-mastery, and so is without a self; it is defiled, and so is not pure. Since the mind of "I" arises here, this is "the view of a self"; the view of a self, whether of existence or nonexistence, still fails to escape being "neither existence nor nonexistence," like an inchworm's own motion — this is named "the extreme view"; holding the view of neither existence nor nonexistence to be the middle way, penetrating through every instance of birth-and-death, is a foolish, deluded theory, mistaking what is not the path for the very word "path" itself — this is named "clinging to precept and vow"; holding this mind of neither existence nor nonexistence to be nirvāṇa, complete with the sharp and dull proclivities of the aggregates, entries, and elements, is named "clinging to views"; holding neither existence nor nonexistence to be the true Dharma thereby sweeps aside every mundane causation, and so is named "not-existence"; and sweeps aside every world-transcending causation as well, and so is named "not-nonexistence." Breaking through right view and right deportment already fails even to meet the standard of mundane reasoning — how, then, could it possibly meet the standard of world-transcending reasoning? "Better to give rise to the view of a self as vast as Mount Sumeru than to wrongly grasp at emptiness" — taking what is not correct to be correct is named "the deviant view." According with it and praising it becomes "craving"; opposing it and reviling it becomes "aversion"; failing to recognize this mind as both a poisonous weed and a healing herb at once becomes "delusion"; presuming upon oneself and looking down on others becomes "pride"; and, afterward, great "doubt" follows. In brief, there are ten such faults; in full, they cannot be exhausted. Faults of this kind, in every case, issue from this very mind holding the view of neither existence nor nonexistence. And each and every one of these faults, too, is complete with the threefold provisional, exactly as explained before. Should one break through this view's own threefold provisional, one uses, once again, the same four propositions of calming-and-contemplation, pursuing and breaking through it exactly as explained before.

Reading note: Each of the four views in turn — existence, nonexistence, both, neither — is shown to collapse into exactly one of the treatise's own three truths (existence into the provisional, nonexistence into emptiness, both into the provisional again more explicitly, neither into the middle), which is the real point of walking through all four rather than stopping once emptiness is reached: the "middle way" is not a fourth, safer position beyond the first three, but itself just as capable of curdling into a view — "better to give rise to the view of a self as vast as Mount Sumeru than to wrongly grasp at emptiness" applies with equal force here. A poisonous weed and a healing herb at once is the passage's own image for why no single insight, however correct, is safe from being turned into the next attachment.

The Four Noble Truths Within Every View, and the Wordless View's Own Trap 見假總結 · 破無言說見

Chinese Source

復次，點出諸見五陰者，是示其苦；點出十使者，是示其集；用止觀破者，是示其道；諸見若伏、若無，是示其滅。夫一切外道邪解，佛法僻計無量過患，皆用四諦破之，無不革凡成聖。如來初說《阿含》四諦之力尚能如此，何況大乘三種四諦，何所不破耶？若非有非無見破者，一切諸惑亦悉斷壞，發正智慧。是名從假入空見第一義。若不入者，當用止觀，信、法迴轉，善巧四隨，方便修習，伏諸見惑；執心即薄，住方便道，成善有漏法。此見不起，度入無言說中，如後破云云。所以節節說見過者，殷勤行人，令於觀心善識毒草，明解藥王。若得此意，終不謬計也。章節雖煩，番番不雜。能了此者，可與論道；兀然如盲，若為識乳？

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, pointing out the five aggregates within every view shows their suffering; pointing out the ten proclivities shows their origin; using calming-and-contemplation to break through them shows the path; and views, whether subdued or entirely absent, show their cessation. Every heterodox path's own deviant understanding, and the Buddhadharma's own mistaken calculation, however boundless their faults may be, are all broken through by means of the Four Noble Truths, and there is not one that fails to transform the ordinary into the sage. If even the Buddha's own first teaching of the Āgamas' Four Noble Truths already carries this much power, how much more, then, the Mahāyāna's own three kinds of Four Noble Truths — what could they possibly fail to break through? Once the view of neither existence nor nonexistence is broken through, every affliction whatsoever is likewise severed and destroyed, and correct wisdom arises. This is entering emptiness from the provisional, seeing the ultimate truth directly. Should one not yet enter through this, one should apply calming-and-contemplation, the practitioner of faith and the practitioner of dharma each turning about accordingly, skillfully following the four accommodations, cultivating through expedient means, subduing the affliction of every view — one's grasping mind grows correspondingly thin, abiding in the expedient path, forming a wholesome, contaminated dharma. This view no longer arising, one passes over into the wordless view, to be refuted below. The reason every view's own faults are explained, section by section, in such careful detail, is to instruct the practitioner earnestly, so that in contemplating the mind, one may clearly recognize the poisonous weed and clearly understand the healing herb alike. Grasping this sense, one will never, in the end, calculate mistakenly. Though these sections may seem tedious, each in turn remains distinct and unconfused with the rest. One who is able to fully grasp this may be spoken with about the path; but one who remains obstinately blind — how could such a person ever come to recognize milk at all?

次、破無言說見假者：若能如上破者，或進發定慧，豁然明靜。復起異解，謂：「適有此有，即有生死；四句皆假，虛妄不實；理在言外，絕於四句乃是無生。」謂出四句，實不出也。略有三種四句外：一、單，二、複，三、具足。若謂理在言外者，乃是出單四句外，不出複見第二句，亦不出具足見初句。故知見網蒙密，難可得出。《法華》云：「魑魅魍魎處處皆有。」複、具諸見，一一皆有三假、苦集；破假之觀皆如上說。若人能於諸見修習道品，皆應節節得悟，從假入空見第一義。若未得入者，單、複、具足一切諸見悉皆被伏，成善有漏五陰，見不得起，或進發禪解。又復言出單、複、具足四句之外，言語道斷，心行處滅，泯然清淨，即是無生絕言之道。如此計者，還是不可說絕言之見，何關正道？徒謂絕言，言終不絕，何以故？待不絕而論絕，絕還是待；待對得起，不應言絕。如避虛空，豈有免理？又竪破不絕者：心不絕故，無言見具起一切生死因果，云何稱絕？

Next, as for breaking through the threefold provisional belonging to the wordless view: should one have already broken through as described above, one may advance further, giving rise to concentration and wisdom, suddenly bright and still. And yet a further, different understanding arises: "The instant 'this' exists, birth-and-death exists; all four propositions are themselves provisional, false and unreal — the true principle lies beyond words altogether, and only what cuts off all four propositions is truly the unborn." Yet in claiming to have emerged beyond the four propositions, one has not, in reality, emerged from them at all. There are, in brief, three kinds of "beyond the four propositions": beyond the single fourfold, beyond the compound fourfold, and beyond the complete fourfold. Should one claim that principle lies beyond words in this way, this is merely emerging beyond the single fourfold proposition — it has not yet emerged beyond even the second proposition of the compound view, nor even the first proposition of the complete view. Hence it is known that the net of views is dense and thick, difficult indeed to escape from. The Lotus Sūtra says: "Goblins and spirits are present everywhere." The compound and complete views, each and every one, likewise carry their own threefold provisional and their own suffering and origin; the contemplation that breaks through this provisional is, in every case, exactly as explained above. Should a person be able to cultivate the branches of the path with regard to every view, they ought to attain awakening section by section, entering emptiness from the provisional and seeing the ultimate truth directly. Should one not yet enter through this, the single, compound, and complete views, every one, are all subdued, forming wholesome, contaminated aggregates, this view no longer able to arise — and one may advance further still, giving rise to some further meditative understanding. And, further, claiming to have emerged beyond the single, compound, and complete fourfold propositions altogether, the path of words cut off, the sphere of the mind's own activity extinguished, dissolved into utter purity — this, one supposes, is the path of the unborn, beyond all words. Yet a calculation of this kind is, itself, still nothing more than the very view of "the unspeakable, beyond words" — what does this have to do with the true path at all? To claim, in vain, to be beyond words, while words are, in the end, never actually cut off at all — why is this? Because depending on "not cut off" in order to discuss "cut off" means that "cut off" is, itself, still a form of dependence; and where dependence and its counterpart both still arise, one ought not to speak of anything truly "cut off" at all. It is like trying to flee empty space itself — how could there possibly be any escaping it? And, examined vertically, this "cutting off" proves not truly cut off either: since the mind itself is not truly cut off, this wordless view still gives rise to every causation of birth-and-death in full — how, then, could it be called truly "cut off" at all?

Reading note: "Goblins and spirits are present everywhere" is a striking borrowing for a purely logical point: the wordless view feels like an escape from the net of views precisely because it has only cleared the single fourfold proposition, and mistakes that partial clearing for total freedom — "it has not yet emerged beyond even the second proposition of the compound view." The refutation of "beyond words" is the treatise's own tightest piece of reasoning in the whole section: claiming words are cut off is itself still a claim made in words, dependent on the very "not cut off" it claims to transcend — "it is like trying to flee empty space itself."

The Vertical Refutation: Testing "Birth" and "Non-Birth" Against Every Stage 絕言見 · 竪破 · 十種不生

Chinese Source

上來節節皆有橫竪兩破。於一有見，是橫破；重累四見，是竪破。因戒假是橫破；相續假是竪破；相待假是亦橫亦竪破；總破是非橫非竪破。大途秖是橫破，今當竪破。汝執心是有，有即是生，汝是何等生？為是五停、總別念處、煗頂忍世第一生？為是苦忍真明生？為是重慮思惟生？為是乾慧似道生？為是八人見諦生？為是神通遊戲誓扶習氣生？為是三賢伏道似解生？為是十聖真解生？為是鐵輪似道生？為是銅輪真道生？為是遍法界自在生？用此諸生，勘汝執心，全無氣分；而言非見，孰是見乎？若計心是無生，無即不生，汝是何等不生？為是見不生？為是思不生？為習氣不生？為塵沙不生？為無明不生？為業不生？為報不生？為行不生？為理不生？世人云不生，不生即是佛；秖道是法佛。今釋此語即是三佛：理不生，即法佛；無明不生，即報佛；塵沙、見、思不生，即應佛。又無明不生，即法佛；見思不生，即報佛；塵沙不生，即應佛。又業行位不生，即應佛；智業不生，即報佛；理不生，即法佛。又應佛從緣因生；報佛從了因生；法佛從正因生。三佛生即無生；無生即三佛生。「若聞阿字門，即解一切義。」云何秖作一解耶？利钁斵地，徹至金剛；聞一不生，遍解法界不生。將諸不生，勘汝執心，了無一分，非見是何？

Ru-Yi Translation

Every section given above carries both a horizontal and a vertical refutation. Within a single view of existence, this is the horizontal refutation; layering the four views one upon another, this is the vertical refutation. The provisional of composite arising is the horizontal refutation; the provisional of continuity is the vertical refutation; the provisional of mutual relativity is both horizontal and vertical at once; and the general refutation is neither horizontal nor vertical. What has come so far has, for the most part, been simply the horizontal refutation — here, the vertical refutation shall now be given. You cling to the mind as existent; existence is itself already birth — what kind of birth, exactly, is yours? Is it the birth of the five methods for stilling the mind, of the general and specific foundations of mindfulness, of warmth, summit, forbearance, and the supreme worldly dharma? Is it the birth of the forbearance of suffering, true clarity? Is it the birth of repeated reflection and consideration? Is it the birth of dry wisdom, resembling the path? Is it the birth of the eight-fold seeing of the truth? Is it the birth of one who has vowed to sustain residual habituation while sporting in spiritual powers? Is it the birth of the three grades of worthy, subduing the path, resembling understanding? Is it the birth of the ten grades of sage, true understanding? Is it the birth of the iron wheel, resembling the path? Is it the birth of the bronze wheel, the true path? Or is it the birth of self-mastery pervading the entire dharma-realm? Examining your own clinging mind against every one of these kinds of birth, not the slightest trace of any of them is to be found — and yet you claim this is not a view? What, then, could possibly be a view, if not this? Should one instead calculate the mind as unborn, "unborn" is itself already a form of non-birth — what kind of non-birth, exactly, is yours? Is it the non-birth of views? Of habituated thought? Of habitual tendencies? Of dust-and-sand afflictions? Of ignorance? Of karma? Of retribution? Of formations? Of principle itself? People of the world say "unborn is itself the Buddha" — but this speaks only of the Dharma-body buddha alone. Here, this saying shall be explained as referring to all three buddha-bodies at once: principle's own non-birth is the Dharma-body buddha; ignorance's own non-birth is the reward-body buddha; and the non-birth of dust-and-sand afflictions, views, and habituated thought is the response-body buddha. Or, alternatively: ignorance's own non-birth is the Dharma-body buddha; the non-birth of views and habituated thought is the reward-body buddha; and the non-birth of dust-and-sand afflictions is the response-body buddha. Or, again: the non-birth of karmic practice and stage is the response-body buddha; the non-birth of the karma of wisdom is the reward-body buddha; and the non-birth of principle itself is the Dharma-body buddha. Further, the response-body buddha arises from the conditioning cause; the reward-body buddha arises from the illuminating cause; the Dharma-body buddha arises from the correct cause. The birth of these three buddha-bodies is itself already the unborn; and the unborn is itself already the birth of these three buddha-bodies. "Should one hear the gate of the letter a, one thereby understands every sense whatsoever" — how, then, could one form only a single, narrow understanding of it? A sharp hoe, breaking the ground, reaches straight down to the vajra layer beneath; hearing of a single non-birth, one understands the non-birth of the entire dharma-realm throughout. Weighing every kind of non-birth against your own clinging mind, not the slightest trace of any of them is truly present — and if this is not a view, what, then, could possibly be a view?

有人難《中論》云「不生不滅」未會深理。何者？煩惱是生法，三相遷謝是滅法；秖不此生滅，故言不生不滅。但是入空，不見中意。《中論》師解云：「不生不滅者，不不生、不不滅，以顯中道。」此解扶中，而傷文失義。何者？龍樹之意，兼通含別，故言「不生不滅」。不生者，不二十五有之生，不三相遷滅之滅，能破二十種身見，成須陀洹乃至無學。豈非兼申通意，亦兼三藏意？若生、若滅皆屬於生；涅槃但空唯屬寂滅。不此之生，不此之滅，雙遮二邊，豈非含別之意？若生滅是因緣所生法，即空即假即中。即空故不生，即假故不滅，不生不滅即是中道。按文解釋，兼二，含別顯中，四義宛然。龍樹之巧，以「不生不滅」一句，廣攝諸法，乃會摩訶衍耳。若開脣動舌，重吃鳳兮之聲；抽筆染毫，加於點淰之字；秖得一意，全失三門。懸疣附贅，雖欲補助，還成漏失。今解不生一句，何啻含於四義？且略出十不生、不不生意也。一者、一切法可破可壞，一切語可轉；非有非無，絕言離句。無一法入心，是一不生；不生亦不生，故名不不生。雖情謂不生，而實是生。如非想謂言無想，而成就細想。此乃邪見外道之不不生也。二者、犢子道人，計我在第五不可說藏中，此是一不生；不生亦不生，故名不不生。若三藏二乘，斷三界見思，一不不見，一不不思，故名不不生，而習氣猶生。若三藏佛，正、習俱盡，名不不生。一不不正，一不不習，故言「不不生」。此析法不不生。若通教，體見本不生，體思本不生，故言「不不生」。《思益》云：「我於無生無作，而得作證。」二乘雖體不見思，而習氣猶生。通教佛坐道場，正習俱盡，亦是不不生。此乃分段不不生耳。若別教人，斷通別惑，一不不通，一不不別，名不不生。此一品一分、二品二分不不生耳；上分猶生。若別教佛，上分盡，名不不生。此猶是方便權說不不生。若圓人，一不不通，一不不別，名不不生。猶居因地，猶有上地行、智、報等生在。若妙覺智滿，其智更不生；無明究竟盡，惑更不生；行、智、報等，畢竟不不生。又真理極故，一不不生；圓理極故，一不不生。又理本本不生，今亦不不生。若作單不生語，攝法亦盡，如前說。若作不不生語，攝法亦盡。汝作不生，齊何處不生？汝作不不生，復齊何處不不生？他尚不識外道不不生，況識最後不不生？那得不愜是見？當苦破之。

There was a person who challenged the Middle Treatise's own phrase "neither born nor ceasing" as failing to grasp the profound principle at all. Why so? Affliction is itself the dharma of birth; the three marks passing away is itself the dharma of cessation — simply saying "not this birth, not this cessation" is named "neither born nor ceasing." But this is merely entering emptiness, without seeing the sense of the middle at all. The Middle Treatise's own commentators explain: "'Neither born nor ceasing' means not-not-born, not-not-ceasing, thereby revealing the middle way." This explanation supports the middle, and yet damages the text and loses its true sense. Why is this? Nāgārjuna's own intent embraces both the shared and the distinct teachings at once, and this is exactly why he says "neither born nor ceasing." "Not born" means not the birth of the twenty-five states of existence; "not ceasing" means not the cessation of the three marks passing away — able thereby to break through the twenty kinds of the view of a substantial self, accomplishing stream-entry all the way through to the state beyond further training. Is this not, then, extending both the shared teaching's own sense, and also the Tripiṭaka teaching's own sense at once? Both birth and cessation belong properly to birth alone; nirvāṇa, mere emptiness, belongs properly to quiescent extinction alone. "Not this birth, not this cessation" obstructs both extremes at once — is this not, then, embracing the distinct teaching's own sense as well? If birth and cessation are dharmas arisen through causes and conditions, they are thereby empty, provisional, and the middle, all at once: being empty, hence unborn; being provisional, hence unceasing; and being neither born nor ceasing is itself already the middle way. Examined against the text, this explanation embraces two teachings, includes a third, and reveals the middle — all four senses vividly present at once. Nāgārjuna's own skill, using this single phrase "neither born nor ceasing," gathers in every dharma broadly, thereby according with the Mahāyāna itself. Should one merely part the lips and move the tongue, repeating this same phoenix-like sound; or take up the brush and dip it in ink, adding one further dotted character — grasping only a single sense while losing all three gates entirely. Hanging on a wart, attaching an excrescence — though one wishes thereby to supplement and assist, this only results, instead, in further loss. Here, explaining this single phrase "unborn," how could it possibly be confined to only four senses? Let ten senses of "non-birth" and "non-non-birth" be briefly set out. First: every dharma can be broken, can be destroyed; every word of it can be turned about; neither existent nor nonexistent, cut off from words, apart from every phrase. Not a single dharma entering the mind at all is "one non-birth"; and this non-birth, too, not itself born, is named "non-non-birth." Though sentiment supposes this to be non-birth, it is, in reality, still birth after all — like the state of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, which claims to be free of perception, and yet still accomplishes a subtle perception all the same. This is the "non-non-birth" of the heterodox path's own deviant view. Second: the followers of Vātsīputrīya calculate that the self resides within a fifth, inexpressible category — this is "one non-birth"; and this non-birth, too, not itself born, is named "non-non-birth." Should the Tripiṭaka teaching's own two lesser vehicles sever the views and habituated thought of the three realms, one non-not-seeing and one non-not-thinking, this is named "non-non-birth" — and yet habitual tendency still remains. Should the Tripiṭaka teaching's own buddha have both correct affliction and habitual tendency alike exhausted, this is named "non-non-birth" — one non-not-correct, one non-not-habituated, hence it is said, "non-non-birth." This is the "non-non-birth" of the method of analysis. As for the shared teaching: embodying that views are, at root, unborn, and embodying that habituated thought is, at root, unborn, this too is said to be "non-non-birth." The Sūtra of Resolving Doubts says, "I myself have, within the unborn and the unconditioned, still attained a realization thereof." The two lesser vehicles, though embodying views and habituated thought as unborn, still retain habitual tendency; the shared teaching's own buddha, sitting at the very seat of enlightenment, has both correct affliction and habitual tendency exhausted alike, and this, too, is "non-non-birth" — though this is merely the "non-non-birth" of segmented birth-and-death alone. As for the distinct-teaching practitioner: severing the affliction shared with the shared teaching and the affliction distinct to this teaching alone, one non-not-shared and one non-not-distinct, this is named "non-non-birth" — this is the "non-non-birth" of a single portion, a single grade, or two grades, with the portion above still unborn in the fuller sense. Should the distinct teaching's own buddha have this portion above exhausted as well, this is named "non-non-birth" — though this, too, remains merely a provisional, expedient account of "non-non-birth." As for the perfect-teaching practitioner: one non-not-shared, one non-not-distinct, this too is named "non-non-birth" — and yet this still abides at the causal stage, with the practice, wisdom, and reward of the higher stages still yet to be born. Only once the sublime awakening's own wisdom is fully complete does that wisdom no longer arise at all; ignorance ultimately exhausted, affliction no longer arises at all; practice, wisdom, reward, and the rest, ultimately, are non-non-birth in the fullest sense. Further, because the real principle itself reaches its very utmost, this is "one non-birth"; and because the perfect principle itself reaches its very utmost, this, too, is "one non-birth." And further, principle, at its very root, is fundamentally unborn — and now, too, is non-non-born. Whether one speaks in terms of the single "unborn," every dharma is thereby gathered in entirely, exactly as explained above; or whether one speaks in terms of "non-non-born," every dharma is likewise gathered in entirely. Where, exactly, does your own "unborn" reach its limit? Where, exactly, does your own "non-non-born" reach its limit? Such a person does not even recognize the heterodox path's own "non-non-birth" — how much less, then, the very furthest and final "non-non-birth" of all? How, then, could this fail to be, itself, still a view? This must be broken through with all diligence.

Reading note: The vertical refutation works by a kind of relentless specificity: rather than arguing in the abstract that "birth" or "non-birth" is empty, Zhiyi forces the claimant to locate their own attainment on an actual ladder of named stages — and finds, inevitably, that it fits nowhere on it. His defense of the Middle Treatise's "neither born nor ceasing" against a rival commentary is itself a small masterclass in the treatise's own reading method: rather than picking one interpretation, he shows the single phrase legitimately supports four at once (Tripiṭaka, shared, distinct, and the middle), and that a commentary supplying only one "loses all three gates entirely" — the same insistence on comprehensiveness that organizes the whole fascicle.

Ten Kinds of the Fourfold Proposition 絕言見 · 竪破 · 十種四句

Chinese Source

竪破亦有亦無見、非有非無見，如上菩提心中，釋名絕待中示其相也。若謂心亦生亦不生者，為是何等亦生亦不生？為是見不生而真生？為是思不生而真生？為是習不生而真生？為是塵沙不生通用生？為是無明不生中道生？為是內業不生外業生？為是內報不生外報生？為是小行不生大行生？為是偏理不生圓理生，而言亦生亦不生？若非如此等亦生亦不生，非見何謂？若言心非生非不生者，為是何等非生非不生？為是析斷常非生非不生？為體斷常非生非不生？為是八地道觀雙流非生非不生？為是初地破生死得涅槃非生非不生？為是十地後果非生非不生？為是初住雙遮二邊非生非不生？為是十行增進中道非生非不生？為是十迴向非生非不生？為是十地非生非不生？為是妙覺極地非生非不生？既非此等非生非不生，非見是何？

Ru-Yi Translation

The vertical refutation of the view of both existence and nonexistence, and of neither existence nor nonexistence, has already had its mark shown above, in the account of "the absolute, beyond all opposition" within the discussion of the bodhi-mind. Should one claim the mind is both born and not born, what kind of "both born and not born" is this, exactly? Is it the non-birth of views alongside the true birth of something else? The non-birth of habituated thought alongside true birth? The non-birth of habitual tendency alongside true birth? The non-birth of dust-and-sand afflictions alongside the birth of shared function? The non-birth of ignorance alongside the birth of the middle way? The non-birth of inward karma alongside the birth of outward karma? The non-birth of inward retribution alongside the birth of outward retribution? The non-birth of the lesser practice alongside the birth of the greater practice? The non-birth of partial principle alongside the birth of perfect principle — is this what is meant by "both born and not born"? If it is nothing of this kind, what, then, could it be, if not a view? Should one say the mind is neither born nor not born, what kind of "neither born nor not born" is this, exactly? Is it the analytical severance of the eternalist and annihilationist views, neither born nor not born? Is it the embodying severance of those same views, neither born nor not born? Is it the eighth ground's own dual flow of calming and contemplation, neither born nor not born? Is it the first ground's own breaking of birth-and-death and attainment of nirvāṇa, neither born nor not born? Is it the tenth ground's own final fruit, neither born nor not born? Is it the first abiding's own obstruction of both extremes at once, neither born nor not born? Is it the ten practices' own advance into the middle way, neither born nor not born? Is it the ten dedications, neither born nor not born? Is it the ten grounds, neither born nor not born? Or is it the very culminating stage of sublime awakening, neither born nor not born? Since it is none of these, what, then, could it be, if not a view?

若絕言者，絕言甚多，是何等絕言？單四句外亦稱絕言；複外、具外亦稱絕言。如婆羅門受啞法者，亦是絕言；又長爪一切法不受，亦是絕言；犢子云：「世諦有我，我在不可說藏中。」不可說亦是絕言。三藏入實證真，亦不可說。故身子云：「吾聞解脫之中，無有言說。」三藏解脫凡有四門入實，即有四種不可說；通教三乘人同以無言說道斷煩惱，亦有四門不可說；別教人觀常住理無言無說，亦有四門不可說；圓教不可宣示，淨名杜口、文殊印之，此亦有四門不可說。不可說眾多，汝所計不可說，為是何等？汝尚不及犢子不可說，何況三藏四不可說？何以故？犢子謂不可說為世諦，不計為涅槃，汝計為實，故知不及犢子。犢子尚是見，汝寧非見？為此見故，廣起煩惱浩然，如前說。更重破絕言者，汝謂絕言在四句外，今明十種四句，汝之絕言在何等四句外？十種者：一往四句、無窮四句、結位四句、襵牒四句、得悟四句、攝屬四句、權實四句、開顯四句、失意四句、得意四句。「一往四句」者：凡聖通途皆論四句，此意可知。「無窮四句」者：四四瀾漫無貲，如四十八番中示其相云云。「結位四句」者：分齊四句，剋定是非，如單、複、具足等，住著不亡，即凡夫四句；若無句義為句義，是聖人四句。「襵牒四句」者：結凡夫四句牒為有句；牒二乘為無句；牒菩薩為亦有亦無句；牒佛為非有非無句。「得悟四句」者：隨句入處，即成悟入之門；四句即成四門。「攝屬四句」者：隨諸句門悟入何法，以法分之，屬諸法門也。「權實四句」者：諸法四句之門，三四為權，一四為實也。「開顯四句」者：開一切四句，皆入一實四句。若入實四句皆不可說也。佛教四句齊此。「失意四句」者：執佛四句而起諍競，過同凡夫也。「得意四句」者：菩薩見失意之過，作小大論，申佛兩四句，破執遣迷，則有得意四句，作論之功息矣。若不愜是絕言見者，前諸四句，汝出何等四句外而謂理在言外耶？前橫破四句，今竪破四句之言外也。

As for "beyond words": there are many things called "beyond words" — what kind of "beyond words" is this, exactly? Beyond the single fourfold proposition is also called "beyond words"; beyond the compound and beyond the complete are likewise called "beyond words." As with the brahmin who takes the vow of muteness — this, too, is "beyond words"; and Dīrghanakha, accepting no dharma whatsoever — this, too, is "beyond words"; the followers of Vātsīputrīya say, "In the conventional truth there is a self, and this self resides within an inexpressible category" — this "inexpressible" is likewise "beyond words." The Tripiṭaka teaching's own entry into the real, realizing the truth, is also said to be inexpressible. Hence Śāriputra says, "I have heard that within liberation itself, there are no words at all." The Tripiṭaka teaching's own liberation has, in general, four gates of entry into the real, and so there are four kinds of "cannot be spoken"; the shared teaching's three vehicles, alike, use the wordless path to sever affliction, and so likewise have four gates of "cannot be spoken"; the distinct-teaching practitioner, contemplating the principle of eternal abiding, wordless and unspoken, likewise has four gates of "cannot be spoken"; and the perfect teaching, beyond all proclamation, as when Vimalakīrti sealed his own mouth shut and Mañjuśrī affirmed it, likewise has four gates of "cannot be spoken." "Cannot be spoken" is thus manifold indeed — what you yourself calculate as "cannot be spoken," which of these, exactly, is it? You do not even reach the followers of Vātsīputrīya's own "cannot be spoken" — how much less, then, the Tripiṭaka teaching's own fourfold "cannot be spoken"? Why is this? The followers of Vātsīputrīya hold "cannot be spoken" to be the conventional truth, not calculating it to be nirvāṇa itself; you, however, calculate it to be real — hence it is known you do not even reach their own standard. Even the followers of Vātsīputrīya are still caught in a view — how, then, could you possibly be free of one? On account of this very view, boundless affliction arises vast and wide, exactly as explained before. To refute "beyond words" once more still further: you suppose "beyond words" lies outside the four propositions — here, ten kinds of the four propositions shall be clarified; your own "beyond words," beyond which of these ten kinds of four propositions does it actually lie? The ten are: the general fourfold proposition; the boundless fourfold proposition; the fourfold proposition fixed by stage; the fourfold proposition nested by category; the fourfold proposition of attained awakening; the fourfold proposition of assigned belonging; the fourfold proposition of the provisional and the real; the fourfold proposition of opening and revealing; the fourfold proposition of missing the sense; and the fourfold proposition of grasping the sense. As for "the general fourfold proposition": both ordinary beings and sages alike, in general, discuss the four propositions — this sense may be understood easily. As for "the boundless fourfold proposition": four times four spreads out without limit or measure, as shown in its own mark among the forty-eight variants. As for "the fourfold proposition fixed by stage": a fourfold proposition with a fixed boundary, determining right from wrong — as with the single, compound, and complete views, dwelling in attachment without release, this is the ordinary being's own fourfold proposition; while taking the very absence of any fixed proposition as the proposition itself is the sage's own fourfold proposition. As for "the fourfold proposition nested by category": nesting the ordinary being's own fourfold proposition as the proposition of "existence"; nesting the two lesser vehicles as the proposition of "nonexistence"; nesting the bodhisattva as the proposition of "both existence and nonexistence"; and nesting the buddha as the proposition of "neither existence nor nonexistence." As for "the fourfold proposition of attained awakening": according with whichever proposition one enters through, this becomes the very gate of awakened entry — the four propositions thereby become four gates. As for "the fourfold proposition of assigned belonging": according with whichever gate one has awakened into, and whichever dharma one has entered, this is distinguished by dharma, belonging to the various gates of the Dharma accordingly. As for "the fourfold proposition of the provisional and the real": among the gates of the four propositions across every dharma, three of the four are provisional, and one of the four is real. As for "the fourfold proposition of opening and revealing": opening every one of the four propositions, all of them enter into the single, real fourfold proposition; and once one has entered this real fourfold proposition, none of it, in the end, can be spoken at all. The Buddha's own teaching of the four propositions reaches exactly this far. As for "the fourfold proposition of missing the sense": clinging to the Buddha's own four propositions and thereby giving rise to contention — a fault exactly the same as the ordinary being's own. As for "the fourfold proposition of grasping the sense": the bodhisattva, seeing the fault of missing the sense, composes treatises great and small, expounding the Buddha's own twin sets of four propositions, breaking through attachment and dispelling delusion — this yields the fourfold proposition of grasping the sense, and, once grasped, the very work of composing further treatises comes to rest. Should you still not be satisfied that this is, itself, a view of "beyond words," then, among all these various fourfold propositions given above, beyond which of them, exactly, do you claim principle to lie beyond words at all? What came before was the horizontal refutation of the four propositions; this, now, is the vertical refutation of "beyond words" itself, among the four propositions.

Reading note: The claim that "beyond words" is itself just one more view, and a weaker one than the followers of Vātsīputrīya's own inexpressible-self doctrine at that, is the sharpest edge of the whole extended refutation: even a genuinely humble-sounding retreat into silence can still smuggle in a fixed position, and Zhiyi's own test is whether the claimant treats that silence as merely provisional (the Vātsīputrīyas' own more modest stance) or as final reality (the error he is diagnosing). The ten kinds of fourfold proposition taxonomy that follows is less a display of erudition than a closing of the last possible refuge: no matter which of the ten senses of "the four propositions" a person means by "beyond them," the treatise has already anticipated and named it.

Against Conflating "Beyond Words" with Zhuangzi and Laozi 絕言見 · 破莊老渾同

Chinese Source

今世多有惡魔比丘，退戒還家，懼畏驅策，更越濟道士。復邀名利，誇談莊老，以佛法義偷安邪典，押高就下，推尊入卑，概令平等。以「道可道，非常道；名可名，非常名。」均齊佛法不可說示；如蟲食木，偶得成字。檢校道理，邪正懸絕；愚者所信，智者所蚩。何者？如前所說，諸生、諸不生、諸四句、諸不可說，汝尚非單四句外不可說，何況複外？何況具足外？何況犢子耶？尚非犢子，何況三藏通別圓耶？諸法理本往望「常名」、「常道」，云何得齊？教相往望，已不得齊，況以苦集往檢，過患彰露，云何得齊？況將道品往望，云何得齊正法之要？本既不齊，迹亦不齊。佛迹世世是正天竺金輪剎利；莊、老是真丹邊地小國，柱下書史、宋國漆園吏，此云何齊？佛以三十二相、八十種好纏絡其身；莊老身如凡流，凡流之形痤小醜篾。經云：「閻浮提人，形狀如鬼。」云何齊佛？佛說法時放光動地，天人畢會叉手聽法；適機而說，梵響如流，辯不可盡。當於語下言不虛發，聞皆得道。老在周朝，主上不知，群下不識，不敢出一言諫諍，不能化得一人；乘壞板車出關西，竊說尹喜，有何公灼？又漆園染毫題簡，句治改足，軋軋若抽；造《內、外篇》以規顯達，誰共同聞？復誰得道？云何得齊？如是不齊，其義無量，倦不能說。云何以邪而干於正？

Ru-Yi Translation

In the present age, there are many evil-demon monks who retreat from their precepts and return to lay life, fearing hardship and its urging, going even further than the Daoist priests they once surpassed. Seeking fame and gain besides, they boast of Zhuangzi and Laozi, stealing the sense of the Buddhadharma to give false comfort through heterodox texts — pressing down what is high and raising up what is low, demoting the honored and promoting the base, forcing them all into a false equality.
