Taking "the way that can be spoken of is not the constant way; the name that can be named is not the constant name," they equate this with the Buddhadharma's own inexpressibility — like a worm gnawing through wood that happens, by chance, to form the shape of a written character. Examined closely, the difference between the deviant and the correct is vast indeed — what fools believe, the wise mock and disdain. Why is this? As already explained above, of every kind of birth, every kind of non-birth, every one of the four propositions, and every "cannot be spoken" — you yourself have not even gone beyond the single fourfold proposition's own "cannot be spoken," how much less beyond the compound? How much less beyond the complete? How much less, even, the followers of Vātsīputrīya's own standard? Not even reaching the followers of Vātsīputrīya, how much less, then, the Tripiṭaka, shared, distinct, and perfect teachings themselves? The very root of every dharma's own principle, set against "the constant name" and "the constant way" — how could these possibly be made equal at all? Even compared merely at the level of doctrinal teaching, they already cannot be made equal — how much less, then, when examined against suffering and its origin, where every fault stands plainly exposed? How, then, could they possibly be made equal? And how much less still, when measured against the branches of the path — how could they possibly be made equal to the very essence of the true Dharma? Since their very roots are not equal, their traces, too, are not equal. The Buddha's own traces, life after life, belong properly to the true kṣatriya caste of India, the wheel-turning golden lineage; Zhuangzi and Laozi belong to Cīnasthāna, a small, peripheral kingdom, one a mere archivist beneath a pillar, the other a minor official at a lacquer garden in the state of Song — how could these possibly be made equal? The Buddha's own body is adorned with the thirty-two major marks and the eighty minor excellences; Zhuangzi's and Laozi's own bodies were like any ordinary person's, and an ordinary person's own form is puny, unsightly, and mean. A sūtra says: "The people of Jambudvīpa have a form and appearance like that of a hungry ghost" — how, then, could this be made equal to the Buddha? When the Buddha taught the Dharma, he radiated light and shook the very earth, gods and humans alike gathering together, folding their hands to listen to the teaching; speaking exactly to each capacity, his brahma-voice flowing like a stream, his eloquence inexhaustible. At the very moment his words fell, not a single one was spoken in vain, and every listener attained the path upon hearing it. Laozi, in the Zhou dynasty, was neither known by his own ruler above nor recognized by the officials below, and dared not utter a single word of remonstrance; he was unable to transform even a single person. Riding out on a battered wooden cart through the pass to the west, he secretly spoke his teaching to Yin Xi alone — what public glory was there in this at all? And the lacquer-garden official, dipping his brush to write on bamboo strips, editing phrase by phrase, correcting line by line, laboring as though hauling a heavy load — composing his Inner and Outer Chapters merely to admonish the eminent of his own day, who else heard it together with him? And who else, besides, ever attained the path through it? How, then, could this possibly be made equal to the Buddha's own teaching? Points of this kind, where the two cannot be made equal, are without limit — I am weary, and cannot enumerate them all. How, then, could the deviant possibly be allowed to intrude upon the correct in this way?

復次，如來行時，帝釋在右，梵王在左，金剛前導，四部後從，飛空而行。老自御薄板青牛車，向關西作田；莊為他所使，看守漆樹。如此舉動，復云何齊？如來定為轉輪聖帝，四海顒顒；待神寶至，忽此榮位，出家得佛。老仕關東悋小吏之職，墾農關西，惜數畝之田；公私怱遽，不能棄此，云何言齊？盲人無眼信汝所說，有智慧者愍而怪之。是故當知，汝不可說是絕言之見，三假具足，苦集成就，生死宛然，抱炬自燒，甚可傷痛！若破此見，如前所說云云。

Furthermore, when the Tathāgata walked, Śakra stood at his right, Brahmā at his left, Vajrapāṇi leading the way before him, the fourfold assembly following behind — flying through the very sky as he walked. Laozi himself drove a thin wooden cart drawn by a black ox, heading west through the pass to till the fields; Zhuangzi was employed by another, watching over a lacquer plantation. Conduct of this kind — how, then, could it possibly be made equal to the Buddha's own? The Tathāgata was, by his very destiny, to become a wheel-turning sage-emperor, the four seas gazing up to him in reverence — yet, awaiting only the arrival of the divine treasures of that office, he cast aside this glorious rank all at once, left home, and attained buddhahood instead. Laozi served as a minor official in the eastern reaches of the Zhou, reluctant to give up even so small a post; he farmed the land to the west of the pass, cherishing his own few acres of field — busy with public and private affairs alike, unable to cast any of it aside. How, then, could this possibly be called equal to the Buddha? Only a blind person, without eyes of their own, could believe what you say; one possessed of wisdom would pity you, and find it strange indeed. Hence one should know: this "cannot be spoken" you claim as the wordless view is, itself, complete with the threefold provisional, suffering and its origin fully accomplished within it, birth-and-death vividly present — embracing a torch and thereby burning oneself, a matter deeply to be lamented! Should this view be broken through, it is exactly as already explained above.

Reading note: This polemic belongs to its own sixth-century moment — the Sui dynasty's real, sometimes state-sponsored rivalry between Buddhist and Daoist establishments — and its argument is worth separating from its rhetoric: Zhiyi's actual objection is that quoting the Daodejing's "the way that can be spoken of is not the constant way" as though it settled the same question as the treatise's own painstaking ten-fold taxonomy of "cannot be spoken" collapses a category distinction his students had, by this point, spent an enormous amount of effort building — the "worm gnawing through wood that happens, by chance, to form the shape of a written character" is his image for a resemblance mistaken for an equivalence. The biographical comparisons that follow are argued in the historiographical terms available to a sixth-century Buddhist monk, not this site's own view of either figure; they are translated here as part of the source text, not endorsed by the translation.

Closing the Refutation of Views, and Weighing Gain and Loss 見假總結 · 二、明得失

Chinese Source

復次，外人或時用「道可道非常道」為絕言，破《中論》「不生不滅」，云是第四句，絕言出過四句。一往聞語，謂言出過，理則不然。言「不生」者，見心不生，既不生，即不滅，故言「不生不滅」。絕言見心，生一切愛、見、疑、慢，云何以生滅破他不生不滅？愚癡戲論，不應如此。又問：起不生不滅見，此復云何？答：應有六句：絕言破不生不滅、不生不滅破絕言、絕言修不生不滅、不生不滅修絕言、絕言即不生不滅、不生不滅即絕言云云。一切凡夫未階聖道，介爾起計，悉皆是見。以有見故，三假苦集煩惱隨從；魚王、貝母眾使具足；結業蕪蔓，生死浩然。一人經歷尚無邊畔，何況多人？當知見惑大可怖畏，勤用止觀而摧伏之。若起單見，用止觀四句逐體破之。若避單入複，避複入具，避具入絕言，無趣薳起，止觀逐之，無遠不屆，常寂常照，治之不休。如金剛刀，所擬皆斷，取悟為期。能如是觀，雖不發真，諸見被伏，成方便五陰。若得入空，眾見消盡。故初果所破，如竭四十里水，功夫甚大。恐聞者生疑，略斷三結，餘殘不盡，如一渧水。思雖未盡，見已無餘。從多為言，亦得明破法遍也。問：從假入空破無量見，下二觀復何所破？答：入空之觀破見及思，束而言之，秖是破有；次觀所破，秖是破無；中觀所破，雙非二邊，正顯中道。故《釋論》云：「有無二見滅無餘，稽首佛所尊重法。」故知諸見縱橫尚不為第二觀所破，云何謬謂為真法耶？問：束生死為有，束二乘為無。有見縱橫無量，無亦應然？答：凡夫妄計，觸處生著，是故「有」多。二乘已斷見、思，無復橫計，唯證於空；大乘破之，名為「空見」耳。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, a heterodox opponent may at times take "the way that can be spoken of is not the constant way" as an instance of "beyond words," using it to refute the Middle Treatise's own "neither born nor ceasing," claiming it to be a fourth proposition, "beyond words," surpassing all four propositions. On first hearing this claim, one might suppose it truly surpasses them — but the principle is not actually so. What is meant by "not born" is that the view-mind is not born; and, being not born, it is thus not ceasing either — hence it is said, "neither born nor ceasing." The view-mind of "beyond words" gives rise to every craving, view, doubt, and pride — how, then, could birth and cessation possibly be used to refute another's own genuine "neither born nor ceasing"? This is foolish idle discourse, and ought not to be indulged. A further question: what, then, of giving rise to the view "neither born nor ceasing" itself? Answer: there ought to be six propositions concerning this: "beyond words" refuting "neither born nor ceasing"; "neither born nor ceasing" refuting "beyond words"; "beyond words" cultivating "neither born nor ceasing"; "neither born nor ceasing" cultivating "beyond words"; "beyond words" as identical with "neither born nor ceasing"; and "neither born nor ceasing" as identical with "beyond words." Every ordinary being, not yet having ascended the sagely path, gives rise to calculation in the very slightest instant, and this is, in every case, already a view. Because of this view, the threefold provisional, suffering and its origin, and affliction all follow along in train; the fish-king and the cowrie-mother, every proclivity, are all complete within it; karmic bondage spreads like an overgrown thicket, and birth-and-death stretches out vast and wide. A single person's own journey through this is already without limit or boundary — how much more, then, that of many persons together? One should know that the affliction of views is profoundly to be feared, and diligently apply calming-and-contemplation to subdue and overcome it. Should a single view arise, one uses the four propositions of calming-and-contemplation to pursue and break through its very substance. Should one flee the single view only to enter the compound, flee the compound only to enter the complete, flee the complete only to enter the wordless view — with nowhere at all for it to arise unpursued, calming-and-contemplation follows it relentlessly, reaching it no matter how far it flees, ever quiescent, ever illuminating, treating it without rest. Like a vajra blade, whatever it is aimed at is severed at once — taking awakening itself as the very goal. Able to contemplate in this way, though the real may not yet be brought forth, every view is subdued, forming the expedient aggregates. Once one is able to enter emptiness, every view is dissolved entirely. Hence what is broken through at the first fruit is like exhausting a river forty leagues long — the labor involved is truly immense. Fearing the listener might grow doubtful at this: in brief, the three fetters are severed, though the remainder is not yet exhausted, like but a single drop of that water remaining. Though habituated thought is not yet exhausted, view itself is already left with nothing remaining. Speaking in terms of the greater portion accomplished, this, too, may be said to clarify comprehensively breaking through every dharma. Question: entering emptiness from the provisional breaks through boundless views — what, then, do the remaining two contemplations break through? Answer: the contemplation of entering emptiness breaks through both views and habituated thought, and, gathered together, this is simply breaking through existence; the following contemplation's own object of breaking-through is simply breaking through nonexistence; and the contemplation of the middle's own object of breaking-through is negating both extremes at once, directly revealing the middle way. Hence the Treatise says: "The two views of existence and nonexistence are extinguished without remainder — I bow my head before the Buddha's own honored Dharma." Hence it is known that every view, spread both horizontally and vertically, has still not even been broken through by the second contemplation yet — how, then, could it possibly be mistaken for the true Dharma itself? Question: birth-and-death is gathered together as "existence," and the two lesser vehicles are gathered together as "nonexistence." The view of existence, spread horizontally and vertically, is boundless — should not the view of nonexistence be equally so? Answer: the ordinary being's own false calculation clings at every point of contact, and this is exactly why "existence" is so manifold. The two lesser vehicles, having already severed views and habituated thought, no longer calculate wildly in this way, and simply realize emptiness alone — the Mahāyāna, in refuting this, names it simply "the view of emptiness."

二、料簡得失者。問：如此止觀，隨逐諸見，有何得失？答：當四句料簡：一、故惑不除，新惑又生；二、故惑除，新惑又生；三、故惑不除，新惑不生；四、故惑除，新惑不生。一、譬如服藥，故病不差，藥更成病；二、所治病差，而藥作病；三、病雖不差，藥不成妨；四、故病既差，藥亦隨歇。前二種是外道得失相；後二種是佛弟子得失相。所以者何？本用止觀治生死惑，而貪欲之心都不休息，因此止觀更發諸見，破因、破果無所不為，是則「故惑不除，而新惑更起」也。二、修止觀時貪求衣食諸鈍煩惱息而不起；忍耐寒苦、刀割香塗，不生憎愛；財物得失，其心平等；而執見之心甚可怖畏。如渴馬護水，搪揬破壞，撥無因果。是則「故惑去，而新惑生」。此兩屬外道，愛處生愛，瞋處生瞋。若學止觀墮如此者，同彼外道也。三、佛弟子修此止觀為方便道，深識見愛無明因緣，介爾心起即知三假，止觀隨逐破性破相。雖復貪瞋尚在，而見著已虛；六十二等被伏不起。是名「故惑不除，而新惑不生」。是為方便道中人也。四、若能如此，三假四觀逐念檢責，體達虛妄，性相俱空，豁然發真，即得見理。非唯「故病永除，新病不發」，是為入見諦道成聖人云云。

Second, weighing gain and loss: Question: this calming-and-contemplation, pursuing every view in this way, what gain or loss results from it? Answer: this should be weighed through four propositions: first, the old affliction not removed, and a new affliction arising as well; second, the old affliction removed, yet a new affliction still arising; third, the old affliction not removed, yet no new affliction arising; fourth, the old affliction removed, and no new affliction arising either. First, it is like taking medicine: the old sickness is not cured, and the medicine itself becomes a further sickness. Second, the sickness being treated is cured, yet the medicine itself becomes a sickness. Third, though the sickness is not cured, the medicine at least causes no further harm. Fourth, the old sickness is cured, and the medicine, too, is set aside accordingly. The first two of these are the marks of gain and loss belonging to the heterodox path; the latter two are the marks of gain and loss belonging to the Buddha's own disciples. Why is this? Originally, calming-and-contemplation was applied to treat the affliction of birth-and-death, and yet the mind of covetous desire fails to rest at all — on account of this very calming-and-contemplation, further views instead arise, breaking through cause, breaking through fruit, leaving nothing undone — this, then, is "the old affliction not removed, and a new affliction arising further still." Second, while cultivating calming-and-contemplation, the dull afflictions of craving for clothing and food come to rest and no longer arise; one bears cold and hardship, the cutting of a blade or the anointing of perfume, alike, giving rise to neither hatred nor attachment; gain or loss of wealth and property, the mind remains equally undisturbed by either — and yet the mind clinging to view is profoundly to be feared. Like a thirsty horse guarding its own water, thrashing about and destroying everything, sweeping aside cause and effect entirely. This, then, is "the old affliction gone, and yet a new affliction arising." Both of these belong to the heterodox path — attachment arising where attachment already was, aversion arising where aversion already was. Should one who studies calming-and-contemplation fall into a pattern of this kind, one becomes exactly the same as that heterodox path. Third, the Buddha's own disciple, cultivating this calming-and-contemplation as the expedient path, deeply recognizing the causal conditions of view, craving, and ignorance — the very instant even the slightest thought arises, one knows it at once as the threefold provisional, calming-and-contemplation pursuing it relentlessly, breaking through both its nature and its mark. Though craving and aversion may still remain, the attachment of view is already hollowed out; the sixty-two views and the rest are subdued and no longer arise. This is named "the old affliction not removed, and yet no new affliction arising." This describes the person upon the expedient path. Fourth, should one be able to go further still, the threefold provisional and the four contemplations pursuing and examining every thought, embodying its falseness directly, nature and mark both alike empty, suddenly bringing forth the real, thereby seeing principle directly. Not only is "the old sickness forever removed, and no new sickness arises" — this is entering the path of seeing the truth directly, and becoming a sage.

Reading note: The four-fold gain-and-loss analysis closes the entire treatment of the affliction of views on a note of genuine caution: calming-and-contemplation is not automatically safe just because it is calming-and-contemplation, and the treatise names, by its own account, two ways a sincere practitioner can still go wrong — either stirring up new views through careless practice, or achieving real equanimity toward ordinary desires while a subtler, more dangerous attachment to view itself goes completely unnoticed, "profoundly to be feared" precisely because it hides behind apparent progress. The thirsty horse guarding its own water, thrashing and destroying rather than simply drinking, is a memorable image for exactly this: the very intensity that looks like discipline can be the affliction itself, unrecognized.

The Stages of Breaking Through View, Across the Four Teachings 三、明破見位

Chinese Source

三、明破見位者：若修此方法，明識四諦，巧用觀慧，諸見被伏者，依三藏法是總別念處，正伏四倒；四倒不生，煖即得發，成方便等位；進破諸見，發真成聖，即初果位也。若依通教伏見之位，是乾慧地；若得理水沾心，即成性地；若進破見者，即是八人、見地位也。若依別教伏見者，是鐵輪十信位；破見，是銅輪十住位。若依圓教伏見，是五品弟子位；破見，是六根清淨位。斷伏名同，觀智大異。三藏觀思議真，析法觀智伏斷。通教觀思議真，體法觀智伏斷。別教雖知中道，次第觀智伏斷。圓教即中，一心觀智伏斷。不可聞名仍混其義。問：若伏見假入賢位者，故惑雖未差，新惑不應生；那得修止觀時，有諸見境發？答：此發宿習。宿習之見還是故惑，如人服藥，藥擊宿病；宿病既動，須臾自差，非是藥為新病也。問：何不直明別、圓入空破假位，而明三藏、通教等入空位為？答：上明修發、不修發、十境交互等，欲示行人淺深法故，敘諸位耳。又欲明半、滿之位，令行者識之耳。又半字入空法，悉是別、圓助道方便。又多僕從而侍衛之，即其義也。又豈離方便而別有真實？即此半字而是滿字。故云：「二乘若智、若斷，即是菩薩無生法忍也。」

Ru-Yi Translation

Third, clarifying the stages of breaking through view: should one cultivate this method, clearly recognizing the Four Noble Truths, skillfully applying contemplative wisdom, and every view is thereby subdued — following the Tripiṭaka teaching's own account, this is the general and specific foundations of mindfulness, directly subduing the four inversions; once the four inversions no longer arise, warmth is thereby brought forth, forming the stage of expedient means and the rest; advancing further to break through every view, bringing forth the real and becoming a sage, this is precisely the stage of the first fruit. Following the shared teaching's own account of the stage of subduing view, this is the stage of dry wisdom; once the water of principle has moistened the mind, this becomes the stage of nature; and, advancing further to break through view, this is precisely the stage of the eight kinds of person, the stage of seeing the truth. Following the distinct teaching's own account of subduing view, this is the ten stages of faith, the iron wheel; breaking through view is the ten abidings, the bronze wheel. Following the perfect teaching's own account of subduing view, this is the stage of the five grades of disciple; breaking through view is the stage of the purification of the six faculties. Though the names "severance" and "subduing" are the same across these accounts, the contemplative wisdom involved differs enormously. The Tripiṭaka teaching contemplates the conceivable real truth, its contemplative wisdom subduing and severing through the method of analysis. The shared teaching likewise contemplates the conceivable real truth, its contemplative wisdom subduing and severing through the method of embodying. The distinct teaching, though it knows the middle way, subdues and severs through sequential contemplative wisdom. The perfect teaching, identical with the middle, subdues and severs through the single mind's own contemplative wisdom. One should not hear these shared names and thereby confuse their actual senses. Question: should one subdue the affliction of view and enter the stage of worthiness, though the old affliction is not yet fully cured, no new affliction ought to arise — how, then, could various view-related objects still arise while cultivating calming-and-contemplation? Answer: this is the arising of habituation from a former life. The view belonging to this former habituation is itself still the old affliction — like a person taking medicine, and the medicine striking against some dormant illness from before; once that dormant illness is thereby stirred, it is quickly cured of itself — this is not the medicine creating some new sickness at all. Question: why not directly clarify the distinct and perfect teachings' own stage of entering emptiness and breaking through the provisional, rather than clarifying the Tripiṭaka and shared teachings' own stage of entering emptiness instead? Answer: what was clarified above — cultivated arising, uncultivated arising, the mutual interaction of the ten objects, and the rest — was intended to show the practitioner the shallow and deep aspects of the Dharma, and this is exactly why these various stages were laid out in this order. It was, further, intended to clarify the stages of the half-word and the full-word teachings, so that the practitioner might recognize them clearly. And the half-word teaching's own method of entering emptiness is, in every case, itself already an auxiliary expedient means for the distinct and perfect teachings. It is, further, like having many servants and retainers in attendance and guard — this is exactly that same sense. And how, indeed, could there be some separate, truly real teaching apart from the expedient means themselves? This very half-word is itself already the full word. Hence it is said: "Whatever wisdom, whatever severance the two lesser vehicles attain is itself already the bodhisattva's own forbearance of the unborn dharma."

體假入空結成止觀義者：諸見輪息，一受不退永寂然，名為「止」；達見無性，性空、相空，名為「觀」。見真諦理，名為「不生」。理既不生，理亦不滅是為不生不滅，名「無生忍」。又見惑不生，名「因不生」；不受三惡報生，名「果不生」。因果不生，亦復不滅，不生不滅，名「無生忍」。是為無生門通於止觀，亦是止觀成無生門。從假入空破見惑遍竟。

As for embodying the provisional and entering emptiness, thereby constituting the very sense of calming-and-contemplation itself: every view's own wheel comes to rest, this single attainment never regressing, forever quiescent — this is named "calming"; realizing that view has no fixed nature, empty in nature, empty in mark — this is named "contemplation." Seeing the principle of the real truth is named "unborn." Since principle itself is unborn, principle, too, is unceasing — and this is "neither born nor ceasing," named "the forbearance of the unborn." Further, the affliction of view no longer arising is named "the cause unborn"; no longer receiving the retribution of the three evil destinies is named "the fruit unborn." Cause and fruit both unborn, and likewise unceasing, neither born nor ceasing, is named "the forbearance of the unborn." This is the gate of the unborn penetrating through to calming-and-contemplation, and is likewise calming-and-contemplation accomplishing the gate of the unborn. Entering emptiness from the provisional, comprehensively breaking through the affliction of views, is hereby complete.

Reading note: The closing question here — why walk through the Tripiṭaka and shared teachings' own stages at all, rather than jumping straight to the distinct and perfect teachings' own superior account — receives an answer that reframes everything the fascicle has just spent so long building: the "lesser" teachings are not a detour around the real path but "an auxiliary expedient means" already built into it, "like having many servants and retainers in attendance and guard." The closing line makes the same point fascicle 3 made about the provisional and the real: "this very half-word is itself already the full word" — a claim that what looked, across two entire fascicles, like a hierarchy of increasingly adequate teachings turns out, examined at the very end, to have been a single teaching all along, refracted through different capacities.

Status of this page: Fascicle 5 of the Mohe Zhiguan is now translated in full, spanning both the upper and lower halves of the fascicle (卷第五上 and 卷第五下). This fascicle opens topic seven, the actual practice (正修止觀) — naming the ten objects of contemplation (陰界入, 煩惱, 病患, 業相, 魔事, 禪定, 諸見, 增上慢, 二乘, 菩薩) and explaining how they arise from and contain one another — and then works the first four of the ten modes of contemplation through the first object, the five aggregates, in full: contemplating the inconceivable object (觀不可思議境, including the celebrated "three thousand in a single thought" passage and its extended defense against the Dilun and Shelun schools), giving rise to the true bodhi-mind (發真正菩提心), skillfully settling the mind (善巧安心, with its eight, thirty-two, sixty-four, and finally one hundred and twenty-eight methods), and comprehensively breaking through every dharma (破法遍) — this last worked out only as far as entering emptiness from the provisional by way of the affliction of views (從假入空破見假), including the exhaustive fourfold refutation of composite arising, continuity, and mutual relativity across all four views, the extended digression distinguishing this from Zhuangzi and Laozi, and the closing account of gain, loss, and stage. The remaining six modes of contemplation, the affliction of habituated thought, entering the provisional from emptiness, the contemplation of the middle, and the nine objects that remain after the aggregates — the whole of topic seven's own further unfolding — remain to be translated, beginning with fascicle 6 of the CBETA source.
